So a place on Revelation's list is a pointer and no more. Each of the strongest was read in the seat, and five were then entered in the engine with controls. Besides those five the list returned what the file already has (the mourning tribes; the wedding in Luke's form) and two left as noted and not entered: Rev 4:4, white garments and crowns on the head, with John 19:2, the crown of thorns on his head and the purple garment; Rev 16:3, everything in the sea died, with Matt 8:32, the herd into the sea, and they died.

2P.4. **Five loci, in the engine.** None was in the file. Four are Luke's, the Gospel that 2N.3 found to have no tie at the passion.

| | Revelation | Gospel | kept | best control | verses of the NT that fit |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| a | 20:2–3 ὁ Σατανᾶς, καὶ ἔδησεν αὐτὸν χίλια ἔτη … δεῖ λυθῆναι αὐτόν | **Luke 13:16** ἣν ἔδησεν ὁ Σατανᾶς ἰδοὺ δέκα καὶ ὀκτὼ ἔτη, οὐκ ἔδει λυθῆναι | Satan, bound, years, must be loosed: 4 of 4, in order after one exchange | 0.5 (the strong man bound, Mark 3:27 and parallels; Tobit 8:3) | 1 |
| b | 20:1–3 ἅλυσιν … ἔδησεν … εἰς τὴν ἄβυσσον | **Luke 8:29–31** ἁλύσεσιν … ἐδεσμεύετο … εἰς τὴν ἄβυσσον ἀπελθεῖν | chain, bound, abyss: 3 of 3 | 0.83, Mark's Gerasene, which has the chain and the binding and lacks the abyss | 1 |
| c | 17:12 δέκα βασιλεῖς … βασιλείαν οὔπω ἔλαβον, ἀλλὰ ἐξουσίαν | **Luke 19:12–17** λαβεῖν ἑαυτῷ βασιλείαν … δέκα … ἐξουσίαν ἔχων ἐπάνω δέκα πόλεων | ten, a kingdom received, authority: 3 of 3 | 0.67 (Matthew's talents has none of the three but the dropped are absent) | Luke 19 only; four stretches of 2 Maccabees 10 fit too |
| d | 14:14 ἐπὶ τὴν νεφέλην καθήμενον ὅμοιον υἱὸν ἀνθρώπου | **Mark 14:62** τὸν υἱὸν τοῦ ἀνθρώπου ἐκ δεξιῶν καθήμενον … μετὰ τῶν νεφελῶν (and Matt 26:64) | son of man, seated, cloud: 3 of 3 | 0.71, Mark 13:26, which has no sitting. Dan 7:13 has no sitting; Luke 22:69 has no cloud | 2, Mark's and Matthew's |
| e | 13:10, 17 ἐν μαχαίρῃ … ὧδε … ἀγοράσαι ἢ πωλῆσαι εἰ μὴ ὁ ἔχων | **Luke 22:36–38** ὁ ἔχων … ὁ μὴ ἔχων πωλησάτω … καὶ ἀγορασάτω μάχαιραν … μάχαιραι ὧδε δύο | sword, here, buy, sell, the one having: 5 of 5 | 0.56, the four arrest scenes, which have the sword and nothing else | 1 |

All five pass and no control passes. The bench runs fifty-eight; fifty-seven pass with no control passing; forty-four fit one verse of the New Testament.

Read.

a. *Luke 13:16.* The innocent reading: binding and loosing are the common words for illness and cure and for a beast at its stall, and Luke's own verse before (13:15, λύει τὸν βοῦν) supplies the loosing. That accounts for bound and loosed. It does not account for Satan as the binder with a count of years and δεῖ λυθῆναι, which stand together in these two places only. The strongest of the five, and found by the scan as four links the Septuagint lacks.

b. *Luke 8:31.* ἄβυσσος is in the New Testament at Luke 8:31, Rom 10:7 and seven times in Revelation. Mark's demons ask not to be sent out of the country (5:10). Luke's ask not to be sent into the abyss. So this is a change Luke makes to Mark, in a scene Mark already tells with a chain and a binding, and the word he brings is the place Revelation 20 puts the bound one. Loci a and b draw on the same three verses of Revelation as Matthew's guard (2O.1): **Rev 20:1–3 now stands under three passages in two Gospels**, the guard at the tomb, the bent woman, the Gerasene.

c. *Luke 19.* The weakest. The nobleman who goes to receive a kingdom and gives authority over ten cities has the usual explanation, Archelaus's journey to Rome (from memory; not seated), and that explains the kingdom received. Revelation's ten who have not yet received a kingdom but receive authority is the nearer wording for the ten and the authority. The engine finds stretches of 2 Maccabees that hold the three members as well, so the Septuagint is not excluded.

d. *Mark 14:62.* Daniel 7:13 has one like a son of man coming with the clouds, and Psalm 109:1 has "sit at my right hand"; the usual account is that the saying joins the two. Revelation 14:14 has the one like a son of man *sitting on the cloud*: the sitting and the cloud are already one image there, and no Septuagint verse has a son of man sitting. Mark's sentence keeps the sitting and the cloud apart and gives the sitting its Psalm.

e. *Luke 22:36–38.* The two swords, Luke's alone. Rev 13:10 is the verse Matthew's "all who take the sword will perish by the sword" is commonly set beside (Matt 26:52), so the sword of Rev 13 was already at the arrest. Luke has, from seven verses on in the same chapter, the buying and selling and "the one who has".

2P.5. **As relations; the opposite deed.** `relations/pairs_rel.py`, the rules of 2J with one added. Every form found in the seat.

| | computed |
|---|---|
| Satan is bound → Satan bound her | agent's side reversed; object's party changed: REVERSE-DOER |
| he must be loosed → she had to be loosed | object's party changed, adversary to the faithful |
| cast into the abyss → that he not command them into the abyss | negative set: NEGATE |
| the ten kings receive authority → the good slave has authority over ten | agent's side reversed |
| seated on the cloud → seated at the right hand | object changed |
| that no one be able to buy → let him buy a sword | negative removed: UNDO |
| the angel shuts → the angel rolls away (2O.2) | **OPPOSE** |

The rule owed: OPPOSE, the action replaced by its opposite, from a declared table (shut and open or roll away; bind and loose; kill and raise; come down and go up; hide and reveal; weep and rejoice; give or cast and take). It fires once in everything entered so far: none of the forty pairs of 2J, none of the thirty-seven of 2M, and the angel at the stone. So it is a real operator and a rare one; the undoing in this corpus is nearly always done by the negative (the tomb refused and given, the buying forbidden and commanded) and seldom by an opposite verb.

Luke's four are of the kinds found before: who is bound and who binds are exchanged; what is forbidden is commanded; what is done to the adversary is not done. Luke 10:19 (2J.6 b) was the same: the locusts' authority given to the disciples, the harm negated. On the pairs now entered Luke's use of Revelation is the reversal of the adversary's part, five times in six. That is said of six loci read by one reader and is not a law (2J.5).

2P.6. **The author's statement on stemmatics.** In his words, 15:34: "stammatics itself and text critical reconstructive will probably bear the mark of suppressed revelation". Recorded as his. What can be set beside it from the seats, and what is owed:

1. What the seat has on the transmission. Scrivener's *Plain Introduction* (1894, seated) reports Scholz's lists as running to 469 cursive codices of the Gospels and 89 of the Apocalypse. The other facts usually given are from memory and are not yet read in a seat: that the Apocalypse is the one book of the New Testament with no place in the Byzantine lectionary; that the Peshitta did not have it; that Erasmus printed it in 1516 from a single manuscript, lacking its last verses, which he supplied from the Latin; that its witnesses do not fall into the families used for the rest of the New Testament. Erasmus's 1516 text, his *Annotationes*, Tregelles's *Account of the Printed Text* and Scrivener are all seated; the reading is owed.
2. Why it bears on this file. Every count here is made on one printed text, Nestle 1904. That text is a reconstruction, and its makers held the received order of the books. Where a Gospel manuscript's wording has been brought toward a parallel, the editor's rule is to prefer the reading that differs from the parallel; but the parallels the rule looks to are the other Gospels and the Septuagint. A Gospel reading that agrees with Revelation would not be counted as an assimilation to anything, since nothing was thought to run that way. So the rule's function, whatever any editor meant, is to leave agreements with Revelation unexamined in both directions: neither removed as harmonizing nor noticed as original.
3. The test this allows. At each of the fifty-eight loci, the variants of the Gospel verse: does the tradition move toward Revelation's wording or away from it, and which reading did the editors print? If the printed text sits further from Revelation than the manuscripts do at these loci and not at others, the reconstruction carries the mark the author names. It needs an apparatus in the seat. None is: Tischendorf's eighth edition and Hoskier's collation of the Apocalypse are the two to seat, both public. A first cut needs only a second printed text of another kind beside Nestle's, and Erasmus's is seated already.

## 2Q. The printed text itself: three texts side by side

2Q.0. The author, 15:47: "yes, thats a good test. lets run it". The test of 2P.6. Scripts: `text/texts.py`, `text/whole2.py`.

2Q.1. **A correction first.** 2P.6 said a first cut could use Erasmus's 1516 text, "seated already". It cannot. The seat's text layer has no Greek letter in it: 3.4 million characters and not one in the Greek alphabet. The scan was read as Latin throughout, so the Greek column is noise. The scan itself is the witness and is intact; the text layer is a seat defect, entered at 2B.6. I had not opened the file before saying it would serve.

2Q.2. **What was used instead.** Two public texts, fetched today and not seated:

- Robinson and Pierpont's Byzantine text, the reading of the majority of the manuscripts (`byztxt/byzantine-majority-text`, CC0, commit 788ffe7c). 7,957 verses.
- Tischendorf's eighth edition, 1869–72, a reconstruction a generation before Nestle's (`morphgnt/tischendorf-data`, commit 795f2f4f). 7,939 verses. It comes with a lemma for every word, which is the lemmatized New Testament this file has listed as owed since section 1.

With Nestle 1904 that is two reconstructions and the majority. It is still not an apparatus: it shows where the majority and the editors part, and not which manuscripts read what.

2Q.3. **The loci.** All 58 derivations with a New Testament target run again with their verses read from each text.

| | pass |
|---|---|
| Nestle 1904 | 58 |
| Tischendorf | 56 |
| the majority text | 47 |

The patterns were written on Nestle's spelling, so most of the eleven the majority fails are the patterns' fault and not a difference of wording: λήψεται for λήμψεται (Jas 1:12), ἐδεσμεῖτο for ἐδεσμεύετο (Luke 8:29), ἐδόξασεν for ἐδόξαζεν (Luke 23:47), a case ending. Read one by one, the places where the texts differ in a member itself are these:

| Locus | Nestle | Tischendorf | majority | Revelation has | nearer Revelation |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Mark 15:45 | τὸ πτῶμα | τὸ πτῶμα | τὸ σῶμα | πτῶμα (11:8, 9) | the reconstructions |
| Mark 15:46 | ἐν μνήματι | ἐν μνήματι | ἐν μνημείῳ | μνῆμα (11:9) | the reconstructions |
| Matt 28:3; 28:4 | ὡς χιών; ὡς νεκροί | the same | ὡσεὶ χιών; ὡσεὶ νεκροί | ὡς χιών (1:14); ὡς νεκρός (1:17) | the reconstructions |
| Luke 4:17 | ἀνοίξας τὸ βιβλίον | ἀναπτύξας | ἀναπτύξας | ἀνοῖξαι τὸ βιβλίον (5:4) | Nestle alone |
| Luke 12:39 | (no watching) | (none) | adds ἐγρηγόρησεν ἂν καί | γρηγορήσῃς (3:3) | the majority, by way of Matthew's verse |
| Rev 2:10 (seed) | μέλλεις πάσχειν | πάσχειν | παθεῖν | Matt 17:12 μέλλει πάσχειν | the reconstructions' Revelation is nearer Matthew |
| Rev 20:3 (seed) | δεῖ λυθῆναι αὐτόν | δεῖ αὐτὸν λυθῆναι | δεῖ αὐτὸν λυθῆναι | Luke 13:16 ἔδει λυθῆναι | Nestle alone, in word order only |

Read. At these loci the movement is one way, and it is the other way from the one the test was set to find. Where the majority and the editors part over a word Revelation has, the editors print Revelation's word and the majority of the manuscripts has another: σῶμα for πτῶμα, μνημεῖον for μνῆμα, ὡσεί for ὡς. Five places to one.

The received account of each is ordinary and sufficient. πτῶμα is a harsh word for the body of Jesus, and copyists gave the usual one; μνημεῖον is the common word and Matthew's; the majority's Luke 12:39 is Matthew's verse copied in. No one needs Revelation to explain any of them. What the table shows is their sum at this scene: in the text most manuscripts carry, Mark's burial has lost two of the four words by which 2M.1 found it standing at Revelation 11. The copying, by its ordinary habits, took Mark away from Revelation here, and the editors, by theirs, put him back.

Two consequences for results already in the file.

a. *Mark's passion at Revelation 11* (2M.1, 2N.3) rests for half its own-form members on readings of the reconstructed text. In the majority text Mark 15:45–46 has neither πτῶμα nor μνῆμα. The finding is a finding about the text the editors print.

b. *Luke 4:17* (the derivation `rev5.4→luke4.17`, one of the four UNDO loci) stands on ἀνοίξας, which Nestle prints and neither Tischendorf nor the majority has. With ἀναπτύξας, he unrolled the book, the shared verb is gone and the derivation fails (0.6 in both). It is marked here as resting on a variant; the derivation itself is left as frozen, since a derivation changed after a run is a new one. Which reading later editions print I have from memory only (ἀναπτύξας) and have not checked in a seat.

2Q.4. **The whole.** Every Gospel verse where Nestle and the majority differ in a word: 1,697. Of the words only one side has, what share are Revelation's words (stems in Revelation, rare in the New Testament), and the same for five other books as the measure.

By count of occurrences the majority looked far richer in Revelation's words: 21.2% of what only it has, against 16.3% of what only Nestle has, a ratio of 1.30, the highest of the six books. That figure is three spellings. Ἰωάννης with two nus where Nestle has one (101 times), εὐθέως where Nestle has εὐθύς (48), Δαυίδ where Nestle has Δαυείδ (35). Each stands in Revelation in the majority's spelling.

Counted by distinct word, once for each Gospel and side:

| words of | among what only Nestle has | among what only the majority has | ratio |
|---|---|---|---|
| Revelation | 0.195 | 0.197 | 1.01 |
| Acts | 0.374 | 0.391 | 1.05 |
| Romans | 0.166 | 0.172 | 1.04 |
| Hebrews | 0.180 | 0.176 | 0.98 |
| 1 Corinthians | 0.190 | 0.193 | 1.01 |
| James | 0.072 | 0.075 | 1.04 |

Against 2,000 random vocabularies of the same size the ratio for Revelation is at the middle (random mean 0.99; 95% of them between 0.77 and 1.24; four in ten are higher). With the forty commonest differing stems taken out and occurrences counted, 0.97.

So across the Gospels the reconstructed text sits no further from Revelation, and no nearer, than the majority text does. Revelation's words are neither favoured nor disfavoured where the two part.

And in Revelation's own text (181 of 405 verses differ): the majority's words are a little more often the Gospels' words than Nestle's are (ratios 1.07 to 1.27 for the four), on some two hundred words a side, with Romans higher still at 1.72. Too few to read.

2Q.5. **What the test found.** Stated against the author's sentence and the form 2P.6 gave it.

1. The form tested was: does the printed reconstruction sit further from Revelation than the manuscripts do? Across the whole, no (2Q.4). At the loci, it sits nearer (2Q.3). In that form the prediction fails.
2. What was found instead is in the transmission and is local. At Mark's burial the text of most manuscripts has ordinary words where the reconstructed text has Revelation 11's. Function, without intent: a copyist's preference for the common word over the rare one removes, wherever the rare one is Revelation's, a point of contact between a Gospel and Revelation. Whether that is more than the general loss of rare words is what 2Q.4 measures, and it is not: Revelation's words are lost and kept at the rate of any book's.
2a. (Added at v0.19.) The whole-text measure of 2Q.4 has since failed a positive control (2R.3). Points 1 and 2 above stand only for the loci read one by one; "across the whole, no" and "at the rate of any book's" are withdrawn to: not measured.
3. What this run cannot see. Two reconstructions and one majority text are three printed texts. The author's sentence is about stemmatics, the method, and the place to test a method is its decisions: the variant units where editors chose, with the witnesses on each side. That needs Tischendorf's apparatus and Hoskier's collation of the Apocalypse in the seat, and the question put to them is the one of 2P.6.2: where a Gospel reading agrees with Revelation and its rival agrees with a Synoptic parallel or the Septuagint, which do the editors call the assimilation? Mark 15:46 is such a unit already (μνήματι with Revelation; μνημείῳ with Matthew), and there the editors chose the reading that agrees with Revelation, on the ground that the other is Matthew's. That is one case and it goes against the sentence.
4. The transmission facts that are not about wording, the count of manuscripts, the lectionary, the versions, the 1516 printing, remain unread in the seats that hold them (2P.6.1).

## 2R. A review of v0.17, verified and worked in

2R.0. Pasted by the author at 15:54, unsigned, with his word: "very solid feedback and next steps". It read v0.17 and so did not have 2Q. Each claim checked before anything was changed; five of its steps run. Scripts: `review3/rescore.py`, `yield.py`, `control.py`, `rev20.py`, `modal.py`.

2R.1. **The engine's score, taken apart** (the review, §9). The claim: Daniel 6 scores 0.45 against the guard while holding one of the seven kept members, because it is credited for lacking the four dropped ones, and most passages lack them. Checked: so. 1 of 7 and 4 of 4 make 5 of 11. The score is now reported in two parts for all 58, and the absence of a dropped member is weighed by how surprising it is (minus log₂ of its rate of absence in stretches of the target's length across the New Testament).

- What the dropped members were worth: the median derivation's dropped members, all absent, carry 0.08 of a bit together; the most any carries is 0.63. In 22 of the 46 derivations that drop anything it is under a tenth of a bit. So the "dropped absent" half of every control's score was nearly free, and the old control scores were inflated by it throughout. The review is right.
- By the kept members alone: every target holds all of its own. The best control of each derivation holds on average 0.39 of them; half or more in 29 of 58; all of them in one (John 6:49 for the manna of Heb 9:4, a one-member derivation already marked as corrected at 2G.1).
- Uniqueness with equal windows (the review's second point: a target of several verses was being compared with single verses). Every stretch of the target's own length, in the New Testament outside Revelation and in the Septuagint, searched for all the kept members. No other New Testament stretch holds them in 50 of 58; no Septuagint stretch in 53; neither in **46 of 58**. The twelve that are not alone are listed in `rescore.json`; they are the short ones (two or three members in one verse), and the two the file had already named: Ps 137:1 for Luke's angels (2B.6) and 2 Maccabees 10 for Luke 19 (2P.4 c).

The state table's line, "44 fit one verse of the New Testament", was the old search and is replaced by the 46.

2R.2. **Discovery yield, by a frozen rule** (§5). The rule, fixed before counting and applied alike to each seed: three consecutive verses of the seed and three of a Gospel sharing three or more rare pairs the Septuagint lacks; overlaps merged.

| Seed (405 verses) | candidates | read |
|---|---|---|
| Revelation | 3 | Luke 13:16; Mark 14:62 with its copy Matt 26:64. Two loci, both entered at 2P.4 |
| Acts 1–15 | 9 | the list of the twelve three times, John's baptism three times, "beginning from Galilee": eight of shared matter, one of chance |
| Acts 16–28 | 4 | the bread taken and broken twice, John's baptism, one of chance |
| Romans, 1 Corinthians | 2 | the commandments (Rom 13:9; Matt 19:18); one of chance (Rom 6:4; Matt 14:2) |
| 2 Corinthians to 1 Thessalonians | 2 | "two or three witnesses" (2 Cor 13:1; Matt 18:16), which is Deuteronomy cited by both; one weak |
| Hebrews, James, 1 Peter | 0 | |

So under a strict rule the instrument yields little and what it yields is mostly real: about sixteen of twenty across all seeds are shared matter a reader would grant. Revelation's yield is of the order of Paul's and below Acts's. The 64 of 2P.3 was a count of overlapping windows and overstated it. As a finder it is sound and small.

Discovery and rediscovery, parted (2V.1): Revelation's three candidates are two loci, and both were already found by the pair instrument at 2P.4. Loci new to the file by this rule: none.

2R.3. **A positive control for the measure of 2Q.4, which it fails** (§8, the third panel). 2Q.4 found that Revelation's words are no commoner among what only the majority text has than among what only Nestle has. The review asks for a case where the answer is known. Copyists are known to have brought Mark's wording toward Matthew's. The same measure, in Mark's verses, for Matthew's words: ratio 0.97. For Luke's verses and Matthew's words, 1.04. Nothing.

So the measure cannot see a movement that is known to be there, and its silence about Revelation at 2Q.4 says nothing. That paragraph's conclusion, "the reconstructed text sits no further from Revelation, and no nearer", is withdrawn to: this measure cannot tell. What stands from 2Q is the table of loci read one by one (2Q.3), where the editors print Revelation's word and the majority has another in five places of six.

2R.4. **Revelation 20:1–10, whole** (§1–3, §10). Forty-four members entered as chapters 11–12 were; each found in its own verse.

By its own form: 12 are in a Gospel, 8 in the Septuagint and no Gospel, 23 in Revelation only. Where the passage lands (forty verses, own forms, three members each): Luke 7:42–8:31 (chain, devil, into the abyss); Luke 12:36–13:16 (bound, must be loosed, Satan); Matt 27:36–28:9 (an angel coming down from heaven, over him, seized). No stretch of the Septuagint holds more than two. With the loosened forms the Septuagint's best is three, and one of them is to be read: *Bel and the Dragon*, with the dragon, a key and a sealing in forty-two verses.

Members that are in Luke and in neither Mark nor Matthew:

| Revelation 20 | Luke | the Septuagint has it |
|---|---|---|
| 20:3 εἰς τὴν ἄβυσσον | 8:31 (own form) | no |
| 20:3 δεῖ λυθῆναι | 13:16 (own form) | no |
| 20:5 the first resurrection | 14:14 ἐν τῇ ἀναστάσει τῶν δικαίων (sense) | no |
| 20:9 they surrounded the camp of the holy ones and the beloved city | 21:20 κυκλουμένην ὑπὸ στρατοπέδων Ἰερουσαλήμ; 19:43 (sense) | four verses |
| 20:9 fire came down from heaven and devoured them | 9:54 πῦρ καταβῆναι ἀπὸ τοῦ οὐρανοῦ καὶ ἀναλῶσαι αὐτούς; 17:29 (sense) | 4 Kgdms 1:10 (Elijah) |
| 20:10 the lake of fire and brimstone | 17:29 ἔβρεξεν πῦρ καὶ θεῖον ἀπ' οὐρανοῦ (sense) | Gen 19:24 |

Two of these are changes Luke makes to Mark that the file did not have. Mark 13:14 has the abomination standing where it should not; Luke 21:20 has Jerusalem surrounded by camps. And Luke 9:54, his alone: the disciples ask to call fire down from heaven to consume a village, and are rebuked. Rev 20:9 has the city surrounded and fire from heaven consuming the besiegers. So in Luke the surrounding falls on Jerusalem and the fire from heaven is refused. Each has its Septuagint source (Elijah's fire; the sieges of the prophets) and the usual account of 21:20, the events of the year 70, and neither needs Revelation. They are entered by sense and as pointers. What they add to 2P.4 is a count: of the six members of Rev 20:1–10 that Luke has and Mark and Matthew lack, two are by Revelation's own wording, and all six concern the adversary's confinement or the siege.

Matthew alone: the angel coming down from heaven, over him, and by loosened form the seal (27:66) and the twelve thrones of judgment (19:28, with Luke 22:30). John alone: ἐσφράγισεν (3:33; 6:27), μικρὸν χρόνον (12:35; 7:33), and ἔχεις μέρος (13:8, by sense, against 20:6 ὁ ἔχων μέρος).

The review's question at §2, whether Luke 13:10–17 as a whole stands to Revelation 20, the Sabbath to the thousand years: not run. Luke 13:14 has the six days of work and the seventh; Rev 20 has the thousand years; 2 Pet 3:8 has the day as a thousand years. That is three texts and a hypothesis, and it is entered at section 6 as a question.

2R.5. **Three axes and a seventh slot** (§4). The review separates role, action and modality, and proposes modality as a slot of its own: done, predicted, promised, required, permitted, prohibited. Entered for the twelve pairs of this cluster (`modal.py`); the seventy-seven earlier pairs are owed.

| | role | action | modality |
|---|---|---|---|
| the sealing (Matt 27:66) | exchanged | kept | kept: done |
| at the closure (Matt 28:2) | kept | opposed | kept: done |
| the deceiving (Matt 27:64) | exchanged | kept | kept: prohibited |
| bound by Satan (Luke 13:16) | exchanged | kept | kept: done |
| must be loosed (Luke 13:16) | the one loosed changes | kept | **kept: required** (δεῖ; ἔδει) |
| into the abyss (Luke 8:31) | kept | kept | done → asked not |
| authority over ten (Luke 19:17) | exchanged in side | changed | kept: permitted |
| seated (Mark 14:62) | kept | kept | done → predicted |
| to buy (Luke 22:36) | agent changes | kept | **prohibited → required** |
| the tomb (Mark 15:46) | agent changes | kept | prohibited → done |
| after three days (Matt 27:63) | the two, the one | changed | done → promised |
| the place prepared (John 14:2) | both parties change | kept | done → promised |

Modality kept in six of twelve. The review's reading of Luke 13:16 is borne out in the form: the necessity is the thing kept. δεῖ λυθῆναι of Satan and ἔδει λυθῆναι of the woman are the same requirement with the party changed, and that is why it was found. And Luke 22:36 is, as it says, not a negation removed: a thing no one is able to do becomes a thing the one who has nothing is told to do. 2J's polarity slot had recorded that as UNDO; with the modality slot it is its own kind, and the tomb (forbidden, then done) is a third. So "the negative removed" at 2M.2 b and 2P.5 covers three different changes, and the slot separates them.

2R.6. **On the printed text** (§6–8). The review proposes three layers (the copying; the editors' choices; what the apparatus shows) and four panels. Set against what 2Q had already run:

- The copying, at the loci: run, 2Q.3. The majority text is further from Revelation at Mark 15:45–46 and Matt 28:3–4.
- The background rate (the review's second panel): attempted at 2Q.4; the measure fails its control (2R.3). Owed in a form that works, which needs the parallels aligned verse to verse, that is, a synopsis in the seat.
- The editors' choices and their reasons: not run; needs Tischendorf's apparatus and the editors' own notes.
- What the apparatus shows: not run. The review's point here is new to the file and is the cheapest of the three to test once an apparatus is seated: for each of the loci, does the margin of a printed edition cite Revelation as a parallel at all? Nestle's margins do cite parallels; whether they cite Rev 20:2–3 at Luke 13:16 or Rev 11:9 at Mark 15:46 is a matter of looking. If they do not, the relation is invisible in the instrument scholars read by, whatever the text says.
- The review's stronger experiment, to reconstruct a small panel twice under the two orders with the criteria otherwise the same: Mark 15:46 is a first unit for it. Under the received order μνημείῳ is Matthew's word copied in and μνήματι is original. Under the other order μνήματι could as well be Revelation's word copied in. The same rule, prefer the reading that does not agree with a parallel, gives opposite verdicts according to which book is allowed to be a parallel. That is the review's closure, stated on one unit, and it is an argument about a rule and not a count.

2R.7. **What the review did not have.** It lists the Gospel named from assigned operators as "above baseline"; 2J withdrew that and the list should read: assigned, above; computed, below; the naming was the reader's. And it proposes Erasmus as a first comparator; the seat has no Greek (2Q.1).

## 2S. By logos: three keys, and what each opens in Matthew

2S.0. The author, 16:11: "now we need to take some of the strongest transforms, and take it as a key for pursuing a possible transform. and now we're looking at it narratively and textually, not just stemmatically … if matthew were transforming revelation, and heres an example or two of one way it might be doing, how and why might it have worked across broader chunks of narrative? stemmatics will only do us so much - we're now searching by logos".

2S.1. **The method, stated.** A key is a transform already in the engine with its controls, restated as a rule about persons and deeds. From the rule: what would it do to a stretch of Matthew not yet read? Then the seat. A lock is counted as turned when the stretch shows a wording that stands in Revelation and there and almost nowhere else, or a change Matthew makes to Mark or to Luke's form of the same matter. The count of where a wording stands is by machine (`logos/check.py`); the choice of what to look for is by the rule, and by one reader. The author's criterion governs: a key that is real turns more than one lock, and nothing has to be moved to make it turn. Septuagint siblings are stated at each lock, and the locks that did not turn are listed at 2S.5.

2S.2. **Key 1: the seer's part is given to the persons of the story.** From Matt 28:4–5 (2B.3; 2J.6 c). In Rev 1:17 the seer falls as dead and is told "fear not". In Matthew the falling as dead goes to the guards and the "fear not" to the women. Rule: what the seer undergoes in the vision, someone in the narrative undergoes; and the one who shows or speaks to the seer becomes a figure of the narrative. Revelation's seer is carried, shown, made to fall, told not to, given to eat. So:

*Lock 1, the testing (Matt 4:1–10).* The seer is three times carried and shown.

| The seer in Revelation | Jesus in Matthew | where the wording stands |
|---|---|---|
| 17:3 ἀπήνεγκέν με εἰς ἔρημον ἐν πνεύματι | 4:1 ἀνήχθη εἰς τὴν ἔρημον ὑπὸ τοῦ πνεύματος | "into the wilderness" with "spirit": Matt 4:1, Mark 1:12, Rev 17:3. No verse of the Septuagint |
| 21:10 ἀπήνεγκέν με … ἐπὶ ὄρος μέγα καὶ ὑψηλόν | 4:8 παραλαμβάνει αὐτὸν … εἰς ὄρος ὑψηλὸν λίαν | a high mountain: Matt 4:8, 17:1, Mark 9:2, Rev 21:10. Luke's testing has no mountain |
| 21:10 καὶ ἔδειξέν μοι τὴν πόλιν τὴν ἁγίαν | 4:5 εἰς τὴν ἁγίαν πόλιν; 4:8 καὶ δείκνυσιν αὐτῷ | the holy city: Matt 4:5, 27:53 and four times in Revelation. Luke says Jerusalem |
| 21:11 ἔχουσαν τὴν δόξαν; 21:24 the kings bring τὴν δόξαν αὐτῶν | 4:8 πάσας τὰς βασιλείας τοῦ κόσμου καὶ τὴν δόξαν αὐτῶν | the kingdom(s) of the world: Matt 4:8 and Rev 11:15 only, New Testament and Septuagint. Luke says τῆς οἰκουμένης |
| 19:10; 22:8 ἔπεσα … προσκυνῆσαι | 4:9 ἐὰν πεσὼν προσκυνήσῃς μοι | falling to worship: Matt 4:9, Rev 19:10, 22:8, 1 Cor 14:25. Luke has no falling |
| 19:10; 22:9 ὅρα μή … τῷ θεῷ προσκύνησον | 4:10 ὕπαγε Σατανᾶ … κύριον τὸν θεόν σου προσκυνήσεις | the Septuagint's verse as seated, Deut 6:13, has φοβηθήσῃ, fear, and not worship |

The whole scene, then. In Revelation an angel carries the seer into a wilderness in spirit, and to a great high mountain, and shows him the holy city and its glory; the seer falls to worship the one who shows him, and is told: do not; worship God. In Matthew the Spirit leads Jesus into the wilderness; the devil takes him to the holy city, and to a very high mountain, and shows him the kingdoms of the world and their glory, and asks to be fallen before and worshipped; and Jesus says: go; you shall worship the Lord your God.

Every station of the seer's tour is there, and two things are turned. The guide's side is reversed, the angel become the devil. And the error changes hands: in the vision the one shown offers the worship and the guide refuses it; in the Gospel the guide demands it and the one shown refuses. The answer, "worship God", is the same sentence and passes from the angel's mouth to Jesus's. Five of the six wordings above are Matthew's and not Luke's in a story the two share, so they are choices of Matthew's telling; and the citation both give has a verb the seated Septuagint does not, and Revelation's angel does.

Sibling: Ezek 40:2, ἔθηκέν με ἐπ' ὄρος ὑψηλὸν σφόδρα, καὶ ἐπ' αὐτῷ ὡσεὶ οἰκοδομὴ πόλεως. A seer set on a very high mountain and shown a city is Ezekiel's, and Matthew's λίαν is nearer Ezekiel's σφόδρα than Revelation's μέγα. So for the mountain Matthew and Revelation may each stand on Ezekiel. Ezekiel has no wilderness in spirit, no holy city by that name, no kingdoms of the world, no falling to worship and no "worship God". And Deut 6:13 has "worship" in another form of the Greek text (from memory; the seat is one form).

*Lock 2, the mountain of the changed appearance (Matt 17:1–8).* Matthew adds three things to Mark: his face shone as the sun; the disciples fell on their faces and feared greatly; Jesus touched them and said, rise, fear not. "His face as the sun" stands at Matt 17:2, Rev 1:16 and Rev 10:1 and nowhere else in the New Testament or the Septuagint. The falling, the touch and the "fear not" are in Rev 1:17, and they are in Daniel 10 in words nearer Matthew's: Dan 10:9–12 Θ, my face to the ground; χεὶρ ἁπτομένη μου, καὶ ἤγειρέν με; μὴ φοβοῦ. Matthew has ἁψάμενος and ἐγέρθητε, Daniel's verbs; Revelation has "he laid his right hand on me". So this lock is turned by Daniel for the fall and the touch, and by Revelation alone only for the sun. The key holds here as a rule (the seer's part is the disciples') and the wording is mostly the Septuagint's. Rev 1:16–17 is thus behind two of Matthew's scenes, this and the tomb, and Daniel 10 is behind both of those and behind Rev 1 itself: 28:3 has Daniel's lightning, which Rev 1 lacks.

*Lock 3, the supper (Matt 26:26).* The seer is given something by the angel's hand and told λάβε καὶ κατάφαγε αὐτό (10:9). Mark's Jesus says λάβετε. Matthew's says λάβετε φάγετε. Take and eat as a command stands at Matt 26:26 and Rev 10:9 and in no verse of the Septuagint (Ezek 3:1 has κατάφαγε alone). One word added to Mark, and it completes Revelation's pair. A small lock, and it turned.

*Lock 4, the opening of the heavens (Matt 3:16).* Mark has the heavens torn. Matthew has ἠνεῴχθησαν οἱ οὐρανοί; Rev 4:1 and 19:11 have heaven opened. Ezek 1:1 has the same in the Septuagint. A sibling; not counted.

2S.3. **Key 2: what is done to the adversary is done to Jesus, by adversaries, and undone.** From the guard (2N.4; 2O). The angel seals the deceiver; the priests seal "that deceiver"; the angel opens. Rule: the adversary's marks and fate in Revelation are laid on Jesus in the passion by the hands of opponents, and the narrative then lifts them. So in the passion one should find Jesus dressed, named and handled as Revelation's beast, harlot and dragon are.

*Lock 1, the dressing (Matt 27:28–29).* Mark's soldiers put on him purple. Matthew's put on him χλαμύδα κοκκίνην, scarlet. κόκκινος in the New Testament: Matt 27:28, Heb 9:19, and Rev 17:3, 17:4, 18:12, 18:16, where it is the beast's colour and the harlot's dress (17:4 περιβεβλημένη πορφυροῦν καὶ κόκκινον: she has both). And first ἐκδύσαντες αὐτόν, having stripped him; the harlot is made γυμνήν (17:16). The usual account: a soldier's cloak is red, and Matthew corrects Mark's unlikely purple. That suffices for the colour alone.

*Lock 2, the reed (Matt 27:29).* Mark's soldiers strike him with a reed. Matthew's first put κάλαμον ἐν τῇ δεξιᾷ αὐτοῦ, a reed in his right hand, as a sceptre. A reed with a right hand stands in no other verse of the New Testament or Septuagint. Rev 11:1: ἐδόθη μοι κάλαμος ὅμοιος ῥάβδῳ, a reed like a rod, the only such phrase in either corpus; ῥάβδος is the sceptre (Rev 2:27; 12:5). And Rev 1:16 has the figure ἔχων ἐν τῇ δεξιᾷ χειρὶ αὐτοῦ. So the reed that is like a sceptre is given, in Matthew, as a sceptre, in mockery: a thing of the vision set as an event, at the hands of adversaries. The reed was already a member of the witnesses' passage found at the cross (2L.2); this is its second place in Matthew's passion, and the nearer one.

*Lock 3, the name (Matt 27:63–64).* ἐκεῖνος ὁ πλάνος; ἡ ἐσχάτη πλάνη. In Revelation the deceiver is the dragon (12:9; 20:3, 10) and the deception is done by one who was slain and lived: 13:14 πλανᾷ … ὃς ἔχει τὴν πληγὴν τῆς μαχαίρης καὶ ἔζησεν. The priests say of Jesus, ἔτι ζῶν, what Revelation says of the beast: a deceiver whose deceit is a return from death. Already in the engine as part of the guard; here it is seen to be the same rule as the scarlet.

*Lock 4, the exchange at the trial.* In Rev 20 the adversary is bound (ἔδησεν, 20:2) and must be loosed (λυθῆναι, 20:3; λυθήσεται … ἐκ τῆς φυλακῆς αὐτοῦ, 20:7). At the trial Jesus is bound (δήσαντες, Mark 15:1; Matt 27:2) and a bound man, a prisoner, is loosed (δεδεμένος, Mark 15:7; δέσμιον, Matt 27:16; ἀπέλυσεν). Luke alone names the prison twice (23:19, 25 ἐν τῇ φυλακῇ; εἰς φυλακήν), and it is Luke who has Rev 20:3's abyss and its "must be loosed" (2P.4). By plot this is the key exactly: the binding that belongs to the adversary falls on Jesus, and the loosing that Revelation owes the adversary is given to Barabbas. By wording it is thin, common verbs in every Gospel, and it is in Mark already, so it is no choice of Matthew's. Entered as a correspondence of plot; not counted as a lock.

2S.4. **Key 3: a stretch of Revelation becomes one told thing.** From the wedding (2.5; `rev19.9+19.21+17.14→matt22.3-14`). The file had three members. Read with keys 1 and 2 in hand, the parable holds ten, from four consecutive chapters (the unit is the engine's, a word or fixed phrase; the seven rows carry ten, three rows holding two; an earlier count here said eight):

| Matthew 22 | Revelation |
|---|---|
| 22:2–3 a king makes a wedding for his son; the called | 19:7, 9 the wedding of the Lamb; the called |
| 22:6 οἱ δὲ λοιποὶ … ἀπέκτειναν | 19:21 οἱ λοιποὶ ἀπεκτάνθησαν (the voice turned: 2J.6 a) |
| 22:7 πέμψας τὰ στρατεύματα αὐτοῦ | 19:14, 19 τὰ στρατεύματα. The word: Matt 22:7, Luke 23:11, Acts twice, Revelation three times |
| 22:7 τὴν πόλιν αὐτῶν ἐνέπρησεν | 18:8 ἐν πυρὶ κατακαυθήσεται; 17:16 (sense; another verb) |
| 22:11–12 a man without ἔνδυμα γάμου | 19:8 the bride given fine linen to wear; 16:15 blessed is the one keeping his garments |
| 22:13 δήσαντες αὐτοῦ πόδας καὶ χεῖρας ἐκβάλετε αὐτὸν εἰς τὸ σκότος | 20:2–3 ἔδησεν αὐτόν … καὶ ἔβαλεν αὐτὸν εἰς τὴν ἄβυσσον |
| 22:14 πολλοὶ … κλητοί, ὀλίγοι δὲ ἐκλεκτοί | 17:14 κλητοὶ καὶ ἐκλεκτοί |

Luke's form of the parable (14:16–24) has the supper and the called and none of the rest: no king, no son, no armies, no burned city, no garment, no binding. So the features in this table, by which Matthew's parable differs from Luke's, are from Revelation 17:14–20:3. (This sentence first said "everything". The full ledger at 2V.5 gives ten features of twenty-four, and the garment's wording to Zephaniah.) That is a stretch followed: the fall of the city, the wedding, the war, the binding, as one king's one feast. The guest bound and cast out receives the dragon's sentence of 20:2–3, which is key 2 inside the parable.

Not in order: Matthew's sequence is wedding, killing, armies, fire, garment, binding, called; Revelation's is called and chosen, fire, wedding and garment, armies, killing, binding. As 2N.2 found, the members and not the sequence.

2S.5. **Locks that did not turn**, by the same keys.

- The seer weeps because no one can open the book (5:4). Nothing in Matthew. (Luke has the book opened: 4:17, and that derivation rests on a variant, 2Q.3.)
- The seer measures the temple (11:1–2). Nothing in Matthew but the reed.
- The seer is told to write, seven times. Nothing.
- The seer hears "Come up here" (4:1; 11:12). Nothing in Matthew.
- The seer marvels at the woman and is asked why (17:6–7). Nothing.
- The adversary's number, his mark on hand and forehead, his image. Nothing in Matthew's passion.
- The harlot's cup (17:4, a golden cup full of abominations). Matthew's cup in Gethsemane and at the supper is every Gospel's; no wording of Revelation's.

So key 1 turned on the seer's being carried, shown, made to fall, and fed, and not on his weeping, measuring, writing or being called up. Key 2 turned on dress, sceptre, name and seal, and not on number, mark or cup.

2S.6. **How it would have worked, and what it does.** Asked as the author asks it: if Matthew were working from Revelation, by these three rules, what is the work? Described by its function.

1. *It gives a life its scenes.* A vision has a seer, a guide and things shown. A life has a person, companions and things that happen. Key 1 is the conversion of the one into the other: where the seer was carried, Jesus is led; where the seer fell, the disciples and the guards fall; what the seer was told, the women are told; what the seer was given to eat, the disciples are given. The scenes this yields are the ones with no ordinary cause in a life: a testing by a guide on a mountain, a shining face, an angel at a tomb. They are where a narrative of a teacher needs matter it could not get from teaching.
2. *It tells the judgment as a trial with the parties exchanged.* Revelation ends with the adversary named deceiver, bound, sealed, and at last loosed, and with the harlot stripped of her scarlet. Key 2 lays each on Jesus by the hands of those who judge him. The result is the irony the passion is known for: the king mocked with a king's things, the truthful one sealed as a liar, the guilty loosed. On this reading that irony is not a tone added to a report of events; it is what the exchange produces, mechanically, each time it is applied. And the lifting of each (the seal broken by the angel who in the vision set it) is the vindication.
3. *It makes parables by following a stretch.* Key 3: four chapters of the end told as one king's feast. Matthew's parables of the end (the tares, the net, the wedding, the virgins, the talents, the sheep and goats) are the place to try it next; only the wedding has been read this way.
4. *Where it does not reach.* Nothing here touches the Sermon, the mission, or the controversies. The keys open the frame of the Gospel, its beginning, its mountain and its end, and the parables of judgment. That agrees with what the counts found without a reader: Matthew's tie to Revelation is at the passion (2N.3), and his additions to Mark lean to Revelation's words (2D.3).

2S.7. **What this section is and is not.** It is a reading by one reader who knows both books, with each wording counted in the seat. Its hits are of two kinds and they should be kept apart. Wordings that stand only in Matthew and Revelation: the kingdoms of the world; "worship God" against the seated Deuteronomy; his face as the sun; take and eat; a reed with a right hand beside a reed like a rod; the holy city; and the eight of the wedding against Luke's none. And correspondences of plot with common words: the bound and the loosed at the trial; the stripping. The first kind can go into the engine with controls, and is owed there. The second kind cannot be tested by any instrument in this file and is recorded as reading.

On direction it says what every section has said. Each lock opens the other way too: a seer who knows Matthew's testing would send his own guide to the same mountain, and give the angel Jesus's answer. What is new is that the rules are few and the same rule opens scenes far apart, which is the author's criterion for a key. Whether three rules opening eight scenes is more than a reader can make any two books do is the question a control would answer: the same three keys tried on Matthew from another apocalypse, Daniel or 4 Ezra, by the same reader. Daniel is seated.

## 2T. Expanding transforms

2T.0. The author, 17:07: "we need to test *expanding* transforms." Every transform in the engine is a selection: a longer passage of Revelation, and a Gospel verse or scene that keeps some of its members and drops others. The other shape had not been tested: a short seed, a verse or two, and a long stretch of a Gospel that holds the seed's members and works them, each said again and again. The wedding read at 2S.4 is of that shape and was entered as a selection. Scripts: `expand/expand.py`, `expand/named.py`. Both run on lemmas, from Tischendorf's text as fetched (2Q.2); neither uses a member chosen by hand.

2T.1. **The shape, as a rule.** A seed's members are its content lemmas (any lemma in more than 120 verses of the New Testament is not content). A stretch of twelve verses EXPANDS a seed if it holds at least three of the seed's lemmas, at least half of them, and says them at least twice as many times as there are of them. The score weighs each lemma held by its rarity and by how often it is said. Fixed before any count.

2T.2. **Blind, over everything: Revelation is not expanded more than other books are.** Every verse of a seed book is a seed.

| Seed book (405 verses) | seeds with an expansion in a Gospel, per hundred | score of 40 or more |
|---|---|---|
| Revelation | 3.1 | 2 |
| Acts 1–15 | 6.3 | 0 |
| Acts 16–28 | 3.7 | 0 |
| Romans, 1 Corinthians | 3.2 | 0 |
| Hebrews, James, 1 Peter | 2.1 | 2 |
| 2 Corinthians to 1 Thessalonians | 2.3 | 0 |
| the Johannine and Petrine letters, Jude, James, the Pastorals | 7.7 | 2 |

So as a habit there is nothing: a verse of Revelation is worked up into a stretch of a Gospel about as often as a verse of Romans is, and less often than a verse of 1 John.

The instrument does see the thing where it is known to be. Its strongest hits for the other books are relations no one disputes: Jas 5:12 with Matt 5:33–37, the oaths (score 41.1); Jas 2:8 with Mark 12:28–33, the neighbour (40.1); 1 John 4:12 with John 15, abiding and love (41.8); 3 John 12 with John 5:31–39, testimony (41.1). Revelation's two at that level are of the same kind and are one place:

- Rev 14:7 with John 4:13–24 (42.5): worship said nine times, water five, the hour twice, the spring.
- Rev 21:6 with John 4:4–15 (41.7): water eight times, thirst three, the spring three.

Two verses of Revelation eight chapters apart, found separately, each expanded most by the same dialogue. By this measure John 4 stands to Rev 21:6 as the Sermon's oaths stand to James 5:12.

The seeds, exactly (2V.1): a seed is a verse with three or more content lemmas. Revelation 386 of 405 verses; Acts 378 and 378; Romans with 1 Corinthians 341; Hebrews, James, 1 Peter 376; 2 Corinthians to 1 Thessalonians 352; the remaining letters 350. Each seed has its one best stretch, so overlapping stretches are not counted twice; a tie goes to the first met.

2T.3. **Named expansions, tested by rank.** Twenty pairs written down before any number was seen: a seed of one to five verses and the stretch a reader would say expands it. The members are still the seed's lemmas by machine. Two questions each: among all stretches of that length in the four Gospels (about 3,700), how many score as high for this seed as the named one? And of 300 other seeds of the same size from Revelation, what share score as high at the named stretch?

| Seed | named stretch | holds, said | other stretches as high | random seeds as high |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Rev 21:6 the water of life, freely | John 4:7–15 | 3 of 6, 12 times | **0** | 0.003 |
| Rev 22:12–13 my reward with me; the first and the last | Matt 20:1–16, the labourers | 4 of 8, 11 times | **0** | 0.010 |
| Rev 3:3 I come as a thief | Matt 24:42–44 | 4 of 9, 6 times | **0** | 0.000 |
| Rev 18:23 the bridegroom's voice | John 3:25–30 | 2 of 10, 4 times | **0** | 0.000 |
| Rev 14:15–16 the sickle and the harvest | Mark 4:26–29 | 3 of 11, 3 times | 3 | 0.000 |
| Rev 12:1–5 the woman and the child | Matt 1:18–2:15 | 5 of 37, 12 times | 6 | 0.013 |
| Rev 22:17 let the thirsty come | John 7:37–39 | 2 of 4, twice | 11 (the best is John 4:13) | 0.003 |
| Rev 19:7–9 the wedding of the Lamb | Matt 22:1–14 | 2 of 13, 8 times | 17 | 0.033 |
| Rev 3:20 I stand at the door and knock | Luke 12:35–40 | 2 of 4, twice | 25 | 0.043 |
| Rev 2:10 faithful unto death | Matt 25:14–30, the talents | 4 of 14, 7 times | 33 | 0.010 |
| Rev 1:17–18 I was dead and am alive | Matt 28:1–10 | 3 of 13 | 59 | 0.033 |
| Rev 3:7–8 the open door | Matt 25:1–13, the virgins | 3 of 14 | 60 | 0.010 |
| Rev 3:5 the confession | Matt 10:26–33 | 1 of 8 | 80 | 0.043 |
| Rev 14:15–16 the harvest | Matt 13:36–43 | 2 of 11 | 235 | 0.10 |
| Rev 7:16–17 no more hunger or thirst | John 6:30–40 | 2 of 17 | 267 (the best is John 4:5) | 0.037 |
| Rev 2:25–26 hold fast till I come; authority | Luke 19:12–27 | 2 of 8 | 292 | 0.16 |
| Rev 14:3–4 they follow the Lamb | John 1:35–43 | 1 of 20 | 444 | 0.027 |
| Rev 20:11–15 the great white throne | Matt 25:31–46 | 2 of 21 | 1,523 | 0.62 |
| Rev 2:25–26 | Matt 25:14–30 | 0 | fails | |
| Rev 7:17 the shepherd | John 10:1–16 | 0 | fails | |

Four of twenty are the single best stretch in all four Gospels for their seed. Eight are within the best twenty-five of 3,700. (An earlier sentence here set this against a chance of one in 3,700. Withdrawn at a reviewer's word, 2V.1: a reader's pairing is informed and is no random draw.) What stands is that the reader chose only the pairing and no member, and the machine's own members put the named stretch first. Seven fail plainly, and their failure is real information. Matthew's sheep and goats is no expansion of the white throne by wording (it was by sense only at 2N.1). The talents and the minas do not work up the promise to Thyatira. And two fail on a defect of the instrument: Rev 7:17 has ποιμανεῖ, he will shepherd, and John 10 has ποιμήν, shepherd, and a lemma does not join a verb to its noun.

Three seeds named for other places turn out to point at John 4: 22:17 (its best stretch is John 4:13), 7:16–17 (John 4:5), and 14:7 by the blind run. With 21:6 that is four passages of Revelation whose strongest expansion is the one dialogue.

2T.4. **Two read.**

a. *John 4:7–24.* The seeds: Rev 21:6 ἐγὼ τῷ διψῶντι δώσω ἐκ τῆς πηγῆς τοῦ ὕδατος τῆς ζωῆς δωρεάν. Rev 7:16–17 οὐδὲ διψήσουσιν ἔτι … ἐπὶ ζωῆς πηγὰς ὑδάτων. Rev 22:17 ὁ διψῶν ἐρχέσθω … λαβέτω ὕδωρ ζωῆς δωρεάν. Rev 14:7 ἦλθεν ἡ ὥρα … καὶ προσκυνήσατε τῷ ποιήσαντι … πηγὰς ὑδάτων.

The dialogue: εἰ ᾔδεις τὴν δωρεὰν τοῦ θεοῦ … ἔδωκεν ἄν σοι ὕδωρ ζῶν (4:10). ὃς δ' ἂν πίῃ ἐκ τοῦ ὕδατος οὗ ἐγὼ δώσω αὐτῷ, οὐ μὴ διψήσει εἰς τὸν αἰῶνα, ἀλλὰ τὸ ὕδωρ ὃ δώσω αὐτῷ γενήσεται ἐν αὐτῷ πηγὴ ὕδατος ἁλλομένου εἰς ζωὴν αἰώνιον (4:14). ἔρχεται ὥρα … προσκυνήσετε τῷ πατρί (4:21, 23).

Counted in the seat. Thirst with spring in one verse: John 4:14 and Rev 21:6, in the New Testament. A spring of water: John 4:14 and Rev 7:17, 14:7, 21:6. The hour with worship: John 4:21, 4:23 and Rev 14:7. The Septuagint has each pair somewhere (Isa 35:7 for thirst and spring) and has them nowhere together.

What the expansion does. Rev 21:6 is one sentence in the first person: I will give, to the thirsty, from the spring of the water of life, as a gift. John 4:14 says every member of it again, ἐγὼ δώσω twice, the thirst, the spring, the water, the life, and 4:10 has the gift as a noun. Then the sentence is staged: the thirsty one becomes a person at a well, and the well is called a spring (4:6, πηγή twice) before the saying uses the word. The second seed is joined to the first by the phrase they share, springs of waters: Rev 14:7 commands worship of the one who made them, because the hour has come, and the dialogue turns at 4:20 from water to where one worships, and says the hour is coming. So the hinge of the dialogue, which commentators mark as an abrupt change of subject, is the join of two verses of Revelation that have one phrase in common.

b. *Matt 20:1–16, the labourers.* Matthew's alone. Rev 22:12–13: ὁ μισθός μου μετ' ἐμοῦ, ἀποδοῦναι ἑκάστῳ ὡς τὸ ἔργον ἐστὶν αὐτοῦ. ἐγὼ … ὁ πρῶτος καὶ ὁ ἔσχατος, ἡ ἀρχὴ καὶ τὸ τέλος. Matt 20:8: κάλεσον τοὺς ἐργάτας καὶ ἀπόδος τὸν μισθόν, ἀρξάμενος ἀπὸ τῶν ἐσχάτων ἕως τῶν πρώτων.

That one verse of the parable has the wage, the paying, the workers for the work, the beginning, the last and the first. Paying with wage in one verse: Matt 20:8 and Rev 22:12 in the New Testament; in the Septuagint Deut 24:15 and Jer 22:13, the law that a hired worker be paid the same day, which is the parable's premise and its sibling source. What Deuteronomy does not have is the first and the last. In Revelation they are a name of the one who pays. In the parable they are the order in which he pays, and then its moral (20:16). So the operation is the one 2S called key 1 with a turn: an attribute of the figure becomes a rule of the story. The usual account is that the parable was attached to a floating saying, "the first will be last" (Mark 10:31; Matt 19:30). That explains the frame and leaves the wage and the paying to Deuteronomy. Rev 22:12–13 has the three together in two consecutive verses.

2T.5. **What an expanding transform is, from these.** Three things, seen in both and in the wedding (2S.4).

1. *Each member is said again.* The seed's words become the stretch's commonest content words: water eight times, worship nine, the last five times, the wedding seven. A selection keeps a word once; an expansion repeats it. That is why the instrument can see it without a reader, and why the engine's present form, which asks only whether a member is present, cannot.
2. *A sentence in the first person is staged.* "I will give to the thirsty" gets a thirsty person and a place. "My reward is with me, to pay each" gets workers, a day and an evening. "Blessed are those called to the wedding supper" gets a king, a hall and guests. The promise of the vision becomes a scene or a story in which it is carried out.
3. *Two seeds are joined at a shared phrase.* John 4 joins 21:6 to 14:7 at "springs of waters". The wedding joins chapters 17 to 20. So an expansion need not have one source verse, and a test that takes one verse at a time will underrate it: John 4 scores 42 for each seed alone and has four.

The first of these is a fact of vocabulary and the other two are facts of relation; they are parted into six operations at 2V.2. And what was not found: order (the members are repeated, not followed), and any habit of it. It is a shape some passages have, and they are few.

2T.6. **Limits and what is owed.** The blind rate says expansion from Revelation is no commoner than from other books, and that stands beside the four top ranks without contradiction: a few passages, strongly. The named pairs were named by a reader who knows what the Gospels say, so the pairing is informed; the rank is not. Lemmas do not join ποιμαίνω to ποιμήν or δωρεάν to δωρεά, and two failures and one understatement come from that; roots are owed. Seeds of several verses joined at a shared phrase are owed as a search of their own: for each pair of Revelation's verses that share a rare phrase, the Gospel stretch that expands both. And the engine needs a second kind of derivation, with EXPAND and STAGE as operators and repetition as part of the check, so that John 4, the labourers and the wedding can be entered as what they are.

## 2U. Expansion in the engine; on roots; the seven promises

2U.0. The author, 17:31: "lets do it". The three operations entered at the end of 2T. Scripts: `engine/midrash.py` and `build_page.py` (two operators added), `engine/derivations.py` (three derivations), `expand/churches.py`.

2U.1. **EXPAND and STAGE in the engine.** Two operators, sixteen now. EXPAND: each named member must stand in the borrower at least a stated number of times; the check is "members said again". STAGE: a figure the seed does not have, who takes the part the seed's sentence leaves unnamed; it must be present. Both are in the Python engine and in the page's port. Three derivations entered with them:

| | seed | target | kept | said again | staged | best control, by kept members |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| the water; the hour of worship | Rev 21:6 with 14:7 | John 4:6–24 | 8 of 8, and the woman | water 5, worship 5, give 2, thirst 2, spring 2, hour 2: 6 of 6 | a woman | Gen 24:11–20, the woman at the well: 3 of 9; two of six repetitions |
| the wage; the first and the last | Rev 22:12–13 | Matt 20:1–16 | 6 of 6, and the householder | last 3, first 3, work 2, wage 2: 4 of 4 | a householder | Luke 13:22–30: 4 of 7; Deut 24:14–15: 2 of 7, one repetition |
| the wedding, as a stretch | Rev 17:14, 18:8, 19:7–9, 19:14, 19:21, 20:2–3 | Matt 22:1–14 | 10 of 10, and the king | wedding 5, called 3: 2 of 2 | a king | Luke's form of the parable: 1 of 11 |

All three pass and no control passes. The bench runs sixty-one; sixty pass with no control passing.

Two of the three failed as first written, and both failures are kept. The labourers failed on a pattern I mistyped for ἀρξάμενος; corrected, it passes. John 4 failed one repetition: I had begun the target at 4:7, and the spring is said once from there. The well is called πηγή twice in 4:6, the verse that sets the scene, and with the scene taken from its first verse the derivation passes. That is a change to the target after a run, and by the engine's own rule it is a new derivation; it is entered as one, with the first form's failure in its note. It also shows the thing 2T.4 said in prose: the staging verse uses the seed's word before the saying does.

Controls, read. For John 4 the Septuagint offers a woman at a well (Genesis 24), the thirsty called to water (Isa 55:1), and the spring of the water of life (Jer 2:13, πηγὴν ὕδατος ζωῆς). Each holds two or three of the nine and none repeats them. For the labourers Deuteronomy gives the wage and the paying and not the first and last. For the wedding Luke's own form of the same parable holds one member of eleven.

2U.2. **On roots.** The instrument of 2T rerun with the first four letters of the lemma in place of the lemma, so that a verb joins its noun. Rates, per hundred seeds:

| Seed | on lemmas (2T.2) | on roots |
|---|---|---|
| Revelation 2–3, the letters | | 6.8 |
| Revelation 1 and 4–22 | 3.1 (whole book) | 6.2 |
| Acts 1–15 | 6.3 | 13.8 |
| Romans, 1 Corinthians | 3.2 | 8.8 |
| Hebrews, James, 1 Peter | 2.1 | 5.9 |
| the Johannine and Petrine letters and the rest | 7.7 | 4.5 |

The answer of 2T.2 does not change: Revelation is not expanded in the Gospels more than other books are, and its letters are not expanded more than its visions. As a habit, promises are not turned into parables.

What roots do change is single cases. Rev 21:6 with John 4 rises from 41.7 to 51.7, the gift now joined (δωρεάν, δωρεά), and is the strongest expansion of any seed in any of the books tried. And one comes up that lemmas had ranked sixth:

**Rev 19:9 with Luke 14:6–17: score 40.5, four of five roots, said sixteen times.** Called ten times, supper three, blessed twice, wedding once.

2U.3. **Luke 14, read.** Rev 19:9: Μακάριοι οἱ εἰς τὸ δεῖπνον τοῦ γάμου τοῦ ἀρνίου κεκλημένοι. Four words: blessed, supper, wedding, called. Luke 14:7–24 is a meal at which Jesus says four things, and each takes one or two of the words:

| Luke 14 | | Revelation |
|---|---|---|
| 14:8 ὅταν κληθῇς ὑπό τινος εἰς γάμους | called, to a wedding | 19:9 |
| 14:12–14 when you make a supper, call the poor: μακάριος ἔσῃ … ἐν τῇ ἀναστάσει τῶν δικαίων | supper, call, blessed; the resurrection of the just | 19:9; 20:6 μακάριος … ὁ ἔχων μέρος ἐν τῇ ἀναστάσει τῇ πρώτῃ |
| 14:15 μακάριος ὅστις φάγεται ἄρτον ἐν τῇ βασιλείᾳ τοῦ θεοῦ | blessed, the meal | 19:9 |
| 14:16–24 ἄνθρωπός τις ἐποίει δεῖπνον μέγα, καὶ ἐκάλεσεν πολλούς … τοῖς κεκλημένοις | a great supper, the called | 19:9; 19:17 τὸ δεῖπνον τὸ μέγα τοῦ θεοῦ |

Counted in the seat. A great supper: Luke 14:16 and Rev 19:17 in the New Testament (Dan 5:1 Θ in the Septuagint, Belshazzar's). Called, with a wedding: Luke 14:8, Matt 22:3, 22:8, Rev 19:9. Blessed, with a supper or the eating of bread: Luke 14:15 and Rev 19:9, and no verse of the Septuagint. Blessed with a resurrection: Luke 14:14 and Rev 20:6 (found from the other side at 2R.4).

So one beatitude of Revelation is, in Luke, a chapter of table talk: its four words dealt out over four sayings, each said again, and the beatitude itself spoken back by a guest at 14:15. This is 2T.5's first mark, each member said again, at its plainest.

And it sets Luke beside Matthew on the same ground. Matthew's wedding (2S.4; 2U.1) is from Rev 17:14–20:3 and has the king, the armies, the burned city, the garment and the binding. Luke 14 is from Rev 19:9, 19:17 and 20:6 and has the beatitude, the great supper and the resurrection of the just. Neither has what the other has, beyond the called. That is the partition of section 2 (two Gospels keeping different halves of one sentence of Revelation) at the scale of three chapters and a whole discourse each. The usual account, that Matthew and Luke each rework one parable of Q, has to give Matthew a king's war and Luke a set of sayings on seating, from nothing. Revelation 19–20 has both sets.

2U.4. **The seven promises, each as a seed.** None meets the rule for an expansion. The best stretch for each, as found:

| Promise | best stretch in the Gospels | holds |
|---|---|---|
| Ephesus (2:7): to eat of the tree of life | John 6:47–58 | eat 6 times, life 5; two roots of six |
| Smyrna (2:10–11): the devil will cast you into prison to be tested; ten days | Luke 4:2–13, the testing | devil 4, test 2, until, cast; four of nineteen |
| Pergamum (2:17): the hidden manna | Matt 13:41–52 | hidden twice, new; three of eight |
| Thyatira (2:26–28): authority; he will shepherd them; as I also received from my Father | John 10:7–18, the good shepherd | shepherd 6, authority 2, κἀγώ; three of fifteen |
| Sardis (3:4–5): I will confess his name | Luke 12:4–15 | confess twice, before three times (the locus of 2.3) |
| Philadelphia (3:12): a pillar in the temple | Matt 26:58–69 | nothing to the purpose |
| Laodicea (3:20–21): I stand at the door and knock | Luke 11:7–18 | knock twice, open twice, door, stand, κἀγώ; five of ten, one saying short of the rule |

So the promises are not, as a set, the seeds of parables, and the guess at the end of 2T is not borne out. Three of the seven point somewhere worth reading, and each is a single phrase and not an expansion:

a. *Thyatira and the good shepherd.* ὡς κἀγὼ εἴληφα παρὰ τοῦ πατρός μου (Rev 2:28) and ταύτην τὴν ἐντολὴν ἔλαβον παρὰ τοῦ πατρός μου (John 10:18). Received from my Father, in the first person: these two and Acts 2:33 in the New Testament, no verse of the Septuagint. Both stand with ἐξουσία and with shepherding. In Revelation the shepherding is with a rod of iron and the authority is over the nations. In John the shepherd lays down his life and the authority is to lay it down and take it again. The roots found this where the lemmas of 2T.3 found nothing.

b. *Laodicea and the door.* ἕστηκα ἐπὶ τὴν θύραν καὶ κρούω· ἐάν τις ἀκούσῃ τῆς φωνῆς μου καὶ ἀνοίξῃ τὴν θύραν (Rev 3:20). The Septuagint's sibling is Song 5:2, the voice of one knocking at the door: open to me. In the Gospels the members are dealt to three places. John 10:3: the doorkeeper opens, and the sheep hear his voice; hearing a voice with a door or an opening stands at John 10:3 and Rev 3:20 only. Luke 11:5–10: a door shut, bread asked, "knock and it will be opened". Luke 13:25, his alone: those shut out stand and knock at the door (the verse has the knocking and the door by the count; the standing is from memory). In Revelation the one who stands and knocks is the Lord and the one within opens. In Luke both times it is the petitioner who knocks and the master who is within. The doer exchanged, as at Luke 13:16.

c. *Smyrna and the testing.* The devil, the testing, a count of days. It points at the scene 2S.2 read from the other side.

2U.5. **Where this leaves expansion.**

1. The engine can now state an expansion and check it: three are in, with controls, and each control holds a third or less.
2. As a habit of any Gospel, or of any part of Revelation, expansion is not found. The count has been taken three ways (2T.2; 2U.2 on roots; the letters apart) and is ordinary each time.
3. As single cases there are four that stand at the level of James 5:12 and the Sermon's oaths: John 4 (from four verses), the labourers, Matthew's wedding, and Luke 14. The last two are two Gospels expanding the same three chapters into different things.
4. What roots add is three single phrases from the promises: received from my Father; the voice and the door; and the door knocked at from the other side.

## 2V. Two reviews of v0.21, verified; the three expansions as whole demonstrations; the keys from Daniel

2V.0. The author, 17:43, with two reviews of v0.21 and an argument of the author's own on reception, which is entered at 2E.8. Each claim of the reviews was checked against the file or the seat before anything was changed. Script: `review4/check.py`. Nothing new is added to the engine in this section: the first review asks that the count of derivations stop rising until three are done whole, and that is the order followed.

2V.1. **What the reviews found, checked.**

| Review's point | Checked | Done |
|---|---|---|
| 2L.4 is cut off at the gloating row, "the third time" | the file's table is whole and runs on to its last row. The cut is in the paste: 2L.4 begins about 20,000 words in, and the reviewer's copy ends there each time | nothing to repair in the file; the reviewer needs it in two parts |
| 2T.3: "chance gives the top place once in 3,700" treats an informed pairing as a random draw | right. The reader chose the pairing because the two read alike; that is not a draw | withdrawn at 2T.3. What stands: a reader named twenty pairings, chose no member, and for four the machine's own members rank the named stretch first |
| 2S.4: eight members, seven rows | the unit was not stated. By the engine's unit, a word or fixed phrase, the table's seven rows carry ten | 2S.4 now says ten and names the unit |
| 2S.4: "everything by which Matthew differs from Luke is from Revelation" is not shown | right, and the ledger at 2V.4 shows it false: ten features of twenty-four | withdrawn at 2S.4 |
| 2B.3 still prints the old control scores | right | 2B.3 marked: its control column is as first computed; rescored at 2R.1 |
| 2R.2: discovery and rediscovery not separated | right. Revelation's three candidates are two loci, both already found by the pair instrument at 2P.4. New loci found by the frozen rule: none | said at 2R.2 |
| 2T.2: how many seeds; how overlapping windows are treated | from the run: seeds with three or more content lemmas, Revelation 386 of 405, Acts 378 and 378, Romans with 1 Corinthians 341, Hebrews with James and 1 Peter 376, 2 Corinthians to 1 Thessalonians 352, the remaining letters 350. Each seed is given its one best stretch, so overlapping stretches are never counted twice; a tie goes to the first met | said at 2T.2 |
| 2Q.3: the Luke 4:17 variant should travel with the derivation | right | the state table's row of transforms now carries it |
| "Compression was a habit at 58 loci" | the file does not say so and the counts are against it. The fifty-eight were written with their targets in view (2B.5), and the blind yield of the selecting shape is ordinary too (2R.2). Neither shape is a habit by any blind count here | the state table says it of both |
| The editors' readings "restored" toward Revelation are evidence for the thesis | narrower than that. At Mark 15:45–46 the editors print what the oldest copies have (from memory: the fourth-century codices; the apparatus is not seated) and the majority has the commoner word. Older copies nearer Revelation, later copies further: that is one datum for 2E.8, and it does not show which way the first borrowing ran | 2E.8 |
| The printed text and the expansions each need a row of the table | both rows were in the table at v0.21 | the table is now grouped, which makes them findable |
| The seven within seven has not moved since v0.10 | right | the table says so |

2V.2. **Expansion is not addition: six operations kept apart.** From the first review, adopted, with what the engine can check for each.

| Operation | What it does | What is checked |
|---|---|---|
| ADD | brings in what the seed does not have | the new member is present (in the engine) |
| REPEAT (the engine's EXPAND) | says a member of the seed again | the count (2U.1) |
| STAGE | supplies a figure and a setting in which the seed's sentence is carried out | the figure is present (2U.1) |
| JOIN | connects two seeds at a member both hold | the hinge stands in each seed and in the target, between what the one seed gives and what the other gives |
| ENACT | a name or a promise becomes a deed that orders the story | the name's members stand in one verse of the target as the terms of a verb of doing |
| EXPAND, in the review's sense | unfolds what a relation already implies: a gift implies one in need | not checkable by any count here; recorded as reading |

So 2T.5's first mark (each member said again) is REPEAT, a fact about vocabulary; its second and third are STAGE and JOIN, facts about relations. The engine's operator called EXPAND checks only the first. JOIN and ENACT are checked below by hand and are owed in the engine.

And the levels at which a source can explain a target, also from the review: the word; the relation (who does what to whom); the event; the passage from one subject to the next; the holding together of unlike things in one composition. The three demonstrations are at the last three levels in turn.

2V.3. **John 4:6–24, whole.**

*The received account.* A scene of the type of the patriarchs' meetings at a well (Genesis 24; 29; Exodus 2), at a well the Gospel calls Jacob's. Living water is Scripture's phrase. The turn to worship follows from the story: she takes Jesus for a prophet and asks the Samaritans' question, and 4 Kingdoms 17 is the account of their worship.

*The Septuagint, with the same freedom Revelation was given.* Sixteen passages, searched for the nine members of the derivation: the wells of Genesis 24 and 29 and Exodus 2, the water of Exodus 17 and Numbers 21, Isaiah 12:3, 44:3, 49:10, 55:1, Jeremiah 2:13 and 17:13, Zechariah 14:8, Ezekiel 47, Psalm 35:10, Sirach 24:21, 4 Kingdoms 17, Deuteronomy 12. The best single passage holds four of nine (Jer 17:13). All together hold eight of nine: everything but the gift. Revelation's four verses (21:6; 14:7; 22:17; 7:16–17) also hold eight of nine: everything but the woman. On presence it is a tie, six or more passages of Scripture against four verses of Revelation.

Three things Scripture has that Revelation lacks. The woman at the well. "Living water", ὕδωρ ζῶν: John 4:10, 4:11 and 7:38 in the New Testament, nine verses of the Septuagint (Gen 26:19, Zech 14:8, Song 4:15 among them); Revelation says ὕδωρ ζωῆς, water of life, three times, and so does Jer 2:13. In its own phrase John is with Zechariah and Genesis. And "not yet the hour" at Jacob's well itself: Gen 29:7, οὔπω ὥρα.

Three things Revelation has that Scripture lacks, each a pairing inside one verse. The gift with the water (δωρεάν, δωρεά): John 4:10, Rev 21:6, Rev 22:17, and no verse of the Septuagint. The hour with worship: John 4:21, 4:23, Rev 14:7; in the Septuagint only Daniel 3:5–6, 15, the hour of falling down to the image. Thirst with giving: John 4:14, 4:15, Rev 21:6; in the Septuagint Isa 44:3 (ἐγὼ δώσω ὕδωρ ἐν δίψει) and one other.

*The seam.* The review's point is that the source should explain why the talk passes from water to worship. JOIN, checked: "spring(s) of water(s)" stands at Rev 21:6 and Rev 14:7, and in John at 4:14, the last verse of the water before the turn. So the hinge is where a join would put it. But the Septuagint has a seam of its own, which the file had not seen: Zechariah 14. At 14:8 living water goes out from Jerusalem; at 14:16–17 the nations go up to Jerusalem to worship. Living water, then where one must worship, Jerusalem named in both: that is John 4:10 and 4:20 in order, in Zechariah's phrase. And John's story gives its own reason for the turn.

*Reverse.* A seer who knew John 4 would have had to part one scene into two verses seven chapters apart, turn "living water" into "water of life" three times, and drop the woman. Revelation's "springs of waters" needs no John: it is one of the book's own four (earth, sea, rivers and springs: 8:10; 14:7; 16:4).

*Residue.* Not from Revelation: the woman, Jacob, the husbands, the mountain, salvation from the Jews, spirit and truth, the Messiah. Not from the sixteen passages: the gift.

*Gain, by level.* Word and relation: three pairings Scripture lacks. Passage from subject to subject: Revelation explains it by a hinge at 4:14; Zechariah 14 explains it by sequence and in John's own phrase; the story explains it by itself. Not a gain Revelation holds alone. This demonstration is weaker after the control than 2T.4 left it.

2V.4. **Matthew 20:1–16, whole.**

*The received account.* The law of the hired worker, Deut 24:15: αὐθημερὸν ἀποδώσεις τὸν μισθὸν αὐτοῦ, οὐκ ἐπιδύσεται ὁ ἥλιος. And the saying that the first will be last, which stands before the parable (19:30, from Mark 10:31) and after it (20:16). The parable is told to carry that saying. The order of payment is needed by the plot: the first must see what the last receive.

*Counted.* Deuteronomy gives the wage, the paying, and the evening (ὀψίας γενομένης, Matt 20:8), which Revelation does not have. Mark gives first and last. Two sources, both certainly known to Matthew, cover the members and one more.

What Revelation has. The two things in adjacent verses, spoken by one voice: Rev 22:12 ὁ μισθός μου μετ' ἐμοῦ, ἀποδοῦναι ἑκάστῳ, and 22:13 ὁ πρῶτος καὶ ὁ ἔσχατος, ἡ ἀρχὴ καὶ τὸ τέλος. Its own sources are in the seat: Isa 40:10 (ἰδοὺ ὁ μισθὸς αὐτοῦ μετ' αὐτοῦ καὶ τὸ ἔργον) and Ps 61:13 with Prov 24:12 (paying each by works). The name is not in the Septuagint: "the first and the last" with ἔσχατος stands at Rev 1:17, 2:8, 22:13 and in no verse of it; Isaiah's Greek has "I am first and I am after these" (44:6; so 41:4, 48:12). So in Scripture the wage and the first-and-last do not meet, and the second is not there in that form.

ENACT, checked. Matt 20:8: ἀπόδος τὸν μισθόν, ἀρξάμενος ἀπὸ τῶν ἐσχάτων ἕως τῶν πρώτων. Wage, pay, beginning, last, first: five members of Rev 22:12–13 in one verse, the name's three as the terms of the paying. A wage with first or last in one verse: Matt 20:8 alone in the New Testament; in the Septuagint Isa 62:11 by chance (ἕως ἐσχάτου τῆς γῆς, with the wage of Isa 40:10 repeated).

*Reverse.* Revelation needs no Matthew here: 22:12 is Isaiah 40:10 with the Psalm. Read the received way the two are independent, and what they share is that each sets a first-and-last beside a wage.

*Residue.* Not from Revelation: the vineyard, the hours, the denarius, the agreement, the grumbling, the evil eye, ἑταῖρε, the evening. Nearly all of the story.

*Gain, by level.* The event: Revelation alone has the wage and the name as one utterance, and Matthew alone has them as one deed. That is the whole of it. The received account has every member and a reason for the order. What the forward reading adds is that Matthew's frame-saying and Matthew's story, which the received account must bring from two places, lie two verses apart in a third.

2V.5. **Matthew 22:1–14, whole, with the ledger the review asked for.**

Every feature of Matthew's parable, twenty-seven, counted in Luke 14:16–24 and in Revelation. Luke has three (the called, the readiness, the house filled). The twenty-four that are Matthew's alone:

| Where it stands | features | count |
|---|---|---|
| Revelation 17:14; 18:8; 19:7–20:3 | king, wedding, killed, armies, burned, gathered (19:17 συνάχθητε to the great supper), garment, bound, cast, chosen | 10 |
| Revelation elsewhere, common words | son, angered, destroyed, worthy, many | 5 |
| Matthew's own stock | the outer darkness (Matt 8:12; 22:13; 25:30 only), the weeping, the gnashing, ἑταῖρε (Matt 20:13; 22:12; 26:50 only) | 4 |
| the Septuagint | the beasts slaughtered | 1 |
| nowhere found | the insult, bad and good, the silence, the few | 4 |

So ten of twenty-four, and the sentence at 2S.4 is withdrawn. Two rows need a note. The garment is in the ten by sense: Rev 19:8 has περιβάληται βύσσινον, and Matthew has ἐνδεδυμένον ἔνδυμα. The Septuagint has Matthew's wording: Zeph 1:7–8, the Lord has made ready his sacrifice, and will punish πάντας τοὺς ἐνδεδυμένους ἐνδύματα ἀλλότρια, all those clothed in strange clothing. (The seat's line at 1:7 is corrupt with apparatus where, from memory, it has "he has hallowed his called".) A feast made ready, the guests, and judgment on the wrongly clothed: Zephaniah has the garment scene. And Prov 9:2–3 has the rest of the summons: ἔσφαξεν τὰ ἑαυτῆς θύματα, ἡτοιμάσατο τὴν ἑαυτῆς τράπεζαν, ἀπέστειλεν τοὺς ἑαυτῆς δούλους. The servants sent twice and killed are Matthew's own preceding parable: Matt 21:36 πάλιν ἀπέστειλεν ἄλλους δούλους is Matt 22:4 word for word.

*The received account.* One parable of the double tradition, told by Luke plainly; Matthew allegorizes it with the tenants just told, the burning of Jerusalem, and his own ending.

*The conjunction.* The review's test: does the source explain an odd holding together? In Matthew a war is fought and a city burned while a dinner stands ready. Wisdom's feast and Zephaniah's have no army; the tenants have killing and no city. Revelation 19 has the wedding (19:7–9), the armies (19:14, 19), the rest killed (19:21) and a supper gathered (19:17) as one chapter, with the city burned a chapter before and the binding a chapter after. The received account explains the burned city by the year 70, which is an event and not a text, and explains the war's place inside the feast by Matthew's lack of care for the story. Here Revelation explains something no other seated text does: six unlike things together, wedding, armies, killing, burning, binding, the called and chosen.

*Reverse.* A seer who knew Matthew's parable would have spread one short story over four chapters. Those chapters have frames of their own that owe Matthew nothing (the birds' supper is Ezekiel 39, from memory).

*Gain, by level.* Holding together: yes, and it is the one gain of the three that the controls leave standing. Word: the garment's wording goes to Zephaniah.

2V.6. **The keys of 2S, tried from Daniel.** The control owed at 2S.7, run before the keys are carried further, as the review asks. Daniel is given what Revelation was given: any region, the parts dealt to other persons, a stretch taken as one thing. Wordings counted by pattern in Matthew, the other Gospels, Revelation, and both Greek Daniels.

| Wording | Matthew | other Gospels | Revelation | Daniel |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| the furnace of fire | 13:42, 50 | none | none | fourteen verses of ch. 3 |
| συντέλεια, the consummation | five times | none | none | twenty-three |
| thrones set; sitting on the throne | 19:28; 25:31 | none | none | 7:9 |
| authority was given (ἐδόθη … ἐξουσία) | 28:18 | none | 13:5, 7, of the beast | 7:14 |
| the stone sealed | 27:66 | none | none | 6:17 |
| his appearance as lightning | 28:3 | none | none | 10:6 |
| the righteous shine out (ἐκλάμψουσιν) | 13:43 | none | none | 12:3 Θ has λάμψουσιν; the pattern met 12:4 |
| λικμήσει, will crush to dust | 21:44 | Luke once | none | 2:44 Θ |
| the abomination of desolation | 24:15, citing Daniel by name | Mark | none | 9:27; 11:31; 12:11 |
