| affliction such as has not been | 24:21 | Mark | none | 12:1 Θ |
| the Son of Man, the clouds of heaven | 24:30; 26:64 | Mark | none in this form | 7:13 |
| **in Revelation and not in Daniel** | | | | |
| scarlet | 27:28 | none | 17:3, 4; 18:12, 16 | none |
| a reed | 27:29, 30 | Mark | 11:1; 21:15, 16 | none |
| the kingdoms of the world | 4:8 | none | 11:15 | none |
| his face as the sun | 17:2 | none | 1:16; 10:1 | none |
| white as snow | 28:3 | none | 1:14 | 7:9 in another order of words (ὡσεὶ χιὼν λευκόν) |

Key 1, the seer's part given to the persons: turns from Daniel 10, as 2S.3 already said of the mountain, and at the tomb (the appearance as lightning). Key 3, a stretch become one told thing: Daniel 7:9–14 against Matthew's sheep and goats holds eight of ten members (throne, glory, Son of Man, all the nations, kingdom, fire, eternal, the attending host); Revelation's white throne holds two. The scene that failed from Revelation at 2N.1 and 2T.3 is Daniel's. Key 2, the adversary's marks laid on Jesus: does not turn. Daniel has no scarlet, no reed, no deceiver. It has purple, put on Daniel in honour (5:29), and purple is what Mark and John have at the mocking; Matthew alone has scarlet, and Rev 17:4 has both words.

What the control shows. Keys 1 and 3 are not Revelation's: one reader with the same freedom opens as much of Matthew from Daniel, and opens Matthew's own material better, at six wordings Matthew alone has among the Gospels and Revelation lacks. Matthew read Daniel directly; nothing here needs Revelation between them. Key 2 is Revelation's alone of the two.

The question the review sets in its place: where does Matthew have Daniel's matter in a state that Revelation has and Daniel does not? Found so far, three. Matt 24:30, Daniel's clouds with Zechariah's mourning tribes, as at Rev 1:7 (section 2). Matt 17:2, his face as the sun, in a scene otherwise Daniel 10's, as at Rev 1:16. Matt 28:3, where Daniel's lightning (10:6) is joined to "white as snow", in Revelation's order of words (1:14) and not Daniel's. Three, each one phrase.

2V.7. **The classes of 2S.7, parted three ways** as the review asks. (a) A wording that stands in Matthew and Revelation and nowhere else seated: the kingdoms of the world; his face as the sun; scarlet among the Gospels. (b) Words each found elsewhere whose combination is found only in the two: called with chosen; a reed with the right hand beside a reed like a rod; take with eat. (c) Likeness of sense with other words: the garment; the burned city; the bound and the loosed. Only (a) and (b) can go to the engine. (c) is reading, and at the garment it has now lost to Zephaniah.

2V.8. **Where the three stand.** After multi-source controls: John 4 keeps three pairings and loses its seam as a gain Revelation holds alone. The labourers keep one thing, the wage and the name met in one verse, against a received account that is complete. The wedding loses the claim to all of Matthew's difference and keeps the conjunction, ten features of twenty-four from three chapters, which no other seated text holds together. The review's last point is right as a statement of what is being looked for: a source that holds less than it generates. The controls say how often that can be shown here: once clearly, of three.

## 2W. The conjunction, searched for

2W.0. The author, 17:59: "can we take what we learned at this locus about how expansion works and use it to find more?" What was learned at the wedding (2V.5), put as an instrument. The thing that stood after controls was neither the repeated word nor the staged figure. It was a conjunction: unlike things held together in one passage of a Gospel, held together in one short stretch of Revelation, and in no other seated text. So the search asks only that. Scripts: `conj/conj.py`, `conj/read.py`.

2W.1. **The instrument.** A passage is twelve verses of a Gospel, taken every three verses: 1,245. A stretch is sixty verses, taken every fifteen: 24 of Revelation, 1,801 of the Septuagint, 205 of the New Testament outside the Gospels. Words are crude stems made the same way in all three (the Septuagint has no lemmas seated, so none are used anywhere). A stem weighs by its rarity in the New Testament; stems in more than 120 verses are dropped. The score of a passage with a stretch is the summed weight of the stems both hold. A source wins a passage when its best stretch outscores every stretch of the other seventy-six sources. Nothing is chosen by hand.

2W.2. **Blind, as a rate: ordinary again.**

| Source | stretches | passages won | per stretch |
|---|---|---|---|
| Isaiah | 83 | 226 | 2.7 |
| Acts | 64 | 158 | 2.5 |
| Jeremiah | 83 | 126 | 1.5 |
| Judges | 39 | 71 | 1.8 |
| Judith | 20 | 58 | 2.9 |
| **Revelation** | 24 | 58 | 2.4 |
| 2 Maccabees | 34 | 57 | 1.7 |
| Tobit | 14 | 37 | 2.6 |
| 1 John | 4 | 27 | 6.8 |

Revelation wins Gospel passages at the rate of Acts, Isaiah and Judith. This is the fourth instrument to say the same (2D.4; 2R.2; 2T.2). No habit.

2W.3. **Calibration.** Ranked by how far Revelation's best stretch stands above the best of anything else, the list finds blind what the file reached by reading: the tomb with Rev 10–13 second; the wedding with Rev 17:7–20:3 fifth; the Sermon's oaths with Rev 8–12 tenth; Matt 24:25–36 with Rev 6 eighteenth. It does not find the expansions of few words: for Luke 14 Revelation ranks nineteenth of seventy-seven sources, for John 4 fifth, for the labourers second. So it sees a conjunction of rare unlike words and not a short seed said again, which is what it was built for.

2W.4. **What it pointed to that the file did not have.** The first of the whole list, and five more, each read in the seat.

**a. The seventy return: Luke 10:17–20, from Rev 9:1–4.** First of 1,245 passages, +22.8 over the best other stretch (Judith 6–8).

| Luke 10 | Revelation 9 |
|---|---|
| 10:18 ἐθεώρουν τὸν Σατανᾶν ὡς ἀστραπὴν ἐκ τοῦ οὐρανοῦ πεσόντα | 9:1 ἀστέρα ἐκ τοῦ οὐρανοῦ πεπτωκότα εἰς τὴν γῆν; 12:9 the Satan cast to the earth |
| 10:19 δέδωκα ὑμῖν τὴν ἐξουσίαν τοῦ πατεῖν ἐπάνω ὄφεων καὶ σκορπίων | 9:3 ἐδόθη αὐταῖς ἐξουσία ὡς ἔχουσιν ἐξουσίαν οἱ σκορπίοι; 9:19 tails like serpents |
| 10:19 οὐδὲν ὑμᾶς οὐ μὴ ἀδικήσει | 9:4 ἵνα μὴ ἀδικήσουσιν … εἰ μὴ τοὺς ἀνθρώπους οἵτινες οὐκ ἔχουσιν τὴν σφραγῖδα |
| 10:20 τὰ ὀνόματα ὑμῶν ἐνγέγραπται ἐν τοῖς οὐρανοῖς | 9:4 the seal; 13:8, 17:8 the name written in the book |

Counted. Fallen from heaven: Luke 10:18, Rev 8:10, 9:1; in the Septuagint Isa 14:12 and Job 1:16. The Satan with heaven in one verse: Luke 10:18 alone. A scorpion with authority in one verse: Luke 10:19, Rev 9:3, 9:10 (the noun stands at Luke 10:19, 11:12 and Rev 9:3, 5, 10; my pattern also caught the verb "scatter", and the Septuagint's three hits are that verb, one read, two not). Not to be hurt, with ἀδικέω: Luke 10:19, Rev 9:4, with Rev 6:6 and 7:3. A name written, in heaven or in the book: Luke 10:20, Rev 13:8, 17:8, no verse of the Septuagint by the pattern.

Four verses of Revelation, three verses of Luke, the same four things in the same order: a fall from heaven, authority and scorpions, a command not to hurt, and those marked as God's. The side is exchanged, as at Luke 13:16: in Revelation the authority is given to the scorpions and they hurt all but the sealed; in Luke it is given over the scorpions to those whose names are written, and nothing hurts them.

The received reading: Ps 90:13 for the treading (asp, basilisk, lion, dragon: none of Luke's words), Deut 8:15 for serpent and scorpion together (ὄφις δάκνων καὶ σκορπίος; the only verse beside Luke's with both), Isa 14:12 for the fall. Three sources for three verses, none with the authority, the not hurting, or the names.

The wider passage is what the machine scored: sent two by two (10:1), sackcloth and Sodom (10:12–13), the street of the city (10:10), woe; and Rev 11:3 two witnesses in sackcloth, 11:8 the street of the city called Sodom. Those are the double tradition's (Matt 10:15; 11:21–24) and are common in the Septuagint. Luke's own verses, 10:17–20, are the ones that are Revelation 9's.

**b. The saying at the supper: Luke 22:28–32, from the seven letters.** +4.0; the passage stands with Rev 1:1–3:11.

| Luke 22 | Revelation 2–3 |
|---|---|
| 22:28 ἐν τοῖς πειρασμοῖς μου | 2:10 ἵνα πειρασθῆτε; 3:10 τῆς ὥρας τοῦ πειρασμοῦ |
| 22:29 κἀγὼ διατίθεμαι ὑμῖν καθὼς διέθετό μοι ὁ πατήρ μου βασιλείαν | 2:28 ὡς κἀγὼ εἴληφα παρὰ τοῦ πατρός μου; 3:21 ὡς κἀγὼ … μετὰ τοῦ πατρός μου |
| 22:30 ἵνα ἔσθητε καὶ πίνητε ἐπὶ τῆς τραπέζης μου | 3:20 δειπνήσω μετ' αὐτοῦ καὶ αὐτὸς μετ' ἐμοῦ |
| 22:30 καθήσεσθε ἐπὶ θρόνων | 3:21 δώσω αὐτῷ καθίσαι μετ' ἐμοῦ ἐν τῷ θρόνῳ μου |
| 22:31 ὁ Σατανᾶς | 2:9, 13, 24; 3:9 |
| 22:32 ἡ πίστις σου | 2:10 γίνου πιστός; 2:13, 19 |
| 22:32 στήρισον τοὺς ἀδελφούς σου | 3:2 στήρισον τὰ λοιπά |

Counted. Στήρισον, strengthen, as a command: Luke 22:32 and Rev 3:2 in the New Testament. A throne with eating in one verse: Luke 22:30 alone; Rev 3:20 and 3:21 are adjacent. A seat on a throne promised: Luke 22:30, Matt 19:28, Rev 3:21, Acts 2:30. Κἀγώ with "my Father" in one verse, outside John: Luke 22:29, Rev 2:28, Rev 3:21, and Matt 10:32–33, which is the confession, already in the engine from Rev 3:5. Outside John that construction stands only where the letters to the churches stand.

This answers 2U.4. Taken one at a time, none of the seven promises is worked up into a passage. Here five of the letters (Smyrna, Thyatira, Sardis, Philadelphia, Laodicea) give seven members to one saying of five verses, spoken at the supper. It is a JOIN and not an expansion. Matthew's form of the saying (19:28) has the thrones and none of the rest.

The received reading: a saying of the double tradition (the thrones), with Luke's own matter on Simon; the table is the Psalm's, the thrones Daniel 7's, the sifting Job's. That accounts for throne, table and Satan, each from a different book, and not for κἀγώ with the Father or for στήρισον.

**c. The woes: Matt 23:25–35, from Babylon, Rev 17:4–6 and 18:24.** +7.4, and Luke's form (11:39–50) +5.6 with the same stretch.

The cup, full: Matt 23:25, Luke 11:39, Rev 17:4, and no verse of the Septuagint (γέμω with ποτήριον). Full, with uncleanness: Matt 23:27 and Rev 17:4 only. Blood on the earth: Matt 23:35 πᾶν αἷμα δίκαιον ἐκχυννόμενον ἐπὶ τῆς γῆς and Rev 18:24 αἷμα προφητῶν καὶ ἁγίων … καὶ πάντων τῶν ἐσφαγμένων ἐπὶ τῆς γῆς, with Rev 6:10 and Col 1:20, and no verse of the Septuagint. The blood of prophets: Matt 23:30, Luke 11:50, Rev 16:6, 18:24. Gold: Matt 23:16–17, Rev 17:4. Woe: seven in Matthew 23; doubled three times over Babylon in Revelation 18.

So the woman's golden cup, full of abominations and unclean things, and the blood of the prophets and of all slain on the earth found in her, are in Matthew the cup of the scribes and Pharisees, clean outside and full within, and the blood of all the righteous shed on the earth coming on them. Babylon's marks laid on the teachers of Jerusalem. It is 2S's second key with the bearer changed: there the adversary's marks were laid on Jesus by his adversaries; here they are laid by Jesus on them. This is the double tradition's matter and Luke has it, more thinly.

**d. Three lesser, noted.** *The sower.* Καυματίζω, to scorch: Mark 4:6, Matt 13:6, Rev 16:8, 16:9, and nowhere else in the New Testament (the Septuagint's hits are the noun in the dative, by the one read). Mark 4:6 has the sun, the scorching and the drying in one verse; Rev 16:8–12 has the sun, the scorching and the drying in five. The sickle with the harvest: Mark 4:29, Rev 14:15–16; Joel 3:13, the usual source, has sickles and a vintage. *Gethsemane.* Watching with an hour: Matt 26:40, Mark 14:37, Matt 25:13, Rev 3:3. An hour with temptation in one verse: Rev 3:10 alone; Matt 26:40–41 has them in two. Sardis and Philadelphia again, on the same night as (b). *The mountain's frame.* Matt 16:27, "he will pay each according to his deed", six verses before the high mountain: paying each stands at Matt 16:27, Rev 22:12 and Rom 2:6 (Ps 61:13). With 2S.3's high mountain (Rev 21:10) and the tents Peter offers (Rev 21:3, the tent of God with them), Rev 21:3–22:12 lies under Matt 16:27–17:4. The tent is too common a word to count.

**e. Read and set aside.** The anointing at Bethany with Rev 2–6 (+16.5): a denarius, a thief, a supper, each in a different letter or seal and no relation between them. The rich one with Rev 13–17 (+16.5 in Luke, +9.3 in Mark): selling, rich and poor, following; common words. The feeding with Rev 7–11: green grass (Mark 6:39; Rev 8:7) and nothing else. Matt 6:1–12 with Rev 2–5: the shut door is Isa 26:20's. John 21 with Rev 14–18: ἀρνίον, which stands at John 21:15 and twenty-seven times in Revelation and nowhere else in the New Testament, with the doer exchanged (Rev 7:17 the Lamb shepherds them; John 21:15 feed my lambs); one word, worth its own line.

2W.5. **What this is.** The shape learned at the wedding does find more: of the twenty-six passages where Revelation stands furthest above everything else, four were already in the file by reading, three are new and hold in the seat with pairings the Septuagint lacks (a, b, c), three are lesser, and the rest are scatter. That is a yield a reader can check line by line, from a search with no member chosen. And the rate says what every rate has said: Revelation is not behind the Gospels more often than Acts or Isaiah is behind them. Where it is, it is there as a short stretch holding several unlike things that a Gospel passage holds together.

Two things about the three. Each is a join of several places in a few chapters of Revelation into a few verses of a Gospel, and two are Luke's own verses (10:17–20; 22:28–32) set inside matter of the double tradition. And the operations are ones already named: the side exchanged (a), several seeds joined (b), the adversary's marks transferred (c). No new operator was needed.

Limits. The stems are crude and treat every corpus alike, which is fair and blunt. Sixty verses is one size; a conjunction drawn from a longer or shorter stretch is missed. The three are not yet in the engine with controls of their own; the search's control is the 1,801 stretches of the Septuagint, which is wide and not aimed. Direction: each reads the other way as it always has, and (b) most easily, since a seer writing to seven churches could deal one saying of the supper among them.

## 2X. The three in the engine; other sizes and other targets; the letters and the night of the supper

2X.0. The author, 18:06: "lets do it". The three operations owed from 2W. Scripts: `engine/derivations.py` (three added), `conj/conj2.py`, `conj/night.py`.

2X.1. **The three in the engine, with controls aimed at each.** A new family, Conjunctions. Declared before the run; all three passed as first written.

| | seed | target | kept | best control |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| the fall, the scorpions, the marked | Rev 9:1, 3–4 | Luke 10:17–20 | 7 of 7, in the seed's order; 4 dropped members absent | none of seven holds a member |
| the saying at the supper | Rev 2:9–10; 2:28; 3:2; 3:10; 3:20–21 | Luke 22:28–32 | 9 of 9; 5 dropped absent | Matthew's form (19:27–30) and John 15:9–15: 2 of 9 each |
| Babylon's cup, the blood on the earth | Rev 17:4, 6; 18:24 | Matt 23:25–35 | 7 of 7; 7 dropped absent; blood, full and cup each said again | Luke's form of the woes: 4 of 7; Lam 4:13–14: 3 of 7 |

The bench runs sixty-four; sixty-three pass with no control passing. Searched over every verse of the seated New Testament and Septuagint, each emission passes at its target and nowhere else.

Three notes, each against the result.

a. *Luke 10.* My pattern for the scorpion was written to keep out the verb "scatter" and so does not match the nominative σκορπίος or the accusative σκορπίον. Deut 8:15 and Luke 11:12 have those forms. By a fair pattern each control holds one member of seven and not none. The derivation is left as run and the defect is said here. The order is the thing: seven members, emitted in the seed's order, found in Luke in that order.

b. *The woes.* Luke's form holds four of seven: the cup, the full, the blood, the prophets. It lacks the uncleanness, the righteous, and "on the earth". So the shared tradition already has Babylon's cup, and Matthew has three members more of the same two verses of Revelation. And Lamentations 4:13 is a true sibling for Matt 23:35: prophets, and αἷμα δίκαιον shed in the midst of the city. The righteous blood is Lamentations' phrase. What it lacks is the cup, the full, the unclean, the earth.

c. *The supper.* The supping is by sense, two verbs (δειπνήσω; ἔσθητε), as the burning was at the wedding.

2X.2. **Other sizes, and other targets.**

| Run | Revelation wins | a stretch | place among sources with eight or more stretches |
|---|---|---|---|
| Gospels, stretches of 30 verses | 38 | 0.79 | 10th of 58 |
| Gospels, stretches of 60 (2W) | 58 | 2.4 | 5th of about 50 |
| Gospels, stretches of 120 | 46 | 4.2 | 4th of 35, after Acts, Isaiah, Judith |
| Acts in the Gospels' place, 60 | 2 of 331 | 0.08 | 18th of 42 |
| the letters in the Gospels' place, 60 | 6 of 866 | 0.25 | 19th of 38 |

Two things. Across sizes the list is stable: Luke 10 is second at thirty verses and the supper saying, the wedding, the sower and John 21 are all in the first twelve; at a hundred and twenty the tomb and the sower hold and Matt 26:39–50, the garden, comes first of all with Rev 1–7. And the relation is the Gospels'. Put Acts or the letters where the Gospels stood and Revelation falls from the first five sources to the middle of the field: it wins two passages of Acts and six of all the letters. Whatever stands between Revelation and the Gospels at the scale of a stretch does not stand between Revelation and Acts or Paul. (The rare-sequence census of section 1 had found Revelation's nearest relatives among the letters to be James and the Petrines; at this scale none of them shows.)

The one letter passage worth a line: Col 3:24–4:10, first of the letters, with Rev 2:11–5:8. Col 4:2–3 has watching and "that God open to us a door"; Col 4:13 names Laodicea. Rev 3:2–3, 3:8, 3:20 have the watching and the door, and the last is to Laodicea.

2X.3. **The seven letters against the night of the supper.** The night in each Gospel, from the preparing of the meal to the cock: Matt 26:17–75, Mark 14:12–72, Luke 22:7–65, John 13:1–18:27. Against Rev 2:1–3:22.

*By rank.* Is Rev 2–3 the part of Revelation nearest the night? For Luke's night, yes: no other stretch of Revelation of that length scores as high, of thirty-one. For John's, nearly: one of thirty-one. For Matthew's and Mark's, no: thirteen and twelve of thirty-one. From the other side, the night is not the part of each Gospel nearest the letters: in Luke eleven other stretches of ninety-eight score as high, in Matthew seven of eighty-nine, in John nearly all. So the tie is Luke's and John's, and it is the letters that point at the night more than the night at the letters.

*Read, by Gospel.*

**All four: the denial.** Οὐκ ἠρνήσω, you did not deny: Rev 2:13 (my faith) and 3:8 (my name). The verb in the night: Peter's, in all four. Rev 3:8 has in one verse a door set open, "you kept my word", and "you did not deny my name"; a door with denying in one verse stands nowhere else in either Testament.

**John 18:16–18, 25: the door, the cold, the denial.** ὁ δὲ Πέτρος εἱστήκει πρὸς τῇ θύρᾳ ἔξω. To stand at the door: John 18:16 and Rev 3:20 (ἕστηκα ἐπὶ τὴν θύραν) in the New Testament, no verse of the Septuagint. Then 18:18, ψῦχος ἦν, καὶ ἐθερμαίνοντο, it was cold and they warmed themselves, Peter standing with them and warming himself; and at 18:25, still warming, he denied. Cold, of ψῦχος or ψυχρός: six verses of the New Testament, two of them Rev 3:15–16 and one John 18:18. So Laodicea's letter has, in six verses, neither cold nor hot, and "I stand at the door"; Philadelphia's has the door and "did not deny". John has, in ten verses, one who stands at the door outside, the cold, the warming, and the denial. The warmth is another root (θερμαίνω; ζεστός); the cold and the standing at the door are the same words. And the side is exchanged again: in Revelation the Lord stands at the door of the lukewarm; in John the disciple stands at the door, in the cold, and is let in to deny.

**John 13–17: the discourse.** Counted:

| Wording | John, the night | the letters | elsewhere |
|---|---|---|---|
| to keep my word | 14:23, 24; 15:20; 17:6 | 3:8, 10 | John 8:51, 52 only; no verse of the Septuagint |
| to keep out of (τηρέω ἐκ) | 17:15 ἵνα τηρήσῃς αὐτοὺς ἐκ τοῦ πονηροῦ | 3:10 κἀγώ σε τηρήσω ἐκ τῆς ὥρας τοῦ πειρασμοῦ | nowhere, either Testament |
| you have, will have, tribulation | 16:33 θλῖψιν ἔχετε | 2:10 ἕξετε θλῖψιν | nowhere, either Testament |
| I have overcome | 16:33 ἐγὼ νενίκηκα | 3:21 ὡς κἀγὼ ἐνίκησα; the overcomer seven times | the verb in the Gospels: here and Luke 11:22 |
| I have loved you | 13:34; 15:12 | 3:9 | three verses of the Septuagint |
| remember, as a command, with keeping | 15:20 | 3:3 | the command alone is common |

John 16:33 and Rev 2:10–11 each have the tribulation and the overcoming together, in one verse and in two. John 15:20 and Rev 3:3, 3:8, 3:10 each have remembering, the word kept, and keeping returned for keeping. This is the farewell discourse's own vocabulary and it is the letters'. That John's Gospel and Revelation share a dialect is the received view and explains some of it. It does not explain why the sharing lies in the night's chapters and the letters' chapters, which the rank above says it does for Revelation's side.

**Luke 22.** The saying at the supper (2W.4 b; 2X.1), and the swords (2P.4). With them the denial: at 22:31–34 the Satan, faith that does not fail, the command to strengthen, and the denial foretold stand in four verses; at Rev 2:13 the Satan's throne, "you did not deny my faith"; at 3:2 the command.

**Mark 14:51–52.** The young one clothed in linen over nakedness, who leaves it and flees naked: περιβεβλημένος σινδόνα ἐπὶ γυμνοῦ. Rev 3:18: white garments ἵνα περιβάλῃ, that the shame of your nakedness not show. Rev 16:15: blessed the one who watches and keeps his garments, that he not walk naked. Watching with garments or nakedness in one verse: Rev 16:15 alone, and in Mark the watching (14:34, 37, 38) is thirteen verses before the naked flight. By my pattern clothed with naked stands at Mark 14:51 and Rev 3:18; the augmented form adds Matt 25:36, 38, 43, so it is not theirs alone. Mark's alone among the Gospels, unexplained in the received reading, and here it is a lesser note.

**The garden, Matthew and Mark.** As at 2W.4 d: watching and the hour (Rev 3:3), the hour of temptation (3:10). At a hundred and twenty verses it is the first passage of the whole list.

2X.4. **What the night holds, set out.**

| The letters | The night |
|---|---|
| Smyrna 2:10–11: the devil, tested, you will have tribulation, be faithful; the overcomer | Luke 22:28, 31–32; John 16:33 |
| Pergamum 2:13: the Satan's throne; you did not deny my faith | Luke 22:31–34; the denial in all four |
| Thyatira 2:28: as I also received from my Father | Luke 22:29 |
| Sardis 3:2–3: watch; strengthen; remember; you will not know the hour | the garden; Luke 22:32; John 15:20 |
| Philadelphia 3:8–10: a door; you kept my word; did not deny my name; I will keep you out of the hour of temptation; I have loved you | John 18:16–17; 14:23; 17:6, 15; the garden; John 13:34 |
| Laodicea 3:15–21: cold; naked, clothed; I stand at the door; I will sup with him; sit on my throne, as I also overcame | John 18:16–18; Mark 14:51–52; Luke 22:30; John 16:33 |
| Ephesus 2:4–6: the first love left; remember; what I also hate | John 15:13, 18–25, by sense and thinly |

Six of the seven letters, each with wordings counted, on one night, and five of the six in Luke and John. The seven promises expand to nothing one by one (2U.4); as a set the letters lie under the supper, the garden and the denial. On the forward reading the words to the churches are staged as the words to the disciples on the last night, and what the churches are praised for or warned of is acted by them: one does not deny, and Peter does; one is told to watch, and they sleep; one is lukewarm with the Lord at the door, and Peter stands at the door in the cold. On the reverse reading a seer wrote seven letters out of the passion night of two Gospels, giving each church a piece. Each is possible. What is new is the object: not a verse and its borrower, but a section of Revelation and an evening.

2X.5. **Limits.** The night's reading is one reader's, after the machine's lists; the counted wordings are checkable and the table of 2X.4 is a reading. The rank test is two-sided and only one side comes out clearly. John's share is entangled with the dialect John and Revelation have in common, and a control is owed: the same wordings counted in 1 John, which has the dialect and no night. The Colossians line is one passage. The Lazarus passage, high at a hundred and twenty verses, was not read.

## 2Y. The dialect control; section against block

2Y.0. The author, 18:17: "lets do so". The two operations owed from 2X: the night's wordings counted in 1 John, and the reading of 2X.3 done for every section of Revelation against every block of every Gospel, by rank first. Scripts: `blocks/blocks.py`, `blocks/read.py`. Nothing is added to the engine here.

2Y.1. **The dialect control.** John and Revelation share a way of speaking, and 1–3 John has it with no night in it. Each wording of 2X.3, counted in five places:

| Wording | John's night | rest of John | 1–3 John | the letters | rest of Revelation | rest of the New Testament |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| to keep out of (τηρέω ἐκ) | 1 | 0 | 0 | 1 | 0 | 0 |
| to have tribulation | 1 | 0 | 0 | 1 | 0 | 0 |
| to stand at the door | 1 | 0 | 0 | 1 | 0 | 0 |
| I have loved you | 2 | 0 | 0 | 1 | 0 | 0 |
| remember, as a command | 2 | 0 | 0 | 2 | 0 | 10 |
| to keep my, his word | 4 | 3 | 1 | 2 | 0 | 0 |
| to overcome | 1 | 0 | 5 | 7 | 8 | 4 |
| to hate | 6 | 3 | 4 | 1 | 1 | 10 |
| κἀγώ with the Father | 5 | 6 | 0 | 2 | 0 | 4 |
| to abide (μένω) | 9 | 10 | 15 | 0 | 1 | 22 |

The control parts them. Overcoming, hating and keeping the word are the dialect: they are in 1 John or in the rest of the Gospel, and prove nothing about the night. Four are not: keeping out of, having tribulation, standing at the door, "I have loved you". Each stands in John's night and in the letters and nowhere else in John, in 1–3 John, or in the rest of Revelation; three of the four nowhere else in the New Testament. And the dialect's own mark, abiding, is in every Johannine place and not once in the letters. So the letters do not simply speak Johannine. They share four wordings with one evening of the Gospel. 2X.3's table for John stands at those four and is withdrawn to "the dialect" for the rest.

2Y.2. **Section against block, as a rate: nothing.** Revelation in ten sections by its own joints; each Gospel in blocks (birth, Baptist and testing, Galilee, the journey, the temple days, the discourse on the end, the night, cross and tomb; thirty-six blocks in all). For each of 360 pairs, two ranks: how many other Gospel stretches of the block's length score as high with the section, and how many other stretches of Revelation of the section's length score as high with the block. A pair stands if both are within one in ten.

| In Revelation's place | pairs standing at one in ten, both sides | at one in twenty |
|---|---|---|
| Revelation, ten sections | 10 | 4 |
| Acts, cut in ten | 10 | 2 |
| Isaiah, cut in ten | 8 | 5 |

At this grain Revelation's sections are tied to the Gospels' blocks no more than slices of Acts or Isaiah are. There is no plan of sections laid on blocks that a count can see. This is the fifth instrument to give an ordinary rate.

2Y.3. **Which pairs stood, and what they are when read.** Ten.

| Section | Block | other Gospel stretches as high | other stretches of Revelation as high | read as |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| the trumpets (8:2–11:19) | Matthew's cross and tomb | 0.06 | none | known: 2H, 2N.3 |
| the seals | Luke's discourse on the end | none | 0.08 | known in the main; three of Luke's own, below |
| the new Jerusalem | Luke's journey, second half | 0.006 | 0.03 | new; below |
| the new Jerusalem | Matthew's journey (19–20) | 0.02 | 0.03 | new; frames the labourers; below |
| the throne and the Lamb | John's tomb and lake | 0.07 | 0.03 | new; below |
| the throne and the Lamb | Luke's Baptist and testing | 0.08 | none | known: who is worthy to loose (2B) |
| the seven letters | Mark's discourse on the end | 0.003 | 0.09 | the watching, Sardis's (2W.4 d) |
| the seven letters | Mark's temple days | none | none | scatter: teaching, sitting, killing; nothing holds together |
| the throne and the Lamb | Matthew's night | 0.03 | 0.05 | scatter: three stems |
| the bowls | Mark's beginnings | 0.09 | 0.05 | scatter: three stems |

The letters with Luke's night do not stand by this rule: no other stretch of Revelation is as high, as at 2X.3, but eleven in a hundred other stretches of the Gospels are. The pair with the best ranks of all, the letters and Mark's temple days, reads as nothing. So the rank test is a pointer and a poor judge, like the pair instrument before it (2P).

2Y.4. **The three new, read in the seat.**

**a. The throne and the Lamb (Rev 4–5), and John's tomb (John 20).**

| John 20 | Revelation 4–5 |
|---|---|
| 20:11 Mary stood outside, weeping: ἔκλαιεν | 5:4 ἔκλαιον πολύ |
| 20:12 two angels ἐν λευκοῖς καθεζομένους, where the body ἔκειτο | 4:4 elders καθημένους … ἐν ἱματίοις λευκοῖς; 4:2 θρόνος ἔκειτο |
| 20:13, 15 τί κλαίεις; | 5:5 μὴ κλαῖε |
| 20:14 she sees Jesus ἑστῶτα | 5:6 ἀρνίον ἑστηκὸς ὡς ἐσφαγμένον |
| 20:19, 26 the doors shut; ἔστη εἰς τὸ μέσον | 4:1 a door opened; 5:6 ἐν μέσῳ … ἑστηκός |
| 20:20, 27 the hands and the side shown | 5:6 as slain |
| 20:28 ὁ κύριός μου καὶ ὁ θεός μου | 4:11 ὁ κύριος καὶ ὁ θεὸς ἡμῶν |
| 20:30 not written ἐν τῷ βιβλίῳ τούτῳ | 5:1 βιβλίον γεγραμμένον, which none could open |
| 21:15 τὰ ἀρνία μου | the Lamb, here first |

Counted. "Lord and God" with a possessive, said to one present: John 20:28 and Rev 4:11, and no verse of the Septuagint in that order. Sitting in white: John 20:12 and Rev 4:4 only. Weeping forbidden or questioned: John 20:13, 15, Rev 5:5, and three places of Luke. The imperfect "wept": John 20:11, Rev 5:4, Luke 8:52, Mark 14:72. To stand in the midst: John 20:19, 20:26, Luke 24:36, Rev 5:6. Ἀρνίον: John 21:15 and Revelation.

Read forward: the seer weeps because none is found to open the book, is told not to weep, and sees the slain one standing in the midst, to whom "our Lord and God" is said. In John the weeping is Mary's, the question is asked twice, the one standing is seen and not known, he stands in the midst showing the wounds, and Thomas says the acclamation in the singular. The seer's part is given to the persons of the story: 2S's first key, and it turns here in a Gospel it was not cut for. Luke 24:36 has the standing in the midst and none of the rest.

The received reading: the acclamation is the emperor's title turned (Domitian's dominus et deus, from memory) or the Psalm's (34:23, in the other order); the angels in white are the common tradition's. That accounts for two rows singly.

**b. The new Jerusalem (Rev 21–22), and Luke's journey to Jerusalem (13:22–19:27).** The block opens "journeying toward Jerusalem" (13:22).

| Luke | Revelation 21–22 |
|---|---|
| 13:24 εἰσελθεῖν διὰ τῆς στενῆς θύρας | 22:14 τοῖς πυλῶσιν εἰσέλθωσιν εἰς τὴν πόλιν |
| 13:25 ἔξω ἑστάναι | 22:15 ἔξω οἱ κύνες |
| 13:27 ἐργάται ἀδικίας; 16:10 ὁ ἄδικος … ἄδικός ἐστιν | 22:11 ὁ ἀδικῶν ἀδικησάτω ἔτι |
| 13:29 ἀπὸ ἀνατολῶν καὶ δυσμῶν καὶ ἀπὸ βορρᾶ καὶ νότου | 21:13 east, north, south, west, three gates each |
| 14:17 ἔρχεσθε, ὅτι ἤδη ἕτοιμά ἐστιν | 22:17 ἔρχου … ἐρχέσθω |
| 15:22 ταχὺ ἐξενέγκατε στολὴν τὴν πρώτην | 22:14 οἱ πλύνοντες τὰς στολάς; 22:12 ἔρχομαι ταχύ |
| 16:15 βδέλυγμα ἐνώπιον τοῦ θεοῦ | 21:27 ὁ ποιῶν βδέλυγμα |
| 16:19–21 purple and fine linen; at his πυλῶνα; οἱ κύνες | 21:12 πυλῶνας; 22:15 οἱ κύνες; 18:12, 16 purple and fine linen |
| 17:29 πῦρ καὶ θεῖον | 21:8 πυρὶ καὶ θείῳ |

Counted. The north: Luke 13:29 and Rev 21:13, and nowhere else in the New Testament; Matthew's form of the saying (8:11) has east and west only. To enter by a door or gates: Luke 13:24, Matt 7:13, Rev 22:14. Outside, of those shut out: Luke 13:25, Rev 22:15 (Luke 8:20 of the family). Dogs: eight verses of the New Testament. Πυλών: Luke 16:20, Matt 26:71, Acts, and Rev 21. Sulfur: Luke 17:29 and Revelation (Acts 17:29 is another word). Purple with fine linen: Luke 16:19, Rev 18:12, 18:16. The robe: Luke 15:22, three other Gospel places, Revelation five times.

So the second half of Luke's road to Jerusalem holds the entry to the city of Rev 21–22: who comes in from the four quarters, by what gate, in what robe, and who stands outside. Luke adds the north and the south to the double tradition's east and west, and the city has gates to all four. The one at whose gate the dogs are is clothed as Babylon is. The Septuagint has all these words many times (north 176, gate 53, robe 88); what it lacks by the patterns is entering by the gate and the outside. The pieces are spread over six chapters of Luke and two of Revelation, and none is a sentence shared. This is a conjunction at the largest scale yet and the loosest.

**c. Rev 21:5–22:13 and Matt 19:27–20:16.** The labourers (2V.4) were left with one verse against a complete received account. The block around them adds this:

| Matthew | Revelation |
|---|---|
| 19:28 ἐν τῇ παλινγενεσίᾳ | 21:5 ἰδοὺ καινὰ ποιῶ πάντα |
| 19:28 ὁ υἱὸς τοῦ ἀνθρώπου ἐπὶ θρόνου | 21:5 ὁ καθήμενος ἐπὶ τῷ θρόνῳ |
| 19:28 δώδεκα θρόνους … τὰς δώδεκα φυλὰς τοῦ Ἰσραήλ | 21:12 τῶν δώδεκα φυλῶν υἱῶν Ἰσραήλ; 21:14 the twelve apostles |
| 19:29 ζωὴν αἰώνιον κληρονομήσει | 21:7 ὁ νικῶν κληρονομήσει ταῦτα |
| 19:30; 20:16 first and last | 22:13 |
| 20:8 the wage paid | 22:12 |

The twelve tribes: Matt 19:28, Luke 22:30, Rev 21:12, Acts 26:7, Jas 1:1. The making new, as a noun or a deed: Matt 19:28, Rev 21:5, Titus 3:5. So Peter's question and its answer, the frame-saying, and the parable hold the members of Revelation's last two chapters: all things new, the throne, the twelve tribes and the twelve, the inheriting, the wage with the first and the last. (I first wrote that they run in Revelation's order. Not strictly: the inheriting and the tribes are exchanged and the first-and-last comes before the wage; corrected at 2Z.4.) The received account has Mark 10:28–31 for the frame (the inheriting, the first and last) and Matthew adding the thrones from the double tradition and the parable from his own stock. It has no source for the regeneration. The labourers stand better than 2V.4 left them: the verse is the last member of a run of six.

**d. The seals and Luke's discourse.** The synoptic discourse's tie to Rev 6 is old in the file. What the rank shows is that it is Luke's form that stands nearest, and three of Luke's own verses carry it. Luke 21:22 ἡμέραι ἐκδικήσεως, and Rev 6:10 ἐκδικεῖς. Luke 21:24 πεσοῦνται στόματι μαχαίρης, and Rev 6:4 μάχαιρα μεγάλη. Luke 21:36 σταθῆναι ἔμπροσθεν τοῦ υἱοῦ τοῦ ἀνθρώπου, and Rev 6:17 τίς δύναται σταθῆναι: that infinitive stands at those two and Mark 3:24 in the New Testament. With it Luke 18:3–8, the widow: avenge me; God will avenge the chosen who cry to him, and quickly. The fifth seal is the same cry.

2Y.5. **Where the sections lie, as the file now holds it.** A reading gathered from 2H to here; the right column is where each was found.

| Section of Revelation | In the Gospels | found by |
|---|---|---|
| the vision (1) | the mountain; the tomb's angel; the mourning tribes | reading; engine |
| the seven letters (2–3) | the night: the supper saying, the garden, the door and the denial, the discourse | conj; reading |
| the throne and the Lamb (4–5) | the Baptist's "worthy to loose"; John's tomb | engine; rank |
| the seals (6–7) | the discourse on the end, Luke's most | census; rank |
| the trumpets (8–11) | the seventy's return; the oaths; Matthew's cross and tomb | conj; engine; machine |
| the woman, the dragon (12–14) | Matthew's birth (2L); the sower's harvest | reading |
| the bowls (15–16) | the sower's sun | conj, lesser |
| Babylon (17:1–19:10) | Matthew's temple days: the wedding, the woes; Luke 14 | reading; conj |
| the rider, the thousand years (19:11–20) | the guard at the tomb; Luke 13:16 | machine; pairs |
| the new Jerusalem (21–22) | John 4; the journey: Luke 13–19, Matt 19–20 | expand; rank |

Every section is somewhere and no order is kept, which is 2N.2. Two regularities can be said and neither is counted: the letters to the churches fall on the night of the disciples, and the city at the end falls on the road to the city. Whether a table like this could be made as well from Isaiah's ten parts is the question 2Y.2 answers at the level of rank (yes, as many pairs), and has not answered at the level of the seat, where the pairings the Septuagint lacks are what distinguish these.

2Y.6. **Limits.** The sections and blocks were cut by me, by the usual joints; other cuts would give other pairs. Three of ten standing pairs are scatter and the best-ranked is among them. The readings at 2Y.4 are one reader's; (b) is loose. Isaiah's standing pairs were counted and not read, and they should be, by the same reader, before 2Y.5 is leaned on.

## 2Z. The pairing the rest lacks, counted for every source alike; four more in the engine

2Z.0. The author, 18:32: "lets continue". The two operations owed from 2Y. The first was to read Isaiah's and Acts's pairs by the same reader as a control on 2Y.4. A reader's impression of three books is a poor control, so the thing the reader had been using was made a count instead and run the same way on thirteen sources. Scripts: `blocks/excl.py`; `engine/derivations.py` (four added).

2Z.1. **The measure.** What has distinguished every locus that held in the seat since 2V is one thing: two rare words together in a verse of Revelation and together in a verse of a Gospel, and together nowhere else. The gift with the water. The scorpion with authority. The cup with the full. Stated for a machine: two stems, each in at most 120 verses of the New Testament, together in one verse of the source and one verse of a Gospel, and in no verse of the New Testament outside the four Gospels and no verse of the Septuagint, the source's own section apart. A link is a source verse and a Gospel verse sharing at least one such pairing. No member, passage or pair is chosen.

2Z.2. **Result.**

| Source | verses | links per hundred verses | its verses that have one, in a hundred | most in one section-and-block |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Revelation** | 405 | **60.0** | **27** | 6 |
| Acts | 1,000 | 53.4 | 25 | 18 |
| Isaiah | 1,270 | 52.0 | 19 | 35 |
| Judith, Tobit | 580 | 47.2 | 22 | 7 |
| Jeremiah | 1,290 | 39.5 | 15 | 14 |
| Daniel (Theodotion) | 510 | 36.1 | 16 | 6 |
| Ezekiel | 1,260 | 35.3 | 13 | 9 |
| 1 Maccabees | 920 | 33.3 | 15 | 7 |
| Zechariah, Joel, Malachi | 330 | 31.5 | 15 | 14 |
| Genesis | 1,520 | 27.3 | 15 | 7 |
| Hebrews, James, 1 Peter | 510 | 23.7 | 16 | 4 |
| Romans, 1 Corinthians | 860 | 22.2 | 13 | 22 |
| Psalms | 2,510 | 12.9 | 7 | 6 |

Revelation is first of thirteen, by both columns. This is the first rate in the file at which it is not ordinary. It is to be read with three things beside it.

a. *The instrument sees what is known.* Its largest cells are quotations and shared lists: Isaiah 61:1 at Luke 4:18 (35 pairings), Isaiah 40:3–5 at Luke 3, Isaiah 29:13 at Mark 7, Isaiah 7:14 at Matt 1:23, the vineyard of Isaiah 5 at the tenants; the list of the Twelve at Acts 1:13; the words over the bread and cup at 1 Cor 11 and Luke 22 (22 pairings). So it measures literary dependence where dependence is not in doubt.

b. *Revelation has the rate without the lump.* Isaiah reaches its rate by quotation, Acts by the list and by Luke's hand (wrong at the level of links: the cells of 35 and 18 count pairings inside a few links; with those links removed Isaiah falls only from 52 to 50 and Acts from 53 to 52. Corrected at 2AB.1 b). Revelation's fullest cell holds six. Its links are spread: 110 of its 405 verses have one, a larger share than any other source's. It stands to the Gospels at the density of the prophet they cite most and the book one of them continues, and it is nowhere quoted and nowhere continued.

c. *Kind and period carry a book most of the way.* Judith and Tobit, which no one takes for a source of the Gospels, reach 47: narrative Greek of about the same age shares pairings with narrative. Paul's argument does not (22). So the excess to be explained is Revelation's 60 over a story-book's 47 and over Acts's 53, and not 60 over nothing. And one bias runs in favour of every New Testament source: a word the Septuagint lacks cannot fail the Septuagint's side of the test. Within the New Testament the comparison is clean: Revelation 60, Acts 53, the letters 22 to 24.

So 2Y.2's question is answered at the level of the seat. By rank of whole sections, Revelation, Acts and Isaiah were alike. By the pairing the rest lacks they are also alike, with Revelation a little ahead, and what differs is how each gets there.

2Z.3. **What the fullest cells are.** Revelation's own, by machine:

| Section and block | pairings | read |
|---|---|---|
| Babylon with Matthew's temple days | 6 | the wedding made ready; the called and chosen; the full cup; full of uncleanness: 2S.4, 2W.4 c, found again with no reader |
| Babylon with Luke's journey, second half | 6 | the called to the wedding and supper (2U.3); and torment with "from afar", below |
| the letters with John 7–10 | 5 | the blind who say; below |
| the trumpets with Luke's journey, first half | 4 | the scorpion with authority, with hurting; hurting with authority: 2W.4 a, three ways |
| the letters with Luke's testing | 3 | the devil, the tempting, "until": Smyrna (2U.4 c) |

Two not in the file.

*The rich one and Babylon: Luke 16:19–25, Rev 18:7–16.* Torment with "from afar" in one verse: Luke 16:23 (ἐν βασάνοις, ὁρᾷ Ἀβραὰμ ἀπὸ μακρόθεν), Rev 18:10 and 18:15 (ἀπὸ μακρόθεν ἑστηκότες διὰ τὸν φόβον τοῦ βασανισμοῦ αὐτῆς), and no third place in the New Testament (one verse of the Septuagint by the pattern, not read). With 2Y.4 b: purple and fine linen at Luke 16:19 and Rev 18:12, 16. And Rev 18:7, as much as she lived in luxury, so much give her torment; Luke 16:25, you received your good things in your life, and now you are in pain. In Revelation the merchants stand far off and see her torment. In Luke the one in torment sees from far off. The clothing, the luxury repaid, the torment and the distance, with the one who looks exchanged.

*Laodicea and the one born blind: Rev 3:17–18, John 9.* λέγεις ὅτι πλούσιός εἰμι … καὶ οὐκ οἶδας ὅτι σὺ εἶ … τυφλός; and John 9:41, εἰ τυφλοὶ ἦτε … νῦν δὲ λέγετε ὅτι βλέπομεν. Blindness with "you say that": John 9:41, 9:19 and Rev 3:17, no verse of the Septuagint. Then the remedy: Rev 3:18 κολλούριον ἐγχρῖσαι τοὺς ὀφθαλμούς σου ἵνα βλέπῃς; John 9:11 ἐπέχρισέν μου τοὺς ὀφθαλμούς … ἀνέβλεψα. To anoint the eyes with a compound of χρίω: John 9:11, Rev 3:18, and Tobit 11:8, where it is the cure of Tobit's blindness. So the Septuagint has the anointing of blind eyes and not the saying; John and Revelation have both. The sixth letter laid on the sixth sign.

And one small: "cast into the sea", of a mountain. Matt 21:21 and Mark 11:23 (ἄρθητι καὶ βλήθητι εἰς τὴν θάλασσαν) and Rev 8:8 (ὄρος μέγα … ἐβλήθη εἰς τὴν θάλασσαν), and nowhere else in either Testament by the pattern.

2Z.4. **Four more in the engine.** Declared before the run; all four passed as first written. One location was mistyped (a range across two chapters, which the engine does not read) and was corrected before anything ran.

| | seed | target | kept | best control, by kept members |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| the weeping, the one standing in the midst, Lord and God | Rev 4:1–2, 4, 11; 5:4–6 | John 20:11–30 | 11 of 11; 7 dropped absent | Luke 24:36–43, Mark's tomb, John 11: 2 of 11 each |
| at the door, in the cold: the denial | Rev 3:8, 15–16, 20 | John 18:15–27 | 5 of 5; "did not deny" undone; door, stand, deny each said again | Mark's denial: 3 of 5, none said again |
| you will have tribulation; the overcoming | Rev 2:10–11 | John 16:33 | 3 of 3, in the emitted order | 1 John 5:4–5: 1 of 3 |
| all things new, the twelve tribes, the inheriting, the wage, first and last | Rev 21:5, 7, 12, 14; 22:12–13 | Matt 19:27–30 with 20:1–16 | 10 of 10; first, last and twelve said again | Luke 22:28–30: 4 of 10; Mark's form: 2 of 10 |

The bench runs sixty-eight; sixty-seven pass with no control passing.

A correction to 2Y.4 c, made there. I wrote that Matthew's run keeps Revelation's order. It does not strictly: the inheriting and the tribes are exchanged, and the first-and-last comes before the wage. Two neighbours swapped in each half. The derivation is declared unordered.

Mark's denial holds three of the five: the denying, the warming, the standing. It has no door and no cold. So the fire in the courtyard is the common tradition's, and John adds the two things that are Laodicea's and Philadelphia's.

2Z.5. **Where this leaves the file.** Five instruments gave Revelation an ordinary rate, and each was counting something a relative has: shared stems, shared stretches, wins. The sixth counts the thing the readings kept turning on, and by it Revelation stands first, beside the two books whose tie to the Gospels no one disputes, and unlike them has no quotation to account for it. That is a statement about kinship and its texture. It is not a statement about direction: a late book steeped in four Gospels would share their pairings too, though it would be the only such book to do so without once citing them, and 2E.8 asks why a book of that kind was then pushed to the edge.

Limits. Crude stems. The 120-verse ceiling and the one-verse window are single settings. Judith and Tobit show how much kind alone gives. The Septuagint's lines carry apparatus in places, which can only add to its side. The two new readings of 2Z.3 are mine, from the machine's cells.

## 2AA. The count at other settings; by Gospel; Revelation's links read whole

2AA.0. The author, 19:00: "yes - making progress. lets proceed". The operations owed from 2Z. Script: `blocks/excl2.py`; the list is `blocks/links.tsv`, 244 lines.

2AA.1. **Does first place hold? Nearly.** The count of 2Z.2 at six settings: the ceiling on how common a stem may be (60, 120, 250 verses), the span of a pairing (one verse or two), the letters of the stem kept (4, 5, 6). Links per hundred source verses:

| Source | 120, one verse, 5 (2Z) | ceiling 60 | ceiling 250 | two verses | stem of 4 | stem of 6 |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Revelation | 60 | 20 | 85 | 375 | 54 | 56 |
| Acts | 53 | 20 | 119 | 377 | 38 | 53 |
| Isaiah | 52 | 28 | 75 | 316 | 51 | 49 |
| Judith, Tobit | 47 | 16 | 67 | 278 | 33 | 41 |
| Jeremiah | 40 | 21 | 68 | 208 | 34 | 40 |
| Daniel (Theodotion) | 36 | 15 | 46 | 190 | 30 | 35 |
| Hebrews, James, 1 Peter | 24 | 9 | 39 | 168 | 19 | 20 |
| Romans, 1 Corinthians | 22 | 7 | 37 | 151 | 21 | 20 |
| Revelation's place of eight | 1 | 3 | 2 | 2 | 1 | 1 |

2Z's "first" is corrected to this: first at three settings of six and never below third. With the rarest words only, Isaiah leads, as a quoted book should. With common words let in, Acts leads, as Luke's own second book should. Revelation is beside those two at every setting and the letters are at two-fifths of it at every setting. The order of the three moves; the group does not.

2AA.2. **By Gospel: relatively even** (the word corrected at a reviewer's instance, 2AB.2; Luke is a fifth below the others). Revelation's 244 links fall on Matthew 75, John 59, Luke 64, Mark 46. Per hundred verses of each Gospel: 7.0, 6.7, 5.6, 6.8. No Gospel has more than its length gives. John is not ahead, so the dialect of 2Y.1 is not what the count is seeing. By where the pairing stands among the Gospels: John alone 45, Luke alone 44, with Mark 43, Matthew alone 35, Matthew and Luke 27, John with a synoptic 27, Mark alone 23. Every layer of the tradition has its share. This is 2J's result again from another side: nothing here belongs to one Gospel.

2AA.3. **The list, read whole.** 244 links, in Revelation's order. Twenty-four fall inside a derivation already in the engine, verse for verse. What the rest are:

*About half are idiom.* A pairing of two very common verbs that happens to stand nowhere else: coming with an eye, having with one, answering with "whence", going with "where". They are in every source's count alike and are why the rate is a rate and not a list of borrowings. At the ceiling of 60 the list is 82, and that is nearer the number worth reading.

*Known to the file by reading, now found with no reader:* the denial with the knowing (Rev 2:13 and 3:8 with all four denials); watching with the hour; the scorpion; the crucified with "where"; the one seated on a cloud (Rev 14:14–16 with Mark 14:62); the wedding and the called, eight links; the supper and the called, three; the cup and the full; torment and afar; Smyrna and the testing; the book opened; the winepress of the vineyard (ληνός: Matt 21:33 and Rev 14:19–20, 19:15 only); unclean with demon in Luke's phrase.

*Not in the file, and held in the seat.* Each counted.

a. **Matt 24:28**, ὅπου ἐὰν ᾖ τὸ πτῶμα, ἐκεῖ συναχθήσονται οἱ ἀετοί. The machine links it twice: the corpse with "where" at Rev 11:8, and the eagle with "where" at Rev 12:14. Πτῶμα, a corpse: Matt 14:12, 24:28, Mark 6:29, 15:45, Rev 11:8, 11:9. The eagle: Matt 24:28, Luke 17:37, Rev 8:13, 12:14. The gathering is Rev 19:17, the birds called συνάχθητε to the great supper. One saying of nine words; three places of Revelation. Luke's form has σῶμα.

b. **Luke 12:35–48.** Blessed, with watching: Luke 12:37 and Rev 16:15 only, and no verse of the Septuagint. The thief follows in both (12:39; 16:15 ἔρχομαι ὡς κλέπτης). Then 12:46, τὸ μέρος αὐτοῦ μετὰ τῶν ἀπίστων θήσει, and Rev 21:8, τοῖς … ἀπίστοις … τὸ μέρος αὐτῶν ἐν τῇ λίμνῃ. A portion with the unfaithful: those two only, and no verse of the Septuagint. Matthew's form of the same sentence (24:51) has "with the hypocrites". So where Luke differs from Matthew in this parable, by one word, the word is Revelation's.

c. **"Follow wherever."** Rev 14:4, οἱ ἀκολουθοῦντες τῷ ἀρνίῳ ὅπου ἂν ὑπάγῃ. Following with "wherever": Matt 8:19 and Luke 9:57 (ἀκολουθήσω σοι ὅπου ἐὰν ἀπέρχῃ), John 12:26, John 13:36, Rev 14:4, and no verse of the Septuagint. John 13:36 has all three stems: ὅπου ὑπάγω οὐ δύνασαί μοι νῦν ἀκολουθῆσαι. Twelve links from one verse of Revelation, and one more on the night of 2X.

d. **The Baptist seized and bound.** Mark 6:17, ἐκράτησεν τὸν Ἰωάνην καὶ ἔδησεν αὐτὸν ἐν φυλακῇ. Rev 20:2, 7: ἐκράτησεν τὸν δράκοντα … καὶ ἔδησεν αὐτόν … ἐκ τῆς φυλακῆς αὐτοῦ. Seized and bound, the two verbs together: Mark 6:17, Matt 14:3, Rev 20:2, no verse of the Septuagint. Four verses on, Mark 6:21 has the magnates and chiliarchs of Rev 6:15 (in the engine). The dragon's treatment given to the Baptist by a king: 2S's second key, in Mark.

e. **The young one in Mark.** Στολὴ λευκή, a white robe: Mark 16:5, Rev 6:11, 7:9 (7:13), and no verse of the Septuagint. Mark 16:5 καθήμενον … περιβεβλημένον στολὴν λευκήν; Rev 4:4 καθημένους περιβεβλημένους ἐν ἱματίοις λευκοῖς. With 2X.3: the same word περιβεβλημένος at Mark 14:51, over nakedness. Naked on the night and in a white robe at the tomb, in the words of Rev 3:17–18 and 6:11.

f. **"Within they are full."** Ἔσωθεν γέμουσιν: Rev 4:8, Matt 23:25, 23:27, Luke 11:39, and no verse of the Septuagint. So the woes (2W.4 c) have a second place in Revelation: the cup that is full from 17:4, and the phrase itself from the living creatures, full of eyes within.

g. **Let the thirsty come.** Rev 22:17 ὁ διψῶν ἐρχέσθω; John 7:37 ἐάν τις διψᾷ ἐρχέσθω πρός με. Ἐρχέσθω: John 7:37, Luke 21:21, Rev 22:17. John 7:37 was a control at 2U.1 and is itself a taker of the verse John 4 expands.

h. **Smaller, each one pairing.** To go up with to be finished: Luke 18:31, Luke's form of the passion prediction, and Rev 11:7 where the witnesses finish and the beast comes up. An image that one has: Luke 20:24, the coin, and Rev 13:14; εἰκών stands in the Gospels only at the coin, and Rev 13:17 has the buying and selling. Manna with "written": John 6:31 and Rev 2:17. To buy with a garment: Luke 22:36 and Rev 3:18, Laodicea on the night again. Bound and loosed: Matt 16:19 and Rev 9:14; by reading the keys stand beside it (Matt 16:19; Rev 1:18, 3:7) with Hades a verse away in each (Matt 16:18; Rev 1:18).

2AA.4. **What reading the list whole shows.** Three things.

1. (This is rediscovery and not independent confirmation, as a reviewer says at 2AB.2: the count was built from what the readings had turned on.) The machine's inventory and the reader's coincide where they overlap. Nearly every locus the file reached by reading since 2S is on the list, found by a rule that knows nothing of them. That is the check on the reader that 2S.7 and 2X.5 said was owed.
2. The new places are of the kinds already named. A saying that joins two or three places of Revelation (a). One word by which Luke differs from Matthew (b). The adversary's treatment transferred (d). No new kind of thing turned up in 244 lines.
3. Half the list is idiom, so the rate of 2Z.2 measures likeness of language as well as contact, for every source. What distinguishes Revelation from Judith and Tobit is not the rate alone, 60 to 47. It is that so much of Revelation's list reads as sayings and scenes. The same reading has not been done on Judith's list, and until it has, that sentence is an impression.

2AA.5. **Limits.** The reading of 244 lines was one pass by one reader. "About half" is a reader's estimate, and the mechanical figure beside it (82 of 244 at the stricter ceiling) is not the same cut. The control that would make 2AA.4.3 a result, Judith's and Tobit's 274 links read the same way, is owed first.

## 2AB. A review of v0.28, verified; the links classed by rules fixed beforehand; positions made into persons

2AB.0. The author, 19:05, "lets keep reading through", and at 19:10 a review of v0.28. The review's claims were checked against the file and the seat before anything was changed, and its largest proposal, that Revelation's kinship is of a distributed kind where Isaiah's and Acts's are concentrated, was put to the count it asks for. It does not hold as stated. Scripts: `blocks/cuts.py`; `engine/derivations.py` (five added).

2AB.1. **The links classed, by rules written before any list was read.** For each link, n is the number of distinct rare stems in its exclusive pairings. *Quotation-like:* n of four or more. *Idiom:* no pairing in which both stems are in at most 60 verses of the New Testament. *Specific:* neither. *Close:* some pairing whose two words stand within three words of each other in both verses, which is a shared phrase and not two words that happen to share a verse.

| Source | links per hundred | quotation-like | idiom, share of links | specific per hundred | close per hundred | specific and close per hundred | per hundred with quotation-like verses removed | held by the top twentieth of verses |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Revelation | 60.2 | 0 | 66% | 20.2 | 8.4 | **4.7** | 60.2 | 18% |
| Acts | 53.4 | 4 | 63% | 19.4 | 11.3 | **5.7** | 52.1 | 21% |
| Isaiah | 52.2 | 16 | 47% | 26.5 | 3.6 | 2.1 | 49.9 | 22% |
| Judith, Tobit | 47.4 | 0 | 66% | 16.2 | 3.1 | 1.4 | 47.4 | 16% |
| Jeremiah | 39.8 | 3 | 49% | 20.2 | 1.5 | 0.9 | 37.9 | 19% |
| Daniel (Theodotion) | 36.1 | 0 | 58% | 15.3 | 1.0 | 0.6 | 36.1 | 18% |
| Hebrews, James, 1 Peter | 23.7 | 0 | 60% | 9.4 | 2.0 | 0.4 | 23.7 | 11% |
| Romans, 1 Corinthians | 22.3 | 3 | 69% | 6.6 | 4.2 | 2.0 | 21.4 | 15% |

Three results, two of them against what the file and the review had said.

a. *The cut of idiom does not part Revelation from a story-book.* Two-thirds of Revelation's links are idiom, and two-thirds of Judith's and Tobit's. I had said "about half"; the rule says 66 in a hundred, the same for both. With idiom removed the two stand 20 to 16, the ratio they had before. 2AA.4.3's impression is not borne out by this cut.

b. *The contrast of spread and lump dissolves.* The review proposes that Revelation's kinship is distributed where Isaiah's and Acts's is concentrated in quotation and list, and that removing the concentrations would leave Revelation standing and bring the others down. Removed by one rule: Isaiah goes from 52.2 to 49.9, Acts from 53.4 to 52.1. They do not come down. And the share of links held by the busiest twentieth of verses is about the same for all (18, 21, 22, 16). 2Z.2 b said Isaiah "reaches its rate by quotation". That was wrong at the level of links: the cells of 35 and 18 were counts of pairings inside a few links, and a line of four shared words makes six pairings. The review's third correction, that pairings and links must not be interchanged, is right and this is where it bites. What is true: Revelation has no link with more than three shared rare stems, where Acts has six and Isaiah nine. It has no quotation. That is a fact about its largest link and not about how its links are spread.

c. *The shared phrase does part them.* Two rare words side by side in both verses, paired nowhere else, and not idiom: Acts 5.7 in a hundred verses, Revelation 4.7, then Isaiah 2.1, Romans with 1 Corinthians 2.0, Judith and Tobit 1.4, Jeremiah 0.9. Here Revelation stands with Luke's second book and at more than three times the story-book. The counts are small and are printed: Revelation 19 links, which are 11 phrases in 13 Gospel verses (the book opened at Luke 4:17 is counted five times and rests on a variant, 2Q.3); Judith and Tobit 8 links, 7 phrases.

The two lists, read. Judith's and Tobit's seven phrases: "began to cry out"; "cried out from fear"; "take courage, child"; "ran on ahead"; "the coast of Tyre"; "scourges"; and "those doing alms" (Tob 12:9 with Matt 6:3), the one that is a known debt. Four are stock of story-telling, two are a word, one is a source. Revelation's eleven: called and chosen; called to the wedding; Satan bound; let the thirsty come; within they are full; a white robe; clothed in bright; the book opened; about to suffer; about to go up; a city which is called. Eight belong to a saying or a scene and three are a word; none is stock. By the review's classes, read by me and not blind to which list is which: relational or compositional, Revelation 8 of 11, Judith and Tobit 1 of 7. So the difference between Revelation and a story-book is real, it is at the phrase, and it rests on nineteen links.

2AB.2. **The review's other points, checked.**

| Point | Checked | Done |
|---|---|---|
| the opening table gives 61, 64 and 68 in turn | right | one row, the latest first: seventy-three, with the earlier runs named |
| finding the known loci again is rediscovery, not independent confirmation | right. The count of 2Z was built from what the readings had turned on | said at 2AA.4 |
| "even" across the Gospels should be "relatively even" | right: 7.0, 6.8, 6.7 and Luke 5.6 | said at 2AA.2 |
| the Muratorian fragment has Paul follow John's seven churches | fetched, not seated: "cum ipse beatus apostolus paulus sequens prodecessuris sui iohannis ordine non nisi nomenati semptae ecclesiis scribat" (the manuscript's spelling, from a transcription online; it was seated all along, `muratorian-fragment-tregelles`, and I had not looked: 2AC) | 2E.8 corrected: one ancient reader does describe a formative function, and with John as Paul's predecessor |
| a judgment on a city becomes a judgment on one person (Luke 16) | a reading; the counted members are in the engine below | 2AB.4 |
| three hypotheses should be kept apart: a common source; four grammars; a fourfold architecture | right. 2Z bears on the first only. The second was tested and not found (2F.1; 2J). The third has not been tested | the table says so |

2AB.3. **Five more in the engine.** Declared before the run. One control named a place the seat does not hold (Proverbs 31, numbered otherwise in Swete) and was removed before anything ran. All five passed.

| | seed | target | kept | best control, by kept members |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| where the corpse is, the eagles gathered | Rev 11:8; 12:14; 19:17 | Matt 24:28 | 5 of 5, in the emitted order | Luke's form: 4 of 5, lacking the corpse |
| blessed the watching; the thief; a portion with the unfaithful | Rev 16:15; 21:8 | Luke 12:37–46 | 6 of 6; blessed said three times | Matthew's form: 5 of 6, lacking the unfaithful |
| seized, bound, in prison | Rev 20:1–3, 7 | Mark 6:17 | 3 of 3, in order | Luke 3:19–20, Acts 12: 1 of 3 |
| purple and fine linen; torment from afar | Rev 18:7, 10, 12, 16 | Luke 16:19–25 | 5 of 5 | Esther 8:15: 2 of 5 |
| you say, and are blind; the eyes anointed | Rev 3:17–18 | John 9 | 7 of 7; blind, eyes, see, "do not know" each said again | Mark 8:22–26: 3 of 7; Tobit 11: 2 of 7 |

The bench runs seventy-three; seventy-two pass with no control passing. Two of the five pass by one member over a control that is the other Gospel's form of the same saying: the corpse against Luke's body, the unfaithful against Matthew's hypocrites. In each the engine is measuring one word, and the derivation says no more than that the word by which the two Gospels differ is Revelation's. For Matt 24:28 that word, πτῶμα, is also the word of 2Q.3.

2AB.4. **Positions made into persons.** The review names what several of the recent loci have in common, and the names are adopted. None is in the engine, which checks words; each is stated with the places it is read at.

*ROLE-FISSION: one addressee becomes several persons.* Laodicea is told: you say you are rich, you do not know you are blind, anoint your eyes to see. In John 9 the blindness and the anointing are one person's and the saying is others': the Pharisees "say, We see" (9:41) and say "we know" three times (9:24, 29, 31), and the one cured says "I do not know; one thing I know" (9:25). The knowing and not knowing of 3:17 is dealt to both sides. And the letter goes on in order: 3:20 has the door, the voice heard and the opening, and John 10:1–3 has the door, the doorkeeper opening and the voice heard, straight after 9:41. A door with a voice and an opening in one verse: John 10:3 and Rev 3:20, and no third place in either Testament. So Rev 3:17–20 lies under John 9:1–10:3 in sequence. Not from the letter: the birth, the parents, Siloam, the Sabbath, the casting out, the worship. Not in John 9: rich, poor, naked, gold, white garments (all dropped in the engine and all absent). Also at John 20, where the seer's weeping is Mary's and the acclamation Thomas's (2Y.4 a).

*ROLE-FUSION: several positions become one person.* In Rev 18 the city is clothed in purple and fine linen and is tormented, and the merchants look on from afar. In Luke 16 one person is so clothed, is tormented, and looks from afar. The city and its onlookers are one rich person. A judgment on an economy is told as a judgment on someone in it.

*TRIAL: a command or a commendation becomes a scene in which someone keeps it or fails it.* The letters' imperatives, set against the night of 2X.

| The letters | The night | what is done |
|---|---|---|
| 2:10 into prison … be faithful unto death | Luke 22:33 ready to go with you εἰς φυλακὴν καὶ εἰς θάνατον | pledged, and failed within the hour |
| 2:13; 3:8 you did not deny | the denial, all four | failed |
| 3:2–3 be watching; if you do not watch | the garden | failed: they sleep |
| 3:2 strengthen | Luke 22:32 | commanded again, for after the turning |
| 2:5; 3:3 remember, and repent | Matt 26:75 ἐμνήσθη ὁ Πέτρος; Mark 14:72; Luke 22:61; he wept | kept, after the failing |
| 2:25; 3:11 κράτει, hold fast | Matt 26:48, Mark 14:44 κρατήσατε αὐτόν | the same command in the betrayer's mouth |
| 2:4 the first love left | John 21:15 do you love me more than these | asked again, after |

Counted: prison with death in one verse stands at Luke 22:33, Rev 2:10, Acts 22:4 and 2 Cor 11:23. The rest of the table is reading, over words that are common singly. Read so, the night is the letters examined: every command given to a church is put to the twelve, and Peter fails each one that can be failed and then does the one that comes after failing, which is to remember.

The review's sentence for all three: Revelation supplied positions that the Gospels made into people. As a description of the forward reading it fits the loci of 2X to 2AA better than any operator the engine has. As evidence of direction it is what the review says it is, two techniques that both exist: a proposition dealt among characters, or characters gathered into a proposition. A test is owed that can tell them apart, and the one in reach is residue: when one address is dealt among persons, the persons should have matter the address lacks (they do: the parents, Siloam); when persons are gathered into an address, the address should have matter no person has (it does: rich, naked, gold). Both hold at John 9. It does not decide.

2AB.5. **Where this leaves the count.** Stated at its present strength, with the two corrections in. By links, Revelation stands with Acts and Isaiah at every setting and above a story-book by about a quarter. Idiom is two-thirds of every such list and does not part them. Spread does not part them. The shared rare phrase does: there Revelation stands with Acts alone, at more than three times the story-book and twice Isaiah, on nineteen links. That last is the result to try to break next, since it is the one that distinguishes and the one resting on least.

## 2AC. The seats checked: an error of mine, and the reception read from the seats

2AC.0. The author, 19:29: "how is the muratorian fragment not yet seated? are you positive?" I was not, and it is. I had written at 2E.8 that the reception was "from memory and none of it seated", and at 2AB.2 that the fragment was "fetched, not seated", without looking. Looked now, on main as of 19:04.

2AC.1. **What is seated.** The Muratorian fragment: `muratorian-fragment-tregelles`, seat EA-CORPORA-34/04, Tregelles's edition of 1867, fetched 5 November. And beside it, for this file's purposes:

| Seat | holds |
|---|---|
| `eusebius-historia-ecclesiastica` | the History, books I–X, Greek (Dindorf) |
| `justin-dialogus` | the Dialogue with Trypho, Greek |
| `irenaeus-adversus-haereses` | the Greek remains and Harvey's two volumes |
| `epiphanius-panarion` | the Panarion |
| `westcott-canon-1896` | Westcott's General Survey of the canon, with the lists |
| `second-temple-pseudepigrapha` | 4 Ezra (Violet 1910), the Sibyllines, the Testaments of the Twelve, Aristeas, the Hebrew Ben Sira, Tischendorf's Apocalypses apocryphae, James's Anecdota, Tichonravov's Slavonic, Patrologia Syriaca I.2 |
| `ethiopic-canon` | 106 texts, Jubilees and the Ascension of Isaiah among them |
| `dss`, `josephus`, `philo`, `hebrew-wlc` | the Scrolls, Josephus, Philo, the Hebrew Bible |
| `lxx-swete` | with the deuterocanonical books in it: Tobit, Judith, Wisdom, Sirach, Baruch, the Letter of Jeremiah, 1–4 Maccabees, the Psalms of Solomon, the Odes, Susanna, Bel |
| `revelation-criticism-1886-1920` | Vischer, Spitta, Gunkel, Bousset, Charles |

So two more of my statements were wrong. 4 Ezra is seated, and the control "from another apocalypse" that 2S.7 and the lists of owed work kept deferring "until 4 Ezra is seated" could have been run. And the deuterocanonical books have been inside every Septuagint count in this file from the start: Judith and Tobit at 2Z were not an addition.

2AC.2. **The reception, from the seats.** 2E.8's third step, rewritten there from these.

| Witness | Seat | What it says |
|---|---|---|
| Justin | Eusebius, HE 4.18.8 | μέμνηται δὲ καὶ τῆς Ἰωάννου ἀποκαλύψεως, σαφῶς τοῦ ἀποστόλου αὐτὴν εἶναι λέγων |
| Melito | HE 4.26.2 | among his books, τὰ περὶ τοῦ διαβόλου καὶ τῆς ἀποκαλύψεως Ἰωάννου |
| Irenaeus | HE 5.8.6 | seen οὐδὲ πρὸ πολλοῦ χρόνου … πρὸς τῷ τέλει τῆς Δομετιανοῦ ἀρχῆς |
| the Muratorian list | Tregelles | apostolus paulus sequens prodecessoris sui Iohannis ordine nonnisi nominatim semptae ecclesiis scribat; and: Iohannis enim in apocalebsy licet septe eccleseis scribat tamen omnibus dicit |
| Origen | HE 6.25.9 | ἔγραψε δὲ καὶ τὴν Ἀποκάλυψιν |
| Papias | HE 3.39.12 | a thousand years after the resurrection, bodily, on this earth; Eusebius: he took what was said in figures and did not see it |
| Dionysius of Alexandria | HE 7.25.1 | τινὲς μὲν οὖν τῶν πρὸ ἡμῶν ἠθέτησαν καὶ ἀνεσκεύασαν πάντη τὸ βιβλίον, chapter by chapter, calling it unknowable and its title false |
| Eusebius himself | HE 3.24.18; 3.25.2; 3.25.4 | opinion on it "is still pulled either way among the many"; placed after the acknowledged books, εἴ γε φανείη; and again among the spurious, εἰ φανείη, "which some reject and others reckon with the acknowledged" |
| Westcott | General Survey | "The position of the Apocalypse is anomalous": but for its omission in the Peshito it would be an acknowledged book |

In one seated book, then, the movement of 2E.8 can be read in order of date. Three writers of the second century name it, ascribe it to the apostle and write on it. A list of the same age makes John's seven churches the pattern of Paul's. In the third century a bishop reports that some before him threw it out whole. In the fourth the historian of the church lists it twice, each time with "if it should appear". The Dialogue's own sentence on John (ch. 81) was not found by my pattern and is given here through Eusebius. Still from memory, because no seat has them: Cyril's list, the Laodicene catalogue, Athanasius's letter, the lectionary, the count of manuscripts.

2AC.3. **What is not seated,** by a search of every seat's name, files and title for each name. Not found: Ignatius, Polycarp, the Didache, Barnabas, Hermas, Clement of Rome, Papias apart from Eusebius; Justin's Apologies; Tatian; Tertullian; Hippolytus; Clement of Alexandria; Origen; Athanasius; Cyril of Jerusalem; Victorinus and Andreas on the Apocalypse; the Odes of Solomon; the Gospel and the Apocalypse of Peter; the Peshitta; the Latin Bible; the Mishnah; the Targums. Thin: 1 Enoch (a Ge'ez seat of two thousand words; no Greek). Possibly present inside a larger volume and not checked: 2 Baruch (Patrologia Syriaca I.2) and the Apocalypse of Abraham (Tichonravov). A round-up would be of these, and waits on the author's word.

2AC.5. **The author, 19:40: "wait eusebius gives a si fas est".** Read again in the seat. HE 3.25.2: ἐπὶ τούτοις τακτέον, εἴ γε φανείη, τὴν Ἀποκάλυψιν Ἰωάννου. HE 3.25.4: ἔτι τε, ὡς ἔφην, ἡ Ἰωάννου Ἀποκάλυψις, εἰ φανείη. Every other book in the list is placed by a bare verbal of obligation: τακτέον, καταλεκτέον, κυρωτέον, κατατετάχθω. This one alone is placed under a condition, and placed twice. Counted: φανείη stands six times in the ten books of the History; four are plain narrative (a shadow of food appearing; if anyone be found; lest anyone be seen; that he might appear mild); the two that are a hedge on the writer's own act are these, and no other hedge of that form (εἰ with δοκοίη or δόξειε) stands anywhere in the work. So the historian's one "if it be allowed" is spent on the Apocalypse, both times. And its verb is φαίνω: not "if it seem right", which was my gloss and adds a word, but "if it should appear". Seated beside it: Catullus 51.2, ille, si fas est, superare divos, the clause Catullus adds to the poem that begins φαίνεταί μοι. What that is, is the author's to say.

2AC.4. **What follows for the method.** The rule is the author's and I broke it three times in one file: nothing is said to be seated, or not seated, without the seat being looked at. Every "from memory" in this file is now suspect of being a seat I did not open, and the list of them is owed a pass against `data/corpora`.

## 2AD. The shared phrase tried to break; names and quotation; the file's "from memory" against the seats

2AD.0. The author, 19:40: "lets run those next checks". Two: the shared-phrase result of 2AB.1 c tried against more sources, the seated Second Temple and apocalyptic Greek among them; and every "from memory" in the file checked against the seats. Scripts: `blocks/phr.py`, `blocks/phr2.py`.

2AD.1. **What could be run.** 4 Ezra cannot: Violet's edition is Latin and the versions, and this count is Greek. Run: Josephus (War, Antiquities, Life), Philo's Life of Moses, the Testaments of the Twelve, the Sibylline Oracles, and Tischendorf's Apocalypses apocryphae, which are later apocalypses (of Moses, Esdras, Paul, John, the Passing of Mary), known to stand after the Gospels and to use them. That last is the control the file has lacked from the start: a late book steeped in the Gospels, which is what the received reading says Revelation is. Sources without verses are cut into units of 25 words, and all rates are now by words, since a verse of Isaiah is half again as long as a verse of Revelation. The three editions are OCR, reduced to their Greek lines; what apparatus survives is noise against them.

2AD.2. **The rate breaks.** Shared rare phrases (2AB.1's "specific and close"), per thousand words of the source:

| Source | words | links | per thousand words | distinct phrases per thousand |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Acts | 18,362 | 53 | 2.89 | 1.91 |
| later apocalypses (Tischendorf) | 17,000 | 32 | 1.88 | 1.18 |
| **Revelation** | 9,726 | 15 | 1.54 | 0.93 |
| Josephus, Life | 15,750 | 23 | 1.46 | 1.33 |
| Josephus, War | 125,000 | 117 | 0.94 | 0.53 |
| Josephus, Antiquities | 305,750 | 260 | 0.85 | 0.49 |
| Sibylline Oracles | 29,000 | 24 | 0.83 | 0.41 |
| Testaments of the Twelve | 29,750 | 22 | 0.74 | 0.57 |
| Genesis | 40,335 | 27 | 0.67 | 0.37 |
| Isaiah | 45,687 | 27 | 0.59 | 0.48 |
| 1 Maccabees | 24,193 | 12 | 0.50 | 0.45 |
| Philo, Life of Moses | 47,250 | 22 | 0.47 | 0.30 |
| Judith, Tobit | 18,073 | 8 | 0.44 | 0.44 |
| Ezekiel | 43,284 | 16 | 0.37 | 0.25 |
| Hebrews, James, 1 Peter | 8,266 | 2 | 0.24 | 0.24 |
| Daniel's visions (7–12) | 5,139 | 1 | 0.19 | 0.19 |

(Revelation's 15 here against 19 at 2AB.1: the book is cut in ten equal parts in this run, as every source is, and not at its own joints.)

Revelation is no longer alone with Acts. A later apocalypse that uses the Gospels stands above it, and Josephus's Life stands beside it. So the rate does not show that Revelation is a source. A book written after the Gospels and out of them has the rate, and so does a book written beside them about the same country. 2AB.5 named this the result to break, and it is broken as a mark of direction. As a mark of kinship it holds and is sharper: Revelation is at three and a half times the story-books, twice the Sibyllines and the Testaments, eight times Daniel's visions, which are the nearest thing in kind.

2AD.3. **How each gets the rate.** The lists read, and then two counts made of what the reading showed.

The later apocalypses' phrases are the Gospels', said again of the same things: "weeping and gnashing of teeth"; "blessed are the meek, for they"; "from two years old and under"; "before the cock crows, thrice"; "a bright cloud"; "Simon the Cananaean"; "the harvest is great". Josephus's are the Gospels' world: "the death of Herod"; "the mountain called of Olives"; "sixty stadia"; "to give to Caesar"; "the whole of Syria"; "sent the army". Revelation's, from 2AB.1, are neither: called and chosen; within they are full; let the thirsty come; a white robe; Satan bound. No name and no incident of the story.

Counted, for every source:

| Source | links of four or more shared rare stems, per thousand words | names of the Gospels that the Septuagint does not have |
|---|---|---|
| later apocalypses | 0.41 (7) | 6: Bartholomew, Herod, Thomas, Lazarus, Tiberias, Hosanna |
| Isaiah | 0.35 (16) | none (it is in the Septuagint) |
| Acts | 0.22 (4) | 7: Alphaeus, Bartholomew, Herod, Caiaphas, Caesar, Nazorean, Pilate |
| Josephus, Life | 0.06 (1) | 4: Herod, Caesar, Capernaum, Tiberias |
| Josephus, War; Antiquities | 0.02 | 6; 10 |
| Testaments of the Twelve | 0 | 1: Herod |
| **Revelation** | **0** | **none** |
| Hebrews, James, 1 Peter | 0 | none |

(A name is a word the seat prints with a capital four times in five. By this rule Revelation has twenty-seven names in common with the Gospels, and every one is also the Septuagint's: Israel, David, Jerusalem, Judah, the tribes, Sodom, Egypt, Babylon, Satan.)

So the three ways of having the rate are distinct, and each has a count. The later apocalypses quote and name. Josephus names. Acts does both. Revelation does neither: no line of four shared words, and not one person or place of the Gospels' own. The letters also do neither, and their rate is a sixth of Revelation's. Revelation is the one source here with the phrases and without the story.

What that is worth. On the received reading Revelation is a late book that knows the Gospels. The seated specimens of such a book quote them and name Herod, Thomas and Lazarus. Revelation would be a late book that took the Gospels' phrases, forty times in four hundred verses by the wider count, and never once a name, a place or an incident, and never four words running. That is possible: it is how Revelation treats Isaiah and Ezekiel too, whom it never quotes by name. But it names their world, Babylon and Sodom and Balaam and Jezebel and the tribes, and of the Gospels' world it names nothing. The forward reading needs no account of that: the names and the incidents are what the Gospels add. This is an argument from texture and is entered beside the author's five at 2E, as a sixth with a count. It is not decisive, and its first test is owed at once: the same count turned round, Revelation against the prophets, to see whether it does take four words running from them.

2AD.4. **The file's "from memory", against the seats.** Twenty-seven places. Those a seat could settle:

| Place | Was from memory | In the seat |
|---|---|---|
| 2AC.2 | Justin, Dialogue 81, not found | found: ἀνήρ τις, ᾧ ὄνομα Ἰωάννης, εἷς τῶν ἀποστόλων τοῦ Χριστοῦ, ἐν ἀποκαλύψει γενομένῃ αὐτῷ χίλια ἔτη ποιήσειν ἐν Ἱερουσαλὴμ … προεφήτευσε (81.4) |
| 2V.5 | Zephaniah 1:7, "he has hallowed his called", the Greek line being corrupt | the Hebrew seat: הִקְדִּישׁ קְרֻאָיו. Confirmed |
| 2V.5 | the birds' supper is Ezekiel 39 | Ezek 39:17: εἰπὸν παντὶ ὀρνέῳ πετεινῷ … Συνάχθητε … ἐπὶ τὴν θυσίαν μου. Confirmed, and the verb is Rev 19:17's |
| 2U.4 b | the standing at Luke 13:25 | ἔξω ἑστάναι καὶ κρούειν. Confirmed (read at 2Y.4 b) |
| section 4 | Philo, Leg. 3.79–82, Melchizedek | confirmed: ἱερεὺς γάρ ἐστι λόγος (3.82) |
| section 3 | "Irenaeus 3.11.8 is not seated and should be" | he is seated. The passage on the four creatures is not found in the Greek remains by my pattern; Harvey's Latin, which is OCR, is not yet searched |
| 2P.4 | Archelaus's journey to Rome | Josephus is seated whole (Antiquities 1–20, War 1–7); the passage is not yet read |

Not in any seat, and still from memory: Bultmann and Fortna on the Signs Source; the handbooks' dates for 2 Peter, Jude and the Pastorals; a tradition of Elijah's three years and six months outside Scripture; the age of the codices at Mark 15:45–46; Domitian's title; Cyril's list, the Laodicene catalogue, Athanasius's letter, the lectionary.
