| of the 425 of Matthew's own (proxy) | 87 (20.5%) | 112 (26.4%) |
| with the machine layer's verses added | not recomputed | 312 (29.2%) |

By standing, the 126: 1 produced blind; 23 sealed and held; 6 sealed and failed as written; 30 counted; 18 engine; 48 read. The union with the machine layer was owed since 2AT and is now computed; most of the machine layer's verses outside the ledger are idiom and the 29.2 is an upper edge, not a reach. Lowest chapters now: 1 (2 of 25), 15 (2 of 39), 21 (3 of 45), 11 (2 of 30), 26 (10 of 75).

2AZ.8. What the round says about method. The first instrument asked for rare shared phrases. The rows of this round came three other ways: from a chapter read as a system against the part of the source that does the same work (Matthew 18 and the letters); from a change Matthew makes to Mark, checked against Revelation's wording (show for teach; opened for torn; before the time); and from reading the machine's leftovers with the moves in hand. The second is an instrument that can be built: every place where Matthew alters Mark's wording, tested for whether the alteration moves toward Revelation, with Luke's alterations of Mark as the control. It is the first test here that could tell this procedure from the received one on the received one's own ground, and it needs a synopsis in seat.

2AZ.9. Not done, and the state of the deposits. The paper (#1812) and the hologram (#1813) say 86 rows and 178 verses, which was true when they were sealed. Nothing in them is changed. The forty rows belong in a next version of each, folded into the paper's existing sections (VIII and a new stretch on the assembly; IX for the virgins; I for the passion prediction) and rebuilt into the kernel, on the author's word. No pack was run this round; the virgins and Matthew 18 are the two places to run one. The Septuagint rivals of the new rows have not been read (Daniel 3 for the furnace; Isaiah 40 is marked; Isaiah 13 and 34 for the stars and the fig tree; Daniel 7 for the cloud). Each of the forty is a relation with a move assigned by a reader who knew both books.

## 2BA. How the lexeme changes, and rows bound by relation

2BA.1. The question, 03:09. A shared lemma is what survives an operation. The operation is the stronger evidence. So: look at the rows already established, see how, why and where the word changes between Revelation and Matthew, and use that to find transforms where no word is shared but the relation is structured and has a motive. Thirteen lines were sealed at 03:11 (Q1–Q13) and six more at 03:14 on a second review (R1–R6), each before its count.

2BA.2. How it changes: the 126 rows measured (`relational/q1.py`).

(a) Matthew's side is the more verbal in 80 of 126 rows (63 per cent; Q2 held). A vision states; a life narrates.

(b) Fourteen places where a row has one root on both sides, formed differently. Eight run from a noun or adjective in Revelation to a verb in Matthew: δόξα to δοξάζω, πένθος to πενθέω, θεραπεία to θεραπεύω, ἐντολή to ἐντέλλομαι, χρόνος to χρονίζω, μάρτυς to μαρτυρέω, σκάνδαλον to σκανδαλίζω, ψυχρός to ψύχω. One runs the other way (ἑτοιμάζω to ἕτοιμος). Five stay in their class (πυλών to πύλη; ἄμπελος to ἀμπελών; βάλλω to ἐκβάλλω; ἀπέρχομαι to παρέρχομαι; ἀκάθαρτος to ἀκαθαρσία). Q1 held, eight to one. The first instrument could see none of these, because it matched lemmas.

(c) The kinds of change, with what each is for.

| Kind | Rows | Examples | Why the word changes |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1. The root reformed | 14 | glory to glorify; mourning to mourn; cold to grow cold | a state in the vision becomes an act in the telling |
| 2. Another thing in the same slot | about 9 | sickle to winnowing fork; gate-tower to gate; nourish to feed; hide to flee; keep from to rescue from | the vision's furniture is exchanged for the land's, the role in the relation held |
| 3. The bearer exchanged | 3 entered before this round | Lamb to sheep; dragon to wolf; dragon to Herod | the moves M2–M7 |
| 4. A member restored from the layer | 3 | manna to bread and word; two-edged sword to divide in two; face to God | P3 |
| 5. A condition made a thing | 2 | "you have a name that you live, and are dead" to a tomb; rich and blind to treasure and eye | F3 LIKEN |
| 6. Scale reduced | 0 entered | (a dog to a little dog, at Matthew 15, not entered) | expected at Q3 and not attested in a row |

So the word changes where the operation requires it and in the direction the operation predicts. Kind 1 follows from narration. Kinds 2 and 3 follow from P2, the named land. Kind 4 is P3. Where none of these applies the word is kept, and those are the rows the lexical instrument found first.

2BA.3. An instrument that binds by root (`relational/q2.py`). Pairs of roots, prefix removed, that Revelation and Matthew share in a verse and no book outside the Gospels has, where at least one lemma differs between the two. It touches 81 verses of Matthew, 41 of them outside the ledger and the lemma layer (Q4 held: between 20 and 80). Most of the 41 are noise of prefixes and false roots (χάραγμα with χαρά). Six were leads and are rows below: the salt thrown out and trampled; the five conditions of Matthew 25; the shepherd struck; the soldiers gathered; the workers sent into the harvest; the son thrown out of the vineyard.

2BA.4. The check for a row without a shared lemma. A row is entered as relational when: (i) three slots or more of one relation (who acts, the act, on whom, with what, until when) stand on both sides; (ii) every word that differs differs by one of kinds 1–5 above; (iii) the move is one the procedure already has; (iv) the configuration, counted over the whole New Testament under the same substitutions, stands in six places or fewer, or the row is marked as read; (v) each word is verified on its own side in the tagged text. The ledger's check already does (v): the differing words are entered as Revelation's and Matthew's and each is looked for where it is said to be. All twenty rows pass it.

2BA.5. The rows of the round: twenty, twelve marked relational.

| Revelation | Matthew | Shared lemmas | Changed: Revelation ⇒ Matthew | Standing |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 11:2 | 5:13 | — | ἐκβάλλω, ἔξωθεν, πατέω ⇒ βάλλω, ἔξω, καταπατέω | counted; relational |
| 2:10 | 5:25-26 | βάλλω, φυλακή | διάβολος ⇒ ἀντίδικος | sealed, held; relational |
| 14:15-16 | 9:37-38 | θερισμός | πέμπω, δρέπανον ⇒ ἐκβάλλω, ἐργάτης | read; relational |
| 21:6; 22:17 | 10:8 | δωρεάν, δίδωμι, λαμβάνω | — ⇒ — | sealed, held |
| 3:4; 5:2 | 10:11-13 | ἄξιος | — ⇒ τίς | sealed, failed as written |
| 3:17-18 | 11:5 | τυφλός, πτωχός | βλέπω ⇒ ἀναβλέπω | sealed, held |
| 7:3; 9:4 | 13:28-30 | δοῦλος | ἀδικέω, ἄχρι ⇒ ἐκριζόω, μέχρι, σῖτος | read; relational |

| 10:9-10; 16:13 | 15:11; 15:17-18 | στόμα, κοιλία | ἀκάθαρτος ⇒ κοινόω, ἐκπορεύομαι | sealed, held; relational |
| 2:13-14 | 16:23 | Σατανᾶς, σκάνδαλον | — ⇒ — | sealed, held |
| 13:2; 13:4 | 21:23 | τίς, ἐξουσία, δίδωμι | — ⇒ — | sealed, held |
| 6:9-11 | 21:35-36 | ἀποκτείνω | σύνδουλος ⇒ δοῦλος | read |
| 14:19-20 | 21:39 | — | ἄμπελος, ἔξωθεν, πατέω, αἷμα ⇒ ἀμπελών, ἔξω, ἐκβάλλω, ἀποκτείνω | read; relational |
| 6:9-10 | 23:35 | αἷμα, θυσιαστήριον | σφάζω, ἐκδικέω ⇒ φονεύω | sealed, held |
| 2:4; 3:15-16 | 24:12 | ἀγάπη | ψυχρός ⇒ ψύχω | sealed, held; relational |
| 7:16; 3:17-18; 2:10 | 25:35-36 | πεινάω, διψάω, γυμνός, περιβάλλω, φυλακή | — ⇒ — | counted |
| 19:15 | 26:31 | πατάσσω | ποιμαίνω ⇒ ποιμήν | counted; relational |
| 2:10; 2:13; 3:8 | 26:35; 26:69-75 | ἀρνέομαι | θάνατος, πιστός ⇒ ἀποθνήσκω, ἀπαρνέομαι | sealed, held; relational |
| 14:4 | 26:56 | — | ἀκολουθέω ⇒ φεύγω | read; relational |
| 19:19 | 27:27 | συνάγω | στράτευμα ⇒ στρατιώτης | counted; relational |

2BA.6. Five of them.

(a) What comes out of the mouth. Revelation 16:13: out of the mouth of the dragon, the beast and the false prophet come three unclean spirits. Revelation 10:9–10: the little book is sweet in the mouth and bitter in the belly. Matthew 15:11: what comes out of the mouth makes a person unclean. 15:17: what goes into the mouth goes on to the belly. στόμα with κοιλία stands within a verse at these two places and nowhere else in the New Testament. Mark's version of the saying has no mouth: "nothing from outside entering into him", "the things coming out of the person". Matthew adds στόμα twice. The unclean is ἀκάθαρτος there and κοινόω here (kind 2). The vision's image is made a rule of conduct, as the city's gate was (2AS; the paper, V.5). Matthew 15, which had no row at #1812, now has five verses.

(b) Thrown into prison. Revelation 2:10: the devil is about to throw some of you into prison, and you shall have affliction ten days. Matthew 5:25–26: your adversary at law hands you to the judge and you are thrown into prison, and do not come out until the last coin. βάλλω with φυλακή and an adversary within two verses: Matt 5:25, Luke 12:58, Rev 2:10 only. The bearer is the devil there and the adversary at law here; 1 Peter 5:8 writes the two as one phrase. The letter to Smyrna is the seed of James in the programme's first derivation.

(c) The love grown cold. ἀγάπη stands once in Matthew, at 24:12. ψύχω stands once in the New Testament, at 24:12. The root of cold stands elsewhere only at Matthew 10:42 and at Revelation 3:15–16. Ephesus: you have left your first love. Laodicea: you are neither cold nor hot. One sentence of Matthew's own, made of two letters, with the adjective reformed as a verb (kind 1), and set as a foretelling among the false prophets (2AZ) and the enduring to the end.

(d) Jezebel and Herodias. Revelation 2:20–23: the woman Jezebel, who calls herself a prophetess; I will kill her children. Matthew 14:3–11: Herod's brother's woman; her daughter; the prophet killed. Woman, a prophet-word, a child-word and killing within three verses: these two places only. The killing the speaker threatens is done by the adversary, to the prophet (M3). With the seizing and binding of 14:3 (2AZ), Matthew 14 now has 24 of 36 verses in rows.

(e) The shepherd struck. Revelation 19:15: that with it he may strike the nations, and he shall shepherd them with a rod of iron. Matthew 26:31: I will strike the shepherd. πατάσσω with the shepherd-root: Matt 26:31, Mark 14:27, Rev 19:15 only. The one who strikes and shepherds becomes the shepherd who is struck: the exchange of patient (M2) in three words. Matthew cites it as written, from Zechariah; the row is three-way and marked.

2BA.7. The second review (03:13), checked. It proposes working from what Revelation must become to be a Gospel: disciples from the letters' conditions of reception, disputes from rival sovereignties, miracles from the restored world, a character as a path through positions; and composing a passage before consulting its wording. It marks its own proposals as not executed. What could be counted was sealed and counted.

Failure enacted. The review's sharpest observation is on watching: the command is placed where its recipients fail to do it. The ledger had that row (Rev 3:2–3; Matt 26:40). The same form holds three more times, all in the passion. "You did not deny my name" (Rev 3:8; 2:13): he denied. "Be faithful to death" (2:10): "though I must die with you". "They follow the Lamb wherever he goes" (14:4): all the disciples left him and fled. Four conditions of the conqueror, each staged and not met. That is an operation the procedure did not have, and it is entered as a variant of F2: ENACT as failure. It predicts where to look next: every "to the one who conquers" and every praise in the letters, against the followers' conduct from Gethsemane to the courtyard.

A position held for one verse. "Get behind me, Satan: you are a stumbling block to me" (Matt 16:23). Mark 8:33 has no stumbling block. Σατανᾶς within two verses of σκάνδαλον stands at Matt 16:23 and Rev 2:13–14 and nowhere else: Pergamum, where Satan dwells, and Balaam's stumbling block. Matthew's addition to Mark supplies the second word of the letter.

A character as a path. Read from the ledger, with no new count: the eight rows that fall on verses where Peter is named put Peter in five positions of the source. The speaker's office (the keys). The seer's (fallen as dead; fear not). The conqueror's (standing on the sea). A foundation's (a name among the twelve). The assemblies', failed (watch; do not deny). And once the adversary's. The review's proposal holds on the rows already entered.

Sovereignty. "Who gave you this authority?" τίς with ἐξουσία and δίδωμι in one verse: the three Synoptic parallels and Rev 13:4, where the dragon gave the beast authority and the earth asks who is like it. ἄξιος never stands in Mark; Matthew has it nine times and Revelation seven. "Who is worthy" was predicted unique to Matt 10:11 and Rev 5:2 and is not: Acts 13:25 has the two words.

Not done from the review: composing a passage from its conditions before reading it (a pack, with blind readers, is the form that already exists for this, and none was run); the miracles as a class from Revelation 21–22 (one row, 11:5, three-way with Isaiah).

2BA.8. Reach.

| | at #1812 | after 2AZ | now |
|---|---|---|---|
| rows | 86 | 126 | 146 |
| verses reached | 178 (16.7%) | 246 (23.1%) | 289 (27.1%) |
| without rows marked relational | 178 | 246 | 257 |
| shared with Mark (458) | 51 | 76 | 94 (20.5%) |
| double tradition (184) | 40 | 58 | 67 (36.4%) |
| Matthew's own (425) | 87 | 112 | 128 (30.1%) |

By standing, the 146: 1 produced blind; 33 sealed and held; 7 sealed and failed as written; 34 counted; 18 engine; 53 read. Sealed this round: 19 lines. Thirteen held outright (Q1, Q2, Q4–Q8, Q11–Q13, R1, R3, R4). R5 held less one verse. Q3 had the order of the kinds right and its fifth kind is not attested. R2 failed as written. Q9, Q10 and R6 were expected to be readings and are.

2BA.9. What this does and does not license. A relational row has less surface than a lexical one and more structure. Its risk is the reader: with five kinds of substitution allowed, a reader who knows both books can join many things. Three things hold that down here. Each row's configuration was counted under its own substitutions, and seven of the twelve stand in three places or fewer. Seven of the twelve were written down before they were counted. And in four rows of the round the change Matthew makes to Mark goes toward Revelation (the mouth; the stumbling block; thrown out and then killed; gathered). What is still missing is the control that would price the method: the same five kinds of substitution, the same check, run from another source into Matthew, or from Revelation into a book that is not a Gospel. Until it is run, the relational rows are reported apart from the others, as in the table.

2BA.10. Owed from this round. The control of 2BA.9. The instrument of 2AZ.8 (Matthew's changes to Mark, toward Revelation or not), which this round fed four more cases. ENACT as failure taken through every promise and praise of the seven letters. A better root rule than the first five letters. The kernel's builder to carry "relational" as a standing of its own.

## 2BB. The order of the operations: a first look, and nothing yet

2BB.1. The distinction, from the review of 03:18. The tests of 2AI, 2AH and 2AM looked for Revelation's order in Matthew and did not find it. That leaves open another thing: an order among the operations, a law that says which transform comes where in the Gospel. The author: if the bulk can be mapped, the larger structure will show; if the order of the transforms resolves as a determinate transform, that is as strong as Q, and Q is a text that can be made to say what is wanted. The review adds the test: freeze the grammar and the law, hold a block of Matthew out, and predict what kind of thing stands there, which relations it develops, and what it depends on.

2BB.2. What was done now. The ledger has 146 rows. Seven lines were sealed at 03:19 and 03:22 and counted (`order2/s1.py`, `s7.py`). A caution was written first: the rows were found by a reader who searched some chapters against some parts, so each test is run also on the 42 rows no reader chose where to look for (engine rows and rows found by the machine layers).

| Line | Asked | Result |
|---|---|---|
| S1 | are the 13 blocks of Matthew and the 12 parts of Revelation associated | no: permutation p = 0.54 on all rows; 0.11 on the unchosen |
| S2 | where does each part go | Revelation 12 most in Matt 1–2, 21–22 in 5–7, 19–20 in 24–27, as written; the letters most in 19–23, not 18; given S1, none of it to be leaned on |
| S3 | does a row in a discourse draw on more parts | in direction, small: 1.24 against 1.14 |
| S4 | does the exchange of patient sit in the passion | no: 8 of 23 rows |
| S5 | is a place of the source taught before it is enacted | no: 54 per cent against a baseline of 52; and the rule that sorts speech from narrative is bad, so this shows nothing either way |
| S6 | does a discourse share more with the narrative before it | expected nothing; two of five do (18; 24–25) |
| S7 | do neighbouring rows in Matthew draw on neighbouring places of Revelation | no: ratio 0.92, p = 0.135 |

2BB.3. What that is. At 146 rows and 27 per cent, by these seven instruments, there is no law of arrangement to report. Which part of Revelation feeds which block of Matthew is not told from chance. That agrees with what the paper says of the source (held open; any place at any point) and it is not yet evidence for it, because a table of 156 cells with 146 rows in it is thin: most cells hold 0, 1 or 2. The knots (the gates at 7:6–13; the virgins; chapter 18 and the letters) are real as counted places and do not add up to a neighbourhood effect across the Gospel (S7).

2BB.4. What the instruments cannot yet see. They ask where Revelation's parts go. The proposal is about the operations. The ledger's "move" column is a reader's phrase per row and is not a fixed vocabulary; S4 and S5 tried to use it and the second needed a sorter of speech and narrative that does not work. Before an order of operations can be tested the operations have to be a closed set assigned by rule: each row given one move of M1–M7 and one form of F1–F5 (with ENACT as failure), by a second reader blind to the first, and the Matthew side sorted into speech and narrative from a text that marks speech. That is owed and it is the next piece of work on this line, ahead of more rows.

2BB.5. Held out. From 03:24 on 27 November, Matthew 11–12 is held out: 80 verses, 12 reached by 6 rows. Nothing more is sought or entered there. When there is a frozen grammar and a stated law, they are run against it, with Revelation and with other sources in its place. It was chosen because it is thin, it mixes narrative and discourse, and it has not been read against Revelation as a whole. If the author would rather hold out another block, that has to be said before the next round of rows.

2BB.6. On Q, what would and would not be equal. Q is asked to explain one thing: about 230 verses that Matthew and Luke share in close wording and often in order, and that Mark lacks. This file reaches 67 of the 184 verses the proxy assigns to that material, by relation and seldom by wording. The asymmetry the author names is the programme's own (#1747): Q's wording and order are reconstructed from the texts it explains, and Revelation is fixed, so the transform has to do the work. To stand where Q stands on Q's ground, three things are needed that are not here: the same transform taking Revelation to Luke; an account of why Matthew and Luke then agree in wording where neither is quoting Revelation (one used the other, or both used a first unfolding now lost, and the second is a hypothetical source again); and the law of arrangement. To stand somewhere Q does not, one thing is needed: the held-out block predicted. That is nearer.

2BB.7. The measures are fixed for rerunning. S1 and S7 are cheap and are rerun as written at each hundred rows. A law that appears only after the measure is changed is not counted.

## 2BC. The fourth review checked: the passion, Peter, and what a row needs

2BC.1. The review (03:23) reads v0.54. Its arithmetic is right (111 verses added since the deposit; 32 of them by rows marked relational). It takes the four failures of 2BA.7 as one composed thing with an inner order, proposes Peter as a sequence of positions to be mapped whole, asks for a field recording what each transform changes in the told world, and names the double tradition as an object of its own. It says it ran no counts. Seven lines were sealed at 03:25 and counted (`order2/t1.py`). Matthew 11–12 stayed held out.

2BC.2. Gethsemane and the courtyard hold more of the letters than 2BA.7 had.

| The letters | Matthew 26 | Count |
|---|---|---|
| be watchful; you shall not know the hour (3:2–3) | could you not watch one hour (26:40) | 2AZ |
| I will keep you from the hour of trial (3:10) | watch and pray, that you enter not into trial (26:41) | ὥρα within a verse of πειρασμός: Matt 26:41, Mark 14:38, Rev 3:10 only (T2 held) |
| be faithful to death (2:10) | though I must die with you (26:35) | 2BA |
| you did not deny my name (3:8; 2:13) | he denied (26:70–74) | 2BA |
| they follow wherever he goes (14:4) | all left him and fled (26:56) | 2BA |
| remember from where you have fallen, and repent (2:5; 3:3) | Peter remembered the word, and wept (26:75) | remembering within a verse of repenting or weeping: five places of the New Testament (T3 held) |

The last is new and it is not a failure. The letters give the fallen a remedy, remember and turn, and the scene ends on it. So the stretch has the letters' own shape: a condition, its failure, the remedy.

2BC.3. What the order of the four does and does not show. The review reads an ascent: the obligation taken, the watch failed, the flight, the denial. Two checks. Mark has the four in the same order; Luke has no flight and John no sleeping (T5 held). So the order belongs to the passion as all the Gospels tell it and says nothing of Matthew's composing apart from Mark's. And the dependencies that can be named without looking at the order (a promise before its breaking) allow 12 of the 24 orders of the four (T6 held). They thin the orders by half and do not fix one. The ascent is a reading of the order. It is not yet a law that yields it.

One test that failed, so that it is not tried again in this form (T1): taking by tag every second-person verb of Revelation 2–3 and counting them in windows of Matthew does not pick out chapter 26. The set is common verbs (see, hear, find, fall). The window from 26:31 ranks 21st of 1,023.

2BC.4. Peter, counted. Peter is named in 23 verses of Matthew. The ledger reaches 12 (T7 failed: 60 per cent was predicted). Not reached: the call (4:18), the mother-in-law (8:14), "explain the parable" (15:15), the confession itself (16:16), the rebuke (16:22), the ascent of the mountain (17:1), the collectors (17:24), "how often shall I forgive" (18:21), "though all stumble" (26:33), taken aside in Gethsemane (26:37), following at a distance (26:58). The review's table of positions agrees with the rows. Its reading of them as one development (authority received, its conditions mistaken, the manifestation met, the test failed) is a reading of eight rows, and the eleven verses above are where it can be tried.

One of them yielded now. At 17:25, in a passage only Matthew has: "from whom do the kings of the earth take toll or tribute?" and "then the sons are free." The kings of the earth stand in the Gospels here only, in Acts once from the psalm, and eight times in Revelation. With ἐλεύθερος within a verse: Matt 17:25–26, Rev 6:15, Rev 19:18–19, and nowhere else (T4 held). In Revelation the kings of the earth and every slave and free are the roll of those judged; in Matthew they are the terms of a question about who owes.

2BC.5. A field for what a row needs and establishes. Entered for eight rows of one stretch (16:19 to 26:75), as a trial of the field and no more.

| Row (Matthew) | Needs first | Establishes |
|---|---|---|
| 16:19 | nothing | a follower holds the keys |
| 16:21 | nothing | the death that must be is announced |
| 16:23 | the announcement | a follower stands against it and is named for it |
| 26:35 with 26:69–75 | a denial foretold | an obligation taken, then broken |
| 26:40–41 | a command to watch | the watch is failed |
| 26:41 | the watch failed once | trial is entered |
| 26:56 | the seizing | the followers are gone |
| 26:75 | the denial, and the word foretelling it | the fallen one remembers |

Every "needs" here is met earlier in Matthew, which it must be in any coherent story and so shows little. The field becomes a test only when it is filled for a whole block by a reader who has not seen the block's order, and the orders it allows are counted against the order Matthew has, as at T6. That is the form of the review's proposal that can fail.

2BC.6. The double tradition, by the proxy. 67 of 184 verses reached. The unreached lie mostly in chapters 5–7 (25 of 41 unreached), 10 (8 of 12), 11–12 (27 of 28, held out), 24–25 (17 of 21). Outside the held-out block 90 are unreached, and 50 of those are in three discourses: the Sermon, the mission and the last. A synopsis in seat is still owed before this class is more than a proxy.

2BC.7. On the Jezebel row, and a correction. At v0.56 this paragraph said that Kings is not in seat and gave its contents from memory: the queen, the prophet, the threat, and no child of hers in the plot. Both statements were wrong. The author asked at 03:28 whether I was positive. The whole of Swete's Septuagint is seated (`data/corpora/lxx-swete`, 56 books, the four of Kingdoms and both Daniels among them). I had taken the six books my own checking script loads for the list of what is seated. The same error stands behind every "not read" of a Septuagint rival in 2AZ.9; those can be read.

Read in the seat. 3 Kingdoms 18:4 and 18:13: Jezebel kills the prophets of the Lord. 18:19: the prophets who eat at Jezebel's table. 19:2: her threat to Elijah. 4 Kingdoms 9:22: "the fornications of Jezebel your mother and her many sorceries". 9:36: the dogs shall eat the flesh of Jezebel. 10:1–8, straight after her death: Ahab's seventy sons are slaughtered, their heads put in baskets and brought.

So Kings has the woman, the prophets killed, the killing of the sons, and heads carried in. Revelation 2:23, "I will kill her children", is that chapter of Kings compressed, and Matthew 14 can be reached from Kings with no Revelation between: the head brought in on a dish is nearer 4 Kingdoms 10:7–8 than anything in the letter. The row stays, by the ruling of 2AV on three-way rows, and is now marked three-way; what it can show is narrowed to the count within the New Testament. It is a case for the third parameter and not against it: a place of Revelation with its layer open, where the target has a member (the head) that the layer has and Revelation dropped. But here the received account needs nothing more than Mark reading Kings, and that is said in the row.

One thing found in passing, entered nowhere yet. Revelation 22:15: "outside are the dogs and the sorcerers and the fornicators". 4 Kingdoms 9:22 and 9:36 have Jezebel's fornications, her sorceries, and the dogs. The three stand together in Kings on one person.

2BC.8. Reach: 149 rows; 291 of 1,067 verses (27.3 per cent); 259 without the rows marked relational. Sealed this round: seven lines; T2–T6 held, T1 and T7 failed.

## 2BD. The map of order: Matthew against Revelation

2BD.1. Asked at 03:32: set the order of the transforms in Matthew against their order in Revelation; if there is a pattern it could predict new relations. And at 03:33: if there is an order, it is at the least running backwards. The map is one point per row, at the row's first verse of Matthew and the median of the places of Revelation it draws on, 149 points (figure: `REV-MATTHEW-ORDER-MAP.png`; scripts `ordermap/u1.py` to `u4.py`, `fig.py`). Six lines were sealed before anything was drawn or counted.

2BD.2. What the map shows.

| Asked | Result |
|---|---|
| Is there a whole-book order, forward or backward (longest chain) | no: longest rising chain 27 (p = 0.17), longest falling 25 (p = 0.50) |
| Is the order backward (rank correlation) | −0.092, p = 0.13: negative, and not told from chance |
| Does cutting Revelation and setting the second piece first help | no: the best of 21 cuts gives 0.122, forward, cut before chapter 7; corrected p = 0.76; the cut at chapter 12 gives −0.085 |
| Halves against halves | flat: Matthew's first half draws on Revelation's first half 33 times and its second 34; Matthew's second half, 40 and 42 |
| Local runs (three rows or more, close in both books, moving one way) | two, both in Matthew 26, both forward; not more than shuffling gives (p = 0.20) |
| Do rows near in Matthew sit near in Revelation more than chance | near pairs are more frequent than shuffling gives (p = 0.009); 30 rise and 23 fall (p = 0.41) |
| Does the neighbourhood predict (interpolation) | no: 4 rare lemmas shared between the stretches that lie between near pairs, against 6.1 from random stretches |

2BD.3. On "running backwards". The sign is backward. The size is small, and at 149 rows it is not established. Broken down after the fact by how the rows were found, which was not sealed:

| Rows | n | Correlation | p |
|---|---|---|---|
| written down before counting, or produced blind | 37 | −0.42 | 0.006 |
| engine | 18 | −0.25 | 0.15 |
| found by the machine layers | 22 | −0.15 | 0.26 |
| read or counted | 94 | +0.06 | 0.73 |

It is negative in every class that was not found by reading and it reaches 0.05 only among the rows I wrote down beforehand. Those are the rows where I chose what to predict, and I predicted Revelation 21–22 in the Sermon and the letters in the last chapters, so the lean there is partly my choosing. On the figure the lean is two clusters: the end of Revelation against Matthew 2–7 at the upper left, and the letters against Matthew 23–27 at the lower right. The middle of both books is scattered. What can be said: the two ends of Revelation go to opposite ends of Matthew more than the reverse, in rows of the stronger standings; the body of the map has no direction.

2BD.4. The two runs. Matthew 26:35, 26:40, 26:41, 26:48 against Revelation 2:13, 3:2, 3:10, 3:19: the night from the promise to the kiss walks the letters in their order, Pergamum, Sardis, Philadelphia, Laodicea. Matthew 26:52, 26:56, 26:64 against Revelation 13:10, 14:4, 14:14: the sword, the flight, the son of man on the cloud. Both run forward, inside a book-wide lean that is backward if it is anything. Two runs are what chance gives at this density, so they are recorded and not leaned on. A first count had eight runs at p = 0.001 and was wrong: five were single rows that draw on several places, which sort into a "run" by themselves. It is kept in the sealed file as the error it was.

2BD.5. Whether the pattern predicts. Interpolation was the test: if neighbours in Matthew are neighbours in Revelation, then the unmapped verses lying between two such rows should share more with the verses lying between in Revelation than with a random stretch. They share less (ratio 0.65). So the nearness of neighbours is the knots already entered (a parable gathered from one stretch of the letters), and it does not extend to the verses around them. Nothing was entered from this test.

2BD.6. What would predict, if there is a law. The map tests one kind of law, position against position. The author's proposal and the reviews' is another kind, order among operations, and 2BB.4 says what that needs before it can be tested: the moves as a closed set assigned by rule, and Matthew sorted into speech and narrative from a text that marks speech. Two smaller things follow from this round and can be done first. The lean of the two ends can be tested where no one has chosen: run the lemma instrument from Revelation 1–3 and from Revelation 20–22 over Matthew's two halves and see whether each end prefers the opposite half. And the two runs in Matthew 26 name their own next members: after Revelation 3:19 comes 3:20, the door and the supper, and before 2:13 comes 2:10; the supper stands before the promise in Matthew, which is backward for that step.

## 2BE. Strands: order inside the units of Revelation, and the one run that predicts

2BE.1. A fifth review came at 03:38 with its own count of order on 112 rows read off the tables of v0.56 (whole-book figure +0.009: nothing), and a proposal: the Gospel is several passes through Revelation, each strand walked in its own order; Laodicea 3:17–21 and Revelation 20:1–3 are named as strands; four relations are predicted and none of them checked; the next move is to cut the ledger into continuous strands and map the order of each. Nine lines were sealed before any count (V1 to V5, V6, V7a to V7c). Matthew 11–12 stayed held out. Scripts `strands/v1.py`, `v6.py`, `fig.py`; figure `REV-MATTHEW-ORDER-BY-UNIT.png`.

2BE.2. Strands by the ledger's own labels. Every row carries a strand label, set by me as the row went in: 69 labels, the largest "teaching" and "adversaries" at ten rows each. Pairs of rows under one label that stand in the same order in both books: 96. In the opposite order: 108. That is 47 per cent, p = 0.73. Sealed as no effect; held. The labels are loose and mine, and a review's strand is another thing.

2BE.3. Strands as the review means them: a continuous passage of Revelation. The units were fixed under seal: each of the seven letters, and each other chapter. One point per row per unit it touches.

| Unit | Rows | Same order | Opposite | p forward | p backward |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| All 27 units together | | 358 | 365 | 0.55 | 0.46 |
| Ephesus 2:1–7 | 5 | 6 | 4 | 0.38 | 0.79 |
| Smyrna 2:8–11 | 6 | 2 | 3 | 0.66 | 0.51 |
| Pergamum 2:12–17 | 5 | 4 | 6 | 0.77 | 0.40 |
| Sardis 3:1–6 | 7 | 6 | 12 | 0.87 | 0.21 |
| Philadelphia 3:7–13 | 6 | 6 | 7 | 0.66 | 0.48 |
| Laodicea 3:14–22 | 11 | 23 | 22 | 0.50 | 0.57 |
| ch. 1 | 10 | 18 | 26 | 0.80 | 0.26 |
| ch. 6 | 13 | 34 | 44 | 0.76 | 0.28 |
| **ch. 12** | **12** | **51** | **12** | **0.003** | 1.00 |
| ch. 13 | 12 | 25 | 39 | 0.85 | 0.19 |
| ch. 14 | 12 | 22 | 44 | 0.95 | 0.068 |
| ch. 19 | 13 | 46 | 28 | 0.14 | 0.88 |
| ch. 20 | 10 | 11 | 28 | 0.95 | 0.079 |
| ch. 21 | 12 | 34 | 30 | 0.44 | 0.61 |
| ch. 22 | 10 | 24 | 17 | 0.26 | 0.75 |

Taken together the units are at one half exactly. Laodicea, the review's first strand, has eleven rows and they land at Matthew 6, 11, 18, 19, 20, 23, 24, 25 and 26 in no order of the letter: 23 against 22. Revelation 20:1–3, its second, is one place drawn on seven times (Matt 14:3; 16:19; 18:18; 22:1–14; 26:50; 27:2; 27:63–66): a point with many landings, and a point has no order to walk. I sealed that no unit of five rows or more would pass p = 0.01 in either direction. One did. That line failed.

2BE.4. Chapter 12 runs forward: 51 pairs in order, 12 against, p = 0.003. It is one unit of twenty-two tested in two directions, so about 0.13 after correction; on the count alone it could be the one tall draw of twenty-two. What it rests on was sealed before it was looked at (V7a): take away the four rows that land in Matthew 2–3 and the eight left stand 14 against 12, p = 0.45. Held. The run is the infancy.

| Revelation 12 | Matthew | |
|---|---|---|
| 12:1 the sign in heaven, the stars | 2:2 his star | |
| 12:5 who shall shepherd the nations | 2:6 who shall shepherd my people | one step out of order |
| 12:4–6 the dragon waits to devour the child; the flight; the place prepared | 2:13–16 Herod seeks the child; the flight; the slaughter | |
| 12:6 the wilderness | 3:3 a voice in the wilderness | |

The received reading of Matthew 2 builds it from scripture: Micah 5:2 for the town, Hosea 11:1 for Egypt, Jeremiah 31:15 for Ramah, Numbers 24:17 for the star, the infancy of Moses for the king who kills the children. Each of those is a place Matthew names or echoes, and none is disputed here. They give the furniture. They do not give the sequence sign, child, devourer, flight, wilderness, and Revelation 12:1–6 has that sequence in six verses. (From memory, not checked this round: commentaries on Revelation 12 set Herod beside the dragon as a matter of course, and read the likeness from Matthew to Revelation.)

2BE.5. The order then predicts. If Matthew 2 walks Revelation 12:1–6 as it stands, then 12:4, which the ledger had only as part of a span, should land between Matthew 2:2 and 2:13. Revelation 12:4: the dragon's tail drags a third of the stars (τῶν ἀστέρων) and throws them to the earth, and the dragon stood (ἕστηκεν) before the woman, to devour her child (τὸ τέκνον) when she bore it. Matthew 2:9: the star (ὁ ἀστήρ) went before them until it came and stood (ἐστάθη) over where the child (τὸ παιδίον) was. Sealed (V7b): a star and a standing in one verse, four places of the New Testament or fewer. Counted: two. Matthew 2:9 and Revelation 12:4, and no other verse. Both have the child.

- A note on the count as written. The tagged text files Revelation 12:4's ἕστηκεν under στήκω (Tischendorf's imperfect) and Matthew's ἐστάθη under ἵστημι. I sealed ἵστημι alone, and that returned Matthew 2:9 only. στήκω is the by-form made from ἕστηκα, the perfect of ἵστημι; with it, the two. The line is recorded as held with this note, and the first return is kept.
- The changes, by the kinds of 2BA.2. The bearer is exchanged: what stands over the child is the star, where it was the dragon. The sign is turned: the stars are thrown down by the enemy; the star goes before and guides. τέκνον to παιδίον, another word in the same slot. ἐνώπιον to ἐπάνω.
- The Septuagint, read in the seat. Numbers 24:17: ἀνατελεῖ ἄστρον ἐξ Ἰακώβ καὶ ἀναστήσεται ἄνθρωπος ἐξ Ἰσραήλ. That reaches Matthew 2:2 (the star ἐν τῇ ἀνατολῇ). It has ἄστρον where Matthew has ἀστήρ, the one who rises is the man, and there is no child and no standing over. The row at 2:9 is not reached by it.

The walk through Matthew 2 is now 2:2, 2:6, 2:9, 2:13–16, 3:3 against 12:1, 12:5, 12:4, 12:4–6, 12:6: five rows, one neighbouring pair exchanged, and the exchanged one is the citation of Micah, which Matthew places where the scribes answer. This is the first row in the ledger found by asking the order where to look. It is one chapter, the smallest possible run, and the chapter was first entered by me with the infancy in mind. It does not make order a law. It makes Revelation 12:1–6 → Matthew 2:1–3:3 a pass, in the review's sense, that can be tested further: 12:2 (the birth pangs) predicted no row at Matthew 2:3 and gave none (V7c, sealed as a miss in advance).

2BE.6. The review's four predictions, each counted.

| Predicted | Counted | Standing |
|---|---|---|
| Matt 18:21–22 ← Rev 2:21; 3:19–20 | shared words: λέγω, ἀφίημι. Genesis 4:24 in the Septuagint has ἑπτάκις … ἑβδομηκοντάκις ἑπτά, Matthew 18:22's phrase entire | no row; scripture reaches it |
| Matt 20:25–28 ← Rev 13:2–7; 5:9–10 | with those places: ἔθνος, δίδωμι, μέγας. Nearer: Rev 1:5–6, the ruler (ἄρχων) of the kings of the earth, who loosed us (λύσαντι) by his blood and made us a kingdom. Matthew: the rulers (ἄρχοντες) of the nations; his life a ransom (λύτρον). ἄρχων beside loosing or ransom: three places in the New Testament (Rev 1:5; Matt 20:25; Luke 24:20–21, where the rulers deliver him and "we hoped he would redeem Israel") | row entered from 1:5–6; relational; shared with Mark, where Mark 10:42 has the verb ἄρχειν and Matthew the noun |
| Matt 25:28–29 ← Rev 3:11; 2:25–26 | ἔχω, δίδωμι; "what he has shall be taken" stands in seven verses of the Gospels | no row; a reading |
| Matt 26:58 ← Rev 14:4 | 14:4 shares ἀκολουθέω: these follow the lamb wherever he goes; Peter followed from afar. The afar is Rev 18:10, 15, 17, the only μακρόθεν in the book: they stand far off and watch her end. Matthew alone adds that Peter sat "to see the end" (Mark 14:54: warming himself at the fire). But μακρόθεν with seeing stands in nine places, one more than I sealed, and Mark's ἀπὸ μακρόθεν is the nearer phrase | row entered from both places, as read, relational, not counted as bound |

The second is the review's relation with the source corrected; the fourth is the review's source with a second beside it. Two of four yield a row, one of them weak.

2BE.7. Reach: 152 rows; 297 of 1,067 verses (27.8 per cent); 260 without the rows marked relational. By the proxy classes: Mark's material 98 of 458, the double tradition 67 of 184, Matthew's own 132 of 425. Matthew 11–12: twelve verses, as before; nothing sought there.

2BE.8. Where order stands. The whole book has none (2BB, 2BD). The strands as I labelled them have none. The units of Revelation taken together have none, and neither of the two strands the review named has one. One unit has: Revelation 12:1–6 is walked forward by Matthew 2:1–3:3, and the walk named a verse before the verse was read. Owed: units finer than a chapter, which need more rows per unit than the ledger has; strands drawn by a second reader, blind; the same walk asked of Exodus 1–2, which is seated and was not read this round; the same test at each hundred rows, as written.

## 2BF. The held-out block opened for ten lines: Matthew 11–12 and the opening of Revelation

2BF.1. At 03:52 the author asked what Matthew 11–12 is, and on being told its contents said (03:54): "it sure sounds a lot like the opening of revelation, and another remix of the letters". That was registered as a prediction on the held-out block, made specific in ten lines from memory with no text opened, and not counted. At 03:55: "lets run it". The way each line is counted, and its bound, were sealed before the count. The ten were run once. The verses of the block that no line names stay held for the frozen grammar (2BB.4). Script `heldout/w1.py`.

2BF.2. The received reading. Matthew 11:2–19 and 11:20–27 are double tradition (Luke 7:18–35; 10:12–15, 21–22), assigned to a sayings source; 11:10 cites Malachi 3:1 with Exodus 23:20; 11:28–30 is Matthew's own and is read from Sirach 51 and Jeremiah 6:16; 12:22–30 stands in Mark 3 and in the double tradition both. None of that is disputed below, and two of the ten lines are settled by it.

2BF.3. The ten lines.

| | Matthew | Revelation | Counted | Standing |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| W1 | 11:2–3 John sends: are you the one who is coming (ὁ ἐρχόμενος) | 1:4, 1:8 John to the seven assemblies: from the one who is and was and is coming | ὁ ἐρχόμενος with John within three verses: Matt 11:3; Luke 7:19, 20; Rev 1:4, 1:8. Five places, no other | held; row; shared with Luke |
| W2 | 11:10 I send my messenger | 1:1 sent through his messenger to John | nine places, six sealed. Malachi 3:1 in the seat reaches it | failed; no row |
| W3 | 11:6 blessed is whoever | 1:3 blessed is the one | 16 verses | failed, as expected |
| W4 | 11:15 who has ears, let him hear | the refrain of the seven letters; 13:9 | sixteen verses: Matthew 3, Mark 3, Luke 2, Revelation 8. None in John, Acts or the letters | held; row |
| W5 | 11:20–21 cities charged: the powers done in them, they did not repent | the letters: a city, its works, repent | μετανοέω within two verses of ἔργον: eleven places, eight sealed. The only place in the Gospels; eight of the eleven are Revelation | failed on the bound; entered as read |
| W6 | 11:27 all handed to me by my Father; to whom the Son wills to reveal | 1:1 the revelation which God gave him, to show | reveal root with give root in one verse: Matt 11:27; Luke 10:22; Rev 1:1; Eph 1:17; 2 Cor 12:7 | held; row; relational; shared with Luke |
| W7 | 11:28–29 all who toil; I will give you rest | 14:13 they shall rest from their toils | rest within a verse of toil: Matt 11:28, 29; Rev 14:13; no other | held on the count; three-way; Sirach reaches further |
| W8 | 12:29 unless he first bind the strong one | 20:2 bound him, the devil and Satan | δέω within three verses of Satan or the devil: Matt 12:29; Mark 3:27; Luke 13:16; Rev 20:2 | held; row; shared with Mark |
| W9 | 12:45 seven other spirits | 1:4; 3:1; 4:5; 5:6 the seven spirits | the six named, and Acts 6:3, 21:4; Luke 8:2; Rev 17:3 | failed as written ("no other") |
| W10 | 12:36–37 an account of every word | 20:12–13 | no shared content word | failed, as expected |

Five of ten hold. I sealed seven. W2 and W5 have both verses in the set and miss the bound by three each; W9 has all six verses and four more.

2BF.4. What the five are.

- W1 is the plainest. The greeting of Revelation names the one who is coming, in the nominative against its own preposition (ἀπὸ ὁ ὢν καὶ ὁ ἦν καὶ ὁ ἐρχόμενος), and John is the sender. In Matthew John is the sender and the name is put to Jesus as a question. The title becomes a question (M5), and the bearer of the title moves from the one on the throne to the one asked.
- W6 is the title of the book. Ἀποκάλυψις Ἰησοῦ Χριστοῦ, ἣν ἔδωκεν αὐτῷ ὁ θεός: a noun, a giving, God, him. Matthew 11:27: παρεδόθη, ὑπὸ τοῦ πατρός μου, ὁ υἱός, ἀποκαλύψαι. The noun is reformed as the verb, which is the commonest change of 2BA.2 (eight of fourteen), and the chain God → him → his servants is spoken by him in the first person.
- W4 is the refrain kept as it stands.
- W8 adds 12:29 to a place the ledger already draws on seven times.
- W7 I had called the weakest and it is the narrowest count of the ten: toil and rest together at Matthew 11:28–29 and Revelation 14:13 and nowhere else in the New Testament. But Sirach 51:26–27, read in the seat, has ζυγόν, ψυχή, εὑρεῖν, ἐκοπίασα and ἀνάπαυσιν in two verses. The saying is reached by scripture, and more fully. The row is kept and marked three-way, with Sirach the nearer.

2BF.5. On the author's reading. The opening: held at 1:4 and 1:8 (W1) and at 1:1 (W6); failed at 1:1's messenger (W2) and at 1:3 (W3). The letters: the refrain held (W4); the form of the letter, a city charged for what was done in it, is there and is Matthew's only such place, and misses its bound (W5). So Matthew 11 does stand to Revelation 1–3 as the reading says, at four of the six lines that concern them, two by count and two with scripture or the bound against them. Chapter 12 gave one of three. Order: 11:3, 11:15, 11:20, 11:27 against 1:4, the letters, the letters, 1:1. The title comes last. No walk.

2BF.6. What was spent. The block was held so that a frozen grammar and a stated law could be run on text the ledger was not fitted to. Ten lines of it are now used: 11:2–3, 11:6, 11:10, 11:15, 11:20–21, 11:27, 11:28–29, 12:29, 12:36–37, 12:45. They were used as a registered prediction, stated before the count and scored with its misses, which is a test of its own kind and a fair one. The rest of the 80 verses stay held.

2BF.7. Reach: 158 rows; 305 of 1,067 verses (28.6 per cent); 265 without the rows marked relational. Mark's material 100 of 458, the double tradition 70 of 184, Matthew's own 135 of 425. Matthew 11–12: 20 of 80.

## 2BG. "Matthew is treating Revelation like scripture": the sentence, and a first count of it

2BG.1. At 04:16, slowly, the author: "matthew. is treating. revelation. like scripture." At 04:18: "lets count it". The sentence changes what three earlier results are. The rows where the Septuagint also reaches a verse were being marked down as rivalry; under the sentence they are conflation, which is how Matthew handles two scriptures at once (Malachi 3:1 with Exodus 23:20 at 11:10). The null on order (2BB, 2BD, 2BE) was being read as a failure to find what a source like Q would show; under the sentence it is what is expected, since no one supposes Matthew walks Isaiah in order. And the one run, Revelation 12:1–6 through Matthew 2, stands beside the infancy of Moses in the same chapter: narrative scripture walked for narrative. Four lines were sealed before any count. Script `scripture/x1.py`.

2BG.2. The comparison set, and its weakness stated first. Matthew's use of the Septuagint in two lists: 42 explicit places (the formula quotations and the "it is written" places) and 43 tacit (allusions and built scenes commonly granted, from the star of Numbers 24:17 to the psalm at the cross). **Both lists are from my memory.** Each locus was then checked by machine against the seated Swete text. One instrument for both sources: surface words, accents stripped, short words and a stop list dropped, the first five letters compared. It is crude. It loses Hosea 11:1 at Matthew 2:15 and Psalm 21:2 at 27:46, where Matthew's Greek is not the Septuagint's, and 14 of the 43 tacit places fall under two shared stems. A lemmatized Septuagint is owed (already listed, section 6 item 7).

2BG.3. Order (X3, held). Matthew's uses of each book against their order in the book:

| Book | Uses | Rank correlation | p |
|---|---|---|---|
| Isaiah | 13 | −0.14 | 0.64 |
| Psalms | 14 | +0.01 | 0.99 |
| Exodus | 8 | +0.31 | 0.46 |
| Deuteronomy | 7 | +0.07 | 0.91 |
| Daniel | 5 | +0.40 | 0.51 |
| Revelation (2BD) | 149 | −0.09 | 0.13 |

Matthew does not walk scripture in order. Revelation's figure sits among the others. The test of order was built on the model of a sayings source followed in sequence, and Revelation is not handled as one.

2BG.4. Where the two sources meet (X4). Of the Matthew verses that use scripture, the share a Revelation row also reaches; the base rate over the Gospel is 28.6 per cent.

| Scripture used | Verses | Also reached from Revelation | p |
|---|---|---|---|
| openly, with formula or "it is written" | 45 | 10 (22 per cent) | 0.87 |
| tacitly | 38 | 21 (55 per cent) | < 0.001 |
| both lists | 83 | 31 (37 per cent) | 0.04 |

By passage: 8 of 42 explicit, 22 of 43 tacit. I sealed 35 to 50 per cent for the lists together and that held. The split I did not seal. Where Matthew names scripture, Revelation is at or under its base rate. Where Matthew uses scripture without naming it, Revelation is there at twice the base. The two are worked together in the unmarked places: Matthew 2:13–16, 3:17, 5:8, 5:34, 10:35, 14:16–20, 16:27, 18:16, 21:33, 24:29–31, 26:64, 28:18.

What this count does not separate. Revelation is itself made of scripture. At Matthew 24:30 and 26:64 the Son of Man on the clouds is Daniel 7:13, and Revelation 1:7 has it too; Matthew and Revelation could each have gone to Daniel. The count shows the two sources in the same verses and cannot say which way the words came. The rows that can are those where Revelation carries what the scripture lacks: Numbers has the star rising and Revelation 12:4 has it standing over the child (2BE.5); Kings has Jezebel and the letter to Thyatira has her children struck (2BC.7).

2BG.5. Words kept (X1). Shared stems per row, the same instrument on each:

| | Rows | Median | Mean | Sharing one stem or none |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Scripture, explicit | 37 | 4 | 5.3 | none (by the check) |
| Scripture, tacit, all listed | 43 | 2 | 2.2 | 33 per cent |
| Revelation, all rows | 158 | 1 | 2.1 | 51 per cent |
| Revelation, rows of one or two verses a side | 108 | 1 | 1.5 | 62 per cent |

I sealed a gap of one between tacit scripture and Revelation and the gap is one (p = 0.0004 on the means); both levels came in one lower than sealed. By words kept, Revelation is handled more loosely than the allusions commonly granted. Two things lean on that number from opposite sides: a list from memory holds the allusions that are known because they keep their words, and the ledger holds rows entered for their relation with one word or none in common. And Matthew's own formula quotations share a median of four stems with Swete: the open citations are themselves not close.

2BG.6. The moves (X2; a description, coded by one reader who was not blind). Tacit scripture against Revelation's rows, of those that fall under the four names: kept as the same kind of thing, 53 per cent and 45; a saying staged as an event, 26 and 20; a scene restaged with the bearer exchanged, 19 and 21; turned, 2 and 14. Three of the four sit close. The fourth does not: Matthew turns Revelation seven times as often as he turns tacit scripture. Where he turns scripture he does it in the open, with the formula, six times in a row (5:21–48: you have heard, but I say), and those are in the explicit list. Revelation is turned without notice.

2BG.7. The sentence, as counted tonight. Matthew handles Revelation as he handles the scripture he does not name. It is drawn on from anywhere and in no order; worked into the same verses as unnamed scripture at twice the base rate; kept, staged and re-cast in about the proportions scripture is. In two respects it is handled otherwise: fewer of its words are kept, and it is turned far more often. And it is never named. That last is where Revelation differs from scripture in Matthew, and the mechanics allow it: the book presents itself as his speech (Rev 1:1; the seven letters), and in a telling of his life his speech is spoken by him, not cited.

2BG.8. Owed, so that the count can bear weight: the two scripture lists taken from a published index of quotations and allusions in place of my memory (a commons copy sought first); a lemmatized Septuagint, so the instrument is the ledger's own; the moves coded by a second reader, blind to the source; the common-source case separated, by entering for each shared verse what Revelation has that the scripture lacks and the reverse; the same four counts for Luke.

## 2BH. Whether the reverse would be use or distortion: a count that does not decide

2BH.1. At 04:28 the author: "the reverse operation would not be revelation treating matthew like scripture. it would be sacreligious as compression, completely changing the meaning. not using it - distorting it." The argument is about direction and comes from the transforms: read left to right the rows are a writer using a scripture; read right to left they are a seer encoding a Gospel and changing its sense. I answered that Revelation's own handling of scripture would show it, since this writer keeps Ezekiel's and Daniel's words densely and does not turn them, and proposed the count. At 04:29: "yes lets run the count". Three lines were sealed. Script `scripture/y1.py`.

2BH.2. The baseline: sixty places where Revelation is granted to use scripture, from my memory, each checked in the seated Swete text with the instrument of 2BG.

| | Rows | Median stems shared | Mean | Sharing one or none |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Revelation using scripture | 60 | 2 | 2.27 | 35 per cent |
| Matthew using scripture tacitly (2BG) | 43 | 2 | 2.2 | 33 per cent |
| Revelation and Matthew, rows of one or two verses a side | 108 | 1 | 1.46 | 62 per cent |

2BH.3. The sealed lines. Y1 failed: I expected Revelation's scripture at a median of 4 and it is 2, the same as Matthew's. Y3 failed: the Revelation–Matthew rows stand as far from Revelation's practice as from Matthew's (0.81 and 0.74 of a stem). Y2 held as coded: none of Revelation's sixty is turned to an opposite sense. But Matthew's tacit scripture is turned once in 43, and the rows between the two books are turned one time in seven, so whichever book is the user turns the other far more than it turns scripture.

2BH.4. What this leaves. By words kept and by turning, the count is the same from either end. It does not carry the argument, and what I said of Revelation's dense keeping was wrong on this instrument. Two things are noted and neither is offered as a rescue. Revelation does not follow the Septuagint's wording (1:7 against Zechariah 12:10 shares a single stem), so a count against Swete understates how closely it keeps its sources. And the letters do make scripture's narrative into promise and charge (the manna, Balaam, Jezebel, the tree of life), which is the very move the reverse reading would need. The argument stands as a reading of particular rows: Peter's denial written as an assembly's praise, the guiding star written as the stars thrown down. A count that could test it must code each row for sense in both directions, by a reader who does not know the thesis. That is owed. The paper's 0.4, that every pairing can be read from right to left, is left as it stands.

## 2BI. What a turning does; who is speaking; the figure by stations

2BI.1. At 04:43 a sixth review came with the author's own words under it: "its just i read from philo and the lampstands and logos through revelation and the lampstands and jesus to the epistles and slavonic josephus hesitating on the boundary of calling the wonder worker a man, then doing it, to the gospels". The review says the count of 2BH measured words and turnings and did not measure what a turning does, and asks for three things kept apart in each row (what happens to the event, what happens to the judgment of it, what work the passage does on its source); for a count of whether the source speaker decides how material enters Matthew; for the infancy set against Exodus; and for a section of the paper on scriptural composition. Its figures were checked against 2BG and are right (22, 28.6 and 55.3 per cent). Its Philadelphia and Peter is the ledger's row from Rev 2:10, 2:13, 3:8. Three lines were sealed; one recode was done unsealed and is marked.

2BI.2. The figure by stations, as the author reads it. Philo: the Logos in the midst of the seven, an office and a position. Revelation: the one in the midst of the seven lampstands, named Jesus, who speaks and commissions writing. The epistles: that presence as the body and rule of assemblies. The Slavonic Josephus: a narrator who stops at the word man, "аще мужем достоино есть его нарещи", if it is fitting to call him a man; refuses it, "немощно ми есть нарещи его человѣком"; refuses angel, "не нареку и аггелом"; and then writes him as one man, with a habit, a mountain, servants, a governor and a cross (seated, EA-CORPORA-37; read in The Word That Became Text, #1765, where the same hesitation is set beside Philo, Conf. 62). The Gospels: a mother, a childhood, a land, companions, a death. Each station gives the figure something the one before did not have, and each is a text that exists. The table of 2E.6 set the Slavonic Josephus between Philo and Revelation, held under test; the order read here sets it after the epistles and before the Gospels, at the threshold of the person. Its station is still to be argued (2H.4), and nothing below rests on it. The received account reads the same series as a person progressively abstracted. Read in this order it is a figure progressively given a life, and the historical Jesus stands at the end of it.

2BI.3. What a turning does (a recode by me, with the review's thesis in mind; not blind; a description). The 21 rows the ledger names as turned or as failures, each asked two questions: is the event reversed, and is the source's judgment of it reversed.

| Row | Event | The source's judgment |
|---|---|---|
| Rev 3:8 etc. → Matt 26:35, 69–75 (denial) | fails | kept: not denying is the standard |
| Rev 14:4 → 26:56 (they follow; they flee) | fails | kept |
| Rev 14:4; 18:10 → 26:58 (from afar) | fails | kept |
| Rev 3:10 → 26:41 (kept from trial; pray not to enter) | promise made a petition | kept |
| Rev 3:17–18 → 19:21–23 (buy from me; sell and give) | act reversed | kept: the riches are not these |
| Rev 13:17 → 21:12 (the market) | reversed | kept: the beast's economy is cast out |
| Rev 13:17 → 13:44 (buy and sell) | reversed | kept |
| Rev 6:2 → 27:29 (the crown) | done in mockery | kept: he is the crowned one, said by mockers |
| Rev 8:7 → 3:10 (fire and trees) | direction reversed | kept: judgment by fire |
| Rev 16:9 → 9:3–8 (blasphemy; glory) | response reversed | kept |
| Rev 18:12–13 → 2:11 (Babylon's cargo; the gifts) | goods change hands | kept |
| Rev 13:3; 17:8 → 15:30–31 (healed; they marvel) | another does it | kept: the beast's wonder is the copy |
| Rev 13:2, 12 → 10:1 (authority given) | another does it | kept |
| Rev 2:13–14 → 16:23 (Satan; stumbling block) | said of a follower | kept |
| Rev 6:15; 19:18 → 17:25–26 (kings of the earth; free) | list made a question | kept |
| Rev 12:4 → 2:9 (what stands over the child) | bearer exchanged | kept: the child is to live |
| Rev 3:21 → 20:23 (to sit) | the giver changed | kept: the seat is granted |
| Rev 6:5 → 11:29–30 (ζυγός: balance, yoke) | the thing re-signed | not at issue |
| Rev 8:8 → 21:21 (the mountain into the sea) | plague made a promise | not at issue |
| Rev 18:11–12 → 13:45–46 (merchants and pearls) | reversed | **reversed**: the judged trader is the model |
| Rev 10:5–6 → 5:34 (the angel's oath) | forbidden | **reversed** |

Seventeen keep the source's judgment, two re-sign a thing without touching a judgment, two reverse it. Among the rows read, that is two of 158; Matthew's tacit scripture has one of 43 (Genesis 4:24, vengeance made forgiveness) and Revelation's scripture none of 60. The sentence of 2BG.6, that Matthew turns Revelation seven times as often as scripture, counted events and judgments together and does not stand: Matthew reverses what happens and keeps what it means. And one of the two reversals is the oath. Matthew 5:33–35 forbids the oath of the law by name ("you have heard that it was said"), cites Isaiah 66:1 for heaven the throne and earth the footstool, and in the same sentence forbids what the angel of Revelation 10:5–6 does, who lifts a hand to heaven and swears by the one who made heaven and earth. Revelation is turned at the place where scripture is turned openly, in the one breath.

What this does to direction: nothing by count. A judgment kept is kept from either end. The difference the review points to is in the third question, what the passage does: Matthew's side has an obligation taken, a test and an outcome, and the letter's side has a proposition. That difference is real and is the same from both ends too, since narrative has circumstances and a proposition has none. It becomes a test only where it predicts constituents not yet found (2BC.6; T2–T6 held there).

2BI.4. Who is speaking (Z1, held as sealed; Z2, failed). Revelation's verses in the figure's own voice were fixed (1:17–3:22; 16:15; 22:7, 12–13, 16, 20) and Matthew's verses spoken by Jesus were fixed by ranges written from memory before the count (60 per cent of the Gospel on that map).

| Revelation side | Rows | Land in Jesus's speech | |
|---|---|---|---|
| the figure's own voice | 38 | 30 | 79 per cent |
| vision, narration, other voices | 120 | 78 | 65 per cent |

Fourteen points in the direction the review proposes, p = 0.11: I sealed that it would lean so and not reach significance at this size, and it did. What the figure says in Revelation is mostly what Jesus says in Matthew. The eight that land elsewhere: three are followers tried (the kiss, the denial, the weeping), two the teacher tried (4:1; 4:9–10), then Herodias, the cities, the guards at the tomb. I sealed two thirds for followers tried and it is three of eight; with the teacher's own trial, five of eight are trials.

2BI.5. The infancy against Exodus and against Revelation 12 (Z3). Each verse of Matthew 2 against Exodus 1–4 and against Revelation 12, two shared stems or more. Exodus: fifteen verses, most of them noise of a thin stop list over ninety-seven verses, four of them not noise (2:13, 2:14, 2:20, 2:21), with 2:20 from Exodus 4:19 nearly whole: τεθνήκασιν γὰρ οἱ ζητοῦντες τὴν ψυχήν. Revelation 12: two verses, both common words. In words Exodus is the nearer text by far, as the received reading says. Revelation 12 holds the chapter by other means: the star standing over the child (2BE.5), the one who shall shepherd (12:5; 2:6, with Micah), the flight to a place made ready, and the sequence. One thing Exodus does not have is read here and not counted: Pharaoh's decree falls on all the sons, and Herod's on one child born king; the dragon waits for one child, who is to rule the nations. So the review's question has a first answer. Revelation supplements the scripture Matthew builds with: Exodus and the prophets give the words, and Revelation 12 gives the aimed threat, the star's standing and the order.

2BI.6. For the paper. The review asks that scriptural composition have a section of its own, operation by operation, acknowledged scripture beside Revelation, before the new rows go in. Adopted for v1.1; not written this round. The recode of 2BI.3 and the speaker table of 2BI.4 belong in it.

## 2BJ. John's signs against Revelation's plagues and seals: a reading

2BJ.1. The author, 27 November, 12:29: the prediction on record as "the plagues and the miracles" was made of John, whose signs are the ones for which a pre-existing signs document is supposed, and not of Matthew; the test that failed in the paper (XII) was a variant run on Matthew, and the prediction for John stands untested. He had worked it before and could not finish; that working was a handwritten notebook and is lost. What survives in the archive is one sentence of the Space Ark (#636, #639, #1101): "The seven signs in John mirror the seven seals." At 12:54: "lets examine johns signs and revelations curses and seals. a subjective examination looking at relationships first, not shooting blind." So this section reads first. I read the seven signs and the seals, trumpets and bowls from memory, wrote down what the reading saw (ten lines, sealed), and only then opened the texts and counted. Nothing here is entered in a ledger.

2BJ.2. The received reading. John's signs are seven by the usual count (Cana, the official's son, the pool, the feeding, the sea, the blind man, Lazarus), two of them numbered by the Gospel itself (2:11; 4:54). Since Bultmann they have been assigned to a signs source, a narrative of miracles taken up and commented on by the evangelist; Fortna reconstructed it. No such document is extant. (From memory; not read this round.)

2BJ.3. What Revelation calls a sign. Three things, before any sign of John is looked at.

- The seven last plagues are a sign. Καὶ εἶδον ἄλλο σημεῖον ἐν τῷ οὐρανῷ μέγα καὶ θαυμαστόν, ἀγγέλους ἑπτὰ ἔχοντας πληγὰς ἑπτὰ τὰς ἐσχάτας. The book has its own seven and names it σημεῖον. (Sealed that σημεῖον stands with πληγή only here; it stands also at 13:14, of the beast's wound. Both places are Revelation.)
- In Revelation the ones who do signs are the adversary's. ποιέω with σημεῖον: the second beast (13:13, 13:14), the spirits of demons (16:14), the false prophet (19:20). No sign is done by the Lamb or by the witnesses under that word. In John the phrase stands in fifteen verses and the doer is Jesus. Sealed, held. This is a move Matthew's ledger already has: the adversary's wonder done by the teacher.
- John begins by counting. Ταύτην ἐποίησεν ἀρχὴν τῶν σημείων.

2BJ.4. Sign by sign. Each line gives the relation as read, then what was counted.

| John | Revelation | The relation | Counted |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1. Cana, 2:1–11: they have no wine; water in the jars becomes wine and is given to drink | the third bowl, 16:4–6: the waters become blood, "you have given them blood to drink; they are worthy"; and the third seal, 6:6: "do not harm the oil and the wine" | water turned to a drink and given: as desert there, as gift here. The wine the dearth spares is the wine that runs out | αἷμα with πίνω is not at Cana. It is at John 6:53–56, in 1 Cor 11 and at Rev 16:6 only: the curse "blood to drink" has its counterpart in the bread discourse. Cana and the third bowl share ὕδωρ and the ordinal third. A reading |
| 2. The official's son, 4:46–54: the fever left him at the seventh hour; he believed, and his house | the fourth bowl, 16:8–9: people scorched with great heat; they blasphemed and did not repent | the burning comes and they do not turn; the burning leaves and they believe | no shared word but common ones (sealed so). A relation only |
| 3. The pool, 5:1–15: a multitude of the sick; an angel troubles the water (5:4, in the seated text; doubted in the tradition); "you are well; sin no more, lest worse befall you" | the first and fifth bowls, 16:2, 16:10–11: a sore on the people; they did not repent of their works; and 16:5, "the angel of the waters" | the sore sent; the sick made whole and told to stop sinning. An angel over water in both | not counted. A reading |
| 4. The feeding, 6:1–15: five barley loaves; two hundred denarii would not be enough; all are filled; twelve baskets over | the third seal, 6:5–6: the balance; "a measure of wheat for a denarius, three measures of barley for a denarius" | the dearth priced in barley and denarii; the plenty that barley and no denarius provide | **held.** Barley (κριθή, κρίθινος) stands in the New Testament at Rev 6:6 and John 6:9, 6:13 and nowhere else, with the denarius within two verses both times. The other three Gospels tell the feeding without barley |
| 5. The sea, 6:16–21: darkness, a great wind, he walks on the sea; "I am; do not fear" | the second bowl and second trumpet, 16:3; 8:8–9: the sea becomes blood, what lives in it dies, the ships are destroyed; 1:17, "Do not fear; I am the first and the last" | the sea that kills; the sea walked on, and the boat at once at land | a great wind stands in eight places (sealed four): failed. "I am; do not fear" is Mark's and Matthew's also |
| 6. The blind man, 9: clay put on the eyes; he washes and sees; "give glory to God"; "you say, We see: your sin remains" | Laodicea, 3:17–18: "you say, I am rich … and do not know that you are blind"; "eye-salve to anoint your eyes, that you may see"; and the fifth bowl, 16:10: the kingdom darkened | the counsel to the blind assembly done as a deed to one blind person, and the charge "you say …" kept for those who refuse | **held.** A compound of χρίω with the eye: Rev 3:18 ἐγχρῖσαι τοὺς ὀφθαλμούς σου ἵνα βλέπῃς; John 9:11 ἐπέχρισέν μου τοὺς ὀφθαλμούς (and 9:6 in Tischendorf, where the seated Nestle has ἐπέθηκεν). Neither compound stands anywhere else. "Give glory to God" is common (nine places) |
| 7. Lazarus, 11: four days dead; a loud voice, "come out"; the dead one comes out bound and is loosed; the council gathers: "this person does many signs" | the two witnesses, 11:11–12: after three days and a half the breath of life; they stand on their feet; a loud voice, "come up here"; their enemies watch. The sixth and seventh bowls, 16:13–17: spirits doing signs gather the kings; a loud voice, "It is done" | the dead raised by a loud voice before enemies; the signs that cause a gathering to war, and the gathering against the one who does them | συνάγω beside the doing of signs: John 11:47; Rev 16:14, 16; 19:19; and John 6:12–13 (the fragments gathered): failed by that one passage. The raising is a reading |

2BJ.5. What the reading finds.

- The author's prediction holds as a description of five of the seven: a plague on water, fire, the body, the sea and the light has opposite it a sign on the same thing. Two of these have counts behind them and both are exact: barley, and the anointed eye.
- The seals are in it as well as the bowls, as the sentence in the Space Ark said. One seal, the third, stands behind two signs: its spared wine at Cana and its barley and denarius at the feeding. The dearth is the thing undone.
- One sign comes from a letter. "I counsel you to anoint your eyes that you may see" is said to an assembly that says it is rich and is blind. John has it done, with clay, to one blind from birth, and keeps the letter's "you say" for the Pharisees: "you say, We see." This is the operation of the paper's section S.3, a saying staged as an event, performed by another Gospel on the same letter Matthew draws on for the door and the buying (IX.4a).
- What happens is reversed; what is judged is kept. The bowls end "and they did not repent to give him glory" (16:9, 16:11). The signs end in belief, or in a refusal that is charged as sin, and the refusers are the ones who say "give glory to God." The judgment that not turning is the fault is the same in both books. That is the finding of 2BI.3 for Matthew, found again.
- No order. The signs in John's order answer to bowls 3, 4, 1, the third seal, 2, 5, and 6–7. Only the end is in place: darkness, the gathering, the loud voice. As with Matthew, and as with scripture.
- Where it is thin: the fever and the pool rest on relation alone; the sea shares its best words with Mark and Matthew; and three of the ten sealed lines failed (a wind too common, one sign-and-plague verse I had not remembered, one gathering I had not foreseen).

2BJ.6. What this is and is not. It is a reading by one reader who knew the thesis, with five counts that held, three that failed and two that were readings from the start. It is no procedure for John. It says where one would begin: with the third seal and the letter to Laodicea, which give exact words; with the bowls taken as the sign Revelation says they are; and with the question the signs source was invented to answer, where a narrative of seven signs came from, put to a book that has a seven and calls it a sign.

2BJ.7. Added 27 November at a reviewer's question: are the signs the positive realization of the world Revelation promises, and not only the plagues undone. Counted after the question was read, unsealed; the pairs are well known. The thirsty told to come: διψάω with ἔρχομαι in one verse at John 6:35, 7:37 and Rev 22:17 only. Water with life: John 4:10, 4:11, 4:14, 7:38; Rev 7:17, 21:6, 22:1, 22:17; nowhere else. Never to hunger and never to thirst: John 6:35 and Rev 7:16 (the pair stands also in Matthew 5 and 25, 1 Cor 4:11 and Rom 12:20). These are in John's discourses and not in the signs. So the promise of Rev 7:16–17 and 21–22 is in John as speech, placed beside the signs that undo the dearth (the bread discourse after the barley; living water after Cana's wine and before the pool). The reading of 2BJ.5 gains a second half: the bowls reversed as deeds, the promises spoken as the speaker's own offer. A reading; the placing of discourse against sign is the thing to count next.

## 3. The fourfold

3.1. What the text fixes. The four creatures have an order (Rev 4:7): first like a lion, second like a calf, third with a face as of a human, fourth like an eagle flying. The four riders are called one by one by the four creatures (6:1–8): the second, third and fourth creature are numbered at the second, third and fourth seal; the first seal has "one of the four". So the pairing of creature and rider is given by the book, if "one of the four" is the first:

| | Creature (4:7) | Rider (6:1–8) | Given |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | lion | white horse, bow, a crown given, conquering | στέφανος |
| 2 | calf | red horse, takes peace from the earth | μάχαιρα μεγάλη |
| 3 | human face | black horse, a balance; a voice in the midst of the four prices grain | ζυγός |
| 4 | eagle flying | pale horse, named Death, Hades following | authority over a fourth of the earth |

3.2. The four names. I take these to be the four names of the rider on the white horse at 19:11–16: called Faithful and True; a name written that no one knows but himself; his name is called the Word of God; a name written, King of kings and Lord of lords. Two are called and two are written; one of the written is unknown. If the author means another four, this row changes and nothing else does. A candidate from Philo to hold beside it, not asserted: the high priest's plate bears "four engravings of a name" that only the purified may hear and speak (Vit. Mos. II.114, in #1758 §7b.3).

3.3. The four Gospels. No record assigns creature to Gospel. #65 says only that the four creatures "specify the fourfold structure the gospel tradition will take". The reception did assign them, and disagreed with itself: Irenaeus and Jerome give different pairings (from memory; Irenaeus, Adv. haer. 3.11.8, is seated, in `irenaeus-adversus-haereses`, though I wrote here that it was not; the passage was found and read at 2AO.1: lion John, calf Luke, human face Matthew, eagle Mark, and it should be: he is the earliest witness that reads the four Gospels off the four creatures, which is a reception trace for the arrow itself).

3.4. The test this sets up. Section 2's operators, run per Gospel, give each Gospel a profile: which seed regions it draws on, by which operations. The fourfold hypothesis predicts the profiles differ by rider and creature, and that the differences are the four of 3.1. One observation to start: the first rider is given a στέφανος; the second a μάχαιρα. Matthew alone has "not peace but a sword" (10:34, μάχαιραν), which #407 drew from Rev 1:16's ῥομφαία; the nearer seed is the second rider, who takes peace from the earth and is given a μάχαιρα. That would put Matthew with the calf, against both patristic assignments. One locus; not a result.

## 4. The epistles, and the seven within seven

4.1. What exists (hologram v1.5, §7.17): the seven letters mapped to seven epistolary positions by the slate of 2026-07-28. Ephesus, 1 John (John). Smyrna, James (James); run. Pergamum, 1 Corinthians 8–10 (Paul); adversarial. Thyatira, Philippians (the Lydia line). Sardis, 1 Thessalonians (the watchful hand). Philadelphia, unresolved. Laodicea, Colossians (Epaphras); the exchange recorded at Col 4:16.

4.2. What the census adds, each a pair found nowhere else in the New Testament and absent from the Septuagint:

- **1 Peter.** ἡ δόξα καὶ τὸ κράτος εἰς τοὺς αἰῶνας: Rev 1:6, 5:13; 1 Pet 4:11, 5:11. The Apocalypse's opening doxology is 1 Peter's closing one, twice. No record has this. Peter has no letter on the slate. Two different measures are in play and are kept apart: the exact four-word sequence is in Revelation and 1 Peter only; the formula family, a doxology ascribing κράτος, is wider (4.6: Jude, 1 Timothy).
- **Romans, from Thyatira.** πᾶσαι αἱ ἐκκλησίαι: Rev 2:23 ("all the churches shall know"), Rom 16:4 ("all the churches of the nations"). And from the same verse, ἑκάστῳ κατὰ τὰ ἔργα: Rev 2:23, Rom 2:6 (in LXX). Two Roman loci from one verse of the Thyatira letter. The slate sends Thyatira to Philippians at B−, through Lydia. Romans is a rival target with lexical anchors.
- **James 5:12**, the oath: μήτε τὸν οὐρανὸν μήτε τὴν γῆν μήτε…; Rev 7:3, μὴ ἀδικήσητε τὴν γῆν μήτε τὴν θάλασσαν μήτε… The James derivation lists 5:12 as residue, "other-tradition candidate". The census gives it a seed outside the Smyrna letter. If it holds, James draws on more of Revelation than Smyrna, and the residue ledger shrinks from a different chapter.

4.3. The two sevens, laid side by side, unassigned.

- Seven churches written to by Paul, in the Muratorian fragment's order as seated (Tregelles 1867): "paulus sequens prodecessoris sui Iohannis ordine nonnisi nominatim septem ecclesiis scribat ordine tali": Corinthians first, Ephesians second, Philippians third, Colossians fourth, Galatians fifth, Thessalonians sixth, Romans seventh (the seat's spelling is the manuscript's; "nominatim septem ecclesiis" is the editors' reading of "eumenats semptae eccleses"). This is the first seven: a corpus shaped on a corpus.
- Seven letters under other names: James; 1 and 2 Peter; 1, 2 and 3 John; Jude. Seven letters, four names.
- Named positions: Paul, James, Peter, John, Jude. Five. Hebrews has no name. The slate's own positions add Epaphras, Lydia and an unnamed "watchful hand".

4.4. **Left open, at the author's word**: "lets leave it open for paul and supposedly non pauline until were further into the mapping." No assignment of positions is made here. What is recorded is what would bear on it when it is made.

4.5. **Hebrews counts** (the author). It enters as a position. Four arrows of the hologram already land on it (M3, M4, the arrow from the opening scene to 4:8–14, Philadelphia's 13:1); the stem instrument adds the furniture of Heb 9:2–4.
