4.6. **A mark that divides the letters: the doxology with κράτος.** "To him be … κράτος" is ascribed six times in the New Testament: Rev 1:6; 5:13; 1 Pet 4:11; 5:11; Jude 25; 1 Tim 6:16. It is in none of the seven letters to the Muratorian churches; their doxologies have δόξα alone. So one formula of the Apocalypse's first chapter runs through Peter, Jude and a Pastoral and stops at the Pauline seven. Counted from the seat: the form κράτος stands four more times outside a doxology (Luke 1:51; Acts 19:20; Col 1:11; Heb 2:14), and the genitive at Eph 1:19 and 6:10, neither a doxology. This is the first datum that sorts the letters into two groups by something Revelation holds, which is what the seven within seven needs before any position is assigned. With it: Michael at Jude 9 and Rev 12:7 only.

4.7. **The two sevens sorted by slot: a reading, then counts** (27 November 2026). The author, 13:06: "id like to look at the sevenfold mapping of pauls letters as against the broader mapping of epistolary positions. here is my thought: the mappings within paul will be a fractal of the mappings across positions, with paul as one of the positions, the part containing the whole." 4.4 stands: no assignment is made. What follows is a reading set down under seal before anything was counted, and what the counts did to it.

4.7.1. The received reading. The Muratorian list says Paul wrote to seven churches by name "following the order of his predecessor John", and that John in the Apocalypse, writing to seven, speaks to all ("tamen omnibus dicit" as seated, Tregelles 1867; the words before it are damaged in the seat's text and are given here from memory as "licet septem ecclesiis scribat"). The list itself states the rule of the part that holds the whole, of John, and makes Paul's seven its copy. Scholarship takes the sentence as a late second-century argument for catholicity, with the dependence of Paul on John an anachronism. Nobody maps Paul's seven churches onto Revelation's seven one by one, and the catholic letters are treated as a separate collection formed later.

4.7.2. The slate of 4.1 mixes two levels. James and 1 John are positions (a name with its letters). Colossians, 1 Corinthians, 1 Thessalonians, Philippians are churches inside one position. Sorted, the reading is two columns against one:

| Revelation's letter | Outer: a position | Inner: a church of Paul |
|---|---|---|
| Ephesus | John | Ephesians |
| Smyrna | James | Philippians |
| Pergamum | Jude | Corinthians |
| Thyatira | Peter | Romans |
| Sardis | the letters to persons | Thessalonians |
| Philadelphia | Hebrews | Galatians |
| Laodicea | Paul | Colossians |

4.7.3. What makes it a fractal and not two lists: one hook of a Revelation letter found at both levels. Counted over the tagged New Testament:

- **Thyatira, twice.** "I will give to each of you according to your works" (Rev 2:23): ἕκαστος, ἔργον and κατά in one verse stand in the epistles at Rom 2:6 and 1 Pet 1:17 and nowhere else (also Rev 20:13). "I gave her time to repent" (Rev 2:21): repentance with time or patience within a verse in five places, Rev 2:21, 2:22, Acts 17:30, Rom 2:4, 2 Pet 3:9. One verse of Thyatira lands in Romans 2 and 1 Peter 1; the next lands in Romans 2 and 2 Peter 3. With them, "the one who searches hearts": the verb with καρδία only at Rev 2:23 and Rom 8:27. The Septuagint holds the first (4.2; the places, Ps 61:13 and Prov 24:12, from memory): a three-way row.
- **Philadelphia, once.** Jerusalem called new, above or heavenly: Rev 3:12, 21:2; Gal 4:26; Heb 12:22; nowhere else. The pillar (στῦλος): Rev 3:12, 10:1; Gal 2:9; 1 Tim 3:15. φιλαδελφία at Heb 13:1 is one of five and decides nothing.
- **Pergamum, by the episode and not the word.** Balaam: Rev 2:14, Jude 11, 2 Pet 2:15 only. Corinthians has no Balaam; it has the thing Balaam taught, food offered to idols (εἰδωλόθυτον, shared) and fornication (πορνεύω, shared; 1 Cor 10:8 is the same day in Numbers 25, said from memory). The outer hook is split between Jude and 2 Peter, which are one text twice.
- **Smyrna.** Outer: the run of record (James), crown of life. Inner: "unto death", θάνατος under ἄχρι or μέχρι in five places, Rev 2:10, 12:11, Phil 2:8, 2:30, Acts 22:4. Two different hooks; no one hook at both levels.
- **Ephesus.** Outer: testing the false, πειράζω or δοκιμάζω with a ψευδ- word in one verse only at Rev 2:2 and 1 John 4:1. Inner: the name.
- **Sardis.** Inner: the thief and the watch. Outer: nothing found for the letters to persons; the thief's other epistolary place is 2 Pet 3:10, Peter's.
- **Laodicea.** Inner: the name, and the letter that orders itself exchanged with Laodicea's (Col 4:16). Outer: the position is Paul, and Col 4:16 is the one sentence in the seven churches where a letter to one is ordered read by another. The part says of itself what the Muratorian list says of the whole.

4.7.4. Three counts that could fail, sealed first. The instrument: content lemmas of each Revelation letter standing in 40 or fewer verses outside Revelation, shared with each group of epistles, per 1,000 words of the group.

| Sealed | Result |
|---|---|
| Inner: the set church first or second of seven in 4 slots or more | **Failed: 2 of 7** (Sardis and Laodicea first; Smyrna, Thyatira, Philadelphia third; Ephesus fourth; Pergamum sixth) |
| Outer: the set position first or second of seven in 4 or more | **Failed: 2 of 7** (Pergamum first, Smyrna second; Thyatira and Philadelphia third; Ephesus, Sardis, Laodicea sixth) |
| Inner church against its own-slot outer position, highest of six in 2 or more | 2 of 6 (Romans with Peter, Thessalonians with the letters to persons), **and uninformative**: Peter or the letters to persons stand first for every church |

The measure follows length. The short groups stand first in nearly every row (Thessalonians and Colossians inside Paul; Jude and James outside). It cannot see what the hooks see. What survives it: Thessalonians for Sardis and Colossians for Laodicea stand first among Paul's churches, and Jude for Pergamum first among the positions.

4.7.5. Standing. The thought holds as a reading at two slots by the test it sets itself (one hook, both levels: Thyatira with Romans and Peter; Philadelphia with Galatians and Hebrews), at a third by episode (Pergamum), and is stated by the Muratorian list as its own principle. It does not hold by gross vocabulary. Two slots of the reading are wrong or empty as set: the outer Sardis, and the inner Ephesus beyond its name. The hooks were known before the seal, so their return shows the reading was remembered correctly and no more. Owed: a measure that does not follow length (a hook count taken blind over every letter-to-epistle pair, with the rate at which any two epistles share a hook by chance), and the Muratorian order (Corinthians first, Romans seventh) against Revelation's.

4.8. **The epistles as a layer: a joined crossing.** The author, 13:08: "we need to experiment with adding the epistles as a layer. for example, take a place where there is a conjoined crossing, a passage in matthew that appears to draw on both an epistle and revelation, see how it works and what it opens up." And 13:11: "or vice versa, if an epistle passage can be mapped as a transform into matthew... and the epistolary passage understood as a mapping from revelation. so two experimental hypothesized transforms to test: epistolary and revelation material jointly in matthew, and a transform circuit from revelation to epistle to matthew (or other gospel)."

4.8.1. A test that tells the two shapes apart, fixed before the passages were run. Take the content lemmas of the Matthew passage (M), the epistle passage (E), the Revelation passage (R). A circuit Revelation, epistle, Matthew requires that Matthew hold nothing of Revelation the epistle lacks: M∩R inside E. A joined crossing shows the opposite: Matthew holds words of each that the other lacks.

4.8.2. **Matt 16:17–19**, with Gal 1:15–16, 2:9 and Rev 1:18, 3:7–12. The received reading: the blessing of Peter is Matthew's own, added to Mark's confession; the likeness of 16:17 to Gal 1:16 has long been noticed and is read as Matthew answering Paul or as a shared way of speaking of revelation; the keys go back to Isa 22:22, which Rev 3:7 quotes. The ledger already holds Rev 1:18 to Matt 16:18–19 (keys, Hades; the holder exchanged, speaker for follower) and Rev 20:1–3 to 16:19.

| | Lemmas |
|---|---|
| Matthew with Galatians, not Revelation | σάρξ, αἷμα, ἀποκαλύπτω |
| Matthew with Revelation, not Galatians | κλείς, ᾅδης, ἐκκλησία, οὐρανός, γῆ |
| Galatians with Revelation, not Matthew | στῦλος |

Sealed and held: flesh, blood and revealing within a verse stand together only at Matt 16:17 and Gal 1:16; the key with Hades only at Matt 16:19 and Rev 1:18. The shape is joined. Verse 17 is Galatians; verses 18–19 are Revelation; the seam is between them.

4.8.3. How it works. One operator, applied to both sources in adjoining verses. Paul says of himself that the Son was revealed in him and he did not consult flesh and blood (Gal 1:16). Revelation's speaker says of himself that he holds the keys of death and Hades (Rev 1:18). Matthew moves both to Peter: flesh and blood did not reveal it to you; I will give you the keys. The ledger's name for the second is EXCHANGE the holder. The first is the same exchange made on an epistle. And the third term, which Matthew does not keep as a word: Galatians calls Cephas a pillar (2:9), Revelation promises the one who conquers that he will be made a pillar with a new name written on him (3:12), and Matthew gives Simon a new name and builds on him. ὄνομα with καινός stands in one verse three times (Rev 2:17, 3:12; Mark 16:17, which is new tongues spoken in a name); a new name is given only in the two, and Rev 2:17 writes it on a stone.

4.8.4. What it opens. Revelation has a speaker and almost no followers with names. The epistles are where the names are: Cephas, James, John, Paul. When the speaker is retyped as a teacher with a past, he needs a cast, and at this crossing the cast comes from the epistle while the powers come from Revelation. That is a claim with a test: among the ledger's rows that exchange the holder from speaker to a named follower, an epistle should supply the follower's name and a sentence about him more often than among the other rows. Not run. Two candidate rows, not entered: Rev 2:17 and 3:12 (the new name, on a stone, on a pillar) to Matt 16:18; and the thief, below, which the ledger does not hold.

4.9. **The circuit.** Three passages by the test of 4.8.1.

4.9.1. **The oath: Rev 10:5–6, Jas 5:12, Matt 5:33–37.** Sealed and held: every content lemma Matthew shares with Revelation's oath (ὀμνύω, οὐρανός, γῆ) stands in James; James and Matthew add ὅρκος; nothing joins James to Revelation past Matthew or Matthew to Revelation past James. The chain is not excluded. The count cannot order James and Matthew. The form can be read: the angel swears by the maker of heaven, earth and sea, three, each with what is in it; James forbids heaven, earth, and any other oath, three, bare; Matthew forbids heaven, earth, Jerusalem and the head, four, each with a reason. James stands between in form: Revelation's count of three, Matthew's prohibition. The received reading already holds that James has the saying in an earlier form than Matthew (from memory; to be checked against the commentaries). Standing: the one circuit candidate; read, not shown.

4.9.2. **The genealogy** (the author's example). Sealed and held: the tribe with Benjamin stands in four verses, Rev 7:8, Rom 11:1, Phil 3:5, Acts 13:21, and outside Revelation every one is in Paul's mouth. The tribe with Judah in three: Rev 5:5, 7:5, Heb 7:14. Revelation's sealed list begins with Judah and ends with Benjamin. Matthew 1 has no φυλή at all. So no chain runs through Paul's account of his descent into Matthew's. The shape is a fork from one list: Paul takes its last tribe for himself, Hebrews and Matthew its first for Jesus. What Matthew 1:1 holds that Revelation lacks is Abraham, whom Revelation never names; "son of David" has Rom 1:3 and Rev 5:5, 22:16 behind it, "son of Abraham" has only the epistle (Gal 3:16; Rom 11:1). Matt 1:1 is a joined crossing in five words.

4.9.3. **The thief: Rev 3:3, 16:15; 1 Thess 5:1–6; Matt 24:42–44.** Sealed and held: Matthew shares with Revelation a lemma 1 Thessalonians lacks (ὥρα), so no clean chain. And the join shows as a doublet. Matt 24:42: "you do not know on what day your Lord comes" (ἡμέρᾳ, κύριος: 1 Thess 5:2, "the day of the Lord comes as a thief"). Matt 24:44: "at an hour you do not expect" (ὥρᾳ: Rev 3:3, "you will not know at what hour I will come upon you"). Between them the thief. Matthew says it twice, once in each source's noun.

4.9.4. What the three show. Where the count can decide, the shape is joined: Matthew goes to Revelation and to the epistle each directly, and the seam can fall between verses (16:17 | 16:18) or make a doublet (24:42, 24:44). The one circuit candidate is the oath, where the epistle is James and the count is silent on order. Four passages chosen by eye are four passages. Owed: the test of 4.8.1 run over every ledger row that has an epistle parallel, with the passages chosen by rule.

4.10. **The layer run over the whole ledger: two instruments, both failed; five passages read.** The author, 13:34: "lets run it... im imagining revelation producing epistles and then gospels as a bipgraphicalizing fusion of both."

4.10.1. The first rule (sealed before the run): for each row, the three-verse window in any of the 21 letters sharing the greatest weight of rare content lemmas with the row's Matthew verses, counted as a parallel at two shared lemmas with one in 40 verses or fewer. Sealed: 25–45% of rows would have a parallel. Result: 107 of 158 (68%). The rule returns Gal 5:9 for the vineyard. It finds a window for most spans of any length. Under it 76% of the parallels are joined and 16 rows do not exclude a chain; neither number means anything.

4.10.2. The second rule (made after the first failed, sealed before its run): a parallel requires a pair of shared rare lemmas that stand together in three or fewer chapters of the New Testament, Mark and Luke left out. Sealed: 8 to 30 rows. Result: 105 of 158. Most pairs of uncommon words stand together in few chapters, so the rule excludes almost nothing; and it does not find Jas 5:12 for the oath, since to swear and heaven stand together too widely. An instrument that passes two rows in three and misses the best-known parallel in the set cannot see the thing.

4.10.3. Verse by verse through Matthew, the one comparison with a base rate. First rule: verses reached from Revelation have an epistle parallel at 39.3% (120 of 305), the rest at 34.5% (263 of 762), ratio 1.14. Second rule: 41.3% against 34.3%, ratio 1.21. Sealed at 1.3 or more both times: **failed both times.** Reached verses also carry more rare lemmas (5.4 a verse against 4.58), which may be the whole of the difference. Inside discourse chapters 47% against 43%; inside narrative 35% against 30%. Epistle likeness, as these rules measure it, does not gather where Revelation reaches. It is spread through Matthew at about one verse in three.

4.10.4. What the lists put in front of a reader. Five passages, picked from the run's output after it ran, then counted by the test of 4.8.1. They were not sealed and are a reading.

| Matthew | Epistle | Revelation | With Revelation, not the epistle | With the epistle, not Revelation |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 17:1–8, the mountain | 2 Pet 1:16–18 | 1:13–17 | λευκός, πίπτω, φοβέω, ἥλιος | ἀγαπητός, εὐδοκέω, ἀκούω, ὄρος |
| 10:1–4, the list | 1 Cor 15:5–9 | 21:12–14 | ὄνομα | Ἰάκωβος, ἀδελφός |
| 19:28, the thrones | 1 Cor 6:2–3 | 3:21; 20:4 | θρόνος, καθίζω | κρίνω |
| 23:34–35, the blood | Heb 11:4; 12:24 | 6:9–10; 16:6; 18:24 | θυσιαστήριον, προφήτης, ἐκχέω, γῆ | Ἄβελ, δίκαιος |
| 26:38–45, the garden | 1 Thess 5:5–10 | 3:2–3; 16:15 | ὥρα, εὑρίσκω | καθεύδω |

All five are joined. Counted beside them: the face with the sun in one verse stands at Matt 17:2, Rev 10:1 and Jas 1:11 only; the voice with "beloved" and "son" in the Gospels and at 2 Pet 1:17; the twelve with "apostle" in one verse at Matt 10:2, Luke 6:13 and Rev 21:14 only; Abel at Matt 23:35, Luke 11:51, Heb 11:4 and 12:24 only; watching with sleeping within a verse in the Gospels' garden and watch sayings and at 1 Thess 5:6, 5:10.

4.10.5. What the five do, read against the author's sentence. In each, Revelation gives the sight or the power and the epistle gives what a life needs.

- The mountain. Revelation: a face like the sun, the seer falls, a hand, "do not fear" (the ledger's row). 2 Peter: a mountain, a voice heard, "my beloved son, in whom I am well pleased", and a named witness who says "we were with him". Matthew 17 is the two laid together, and the witness becomes a character.
- The list. Revelation: twelve names of twelve apostles, written on foundations and not given. 1 Corinthians: Cephas, then the twelve, then James, then all the apostles. Matthew: "the names of the twelve apostles are these: first Simon called Peter". Revelation supplies that there are names; the epistle supplies some; Matthew writes the list out, in the epistle's order of first.
- The thrones. Revelation: the one who conquers sits with the speaker on his throne. 1 Corinthians: the saints will judge the world. Matthew: you will sit on twelve thrones judging the twelve tribes.
- The blood. Revelation: the blood of prophets poured out, the souls under the altar. Hebrews: Abel the righteous, whose blood speaks. Matthew: all the righteous blood poured out on the earth, from Abel to the one killed by the altar.
- The garden. Revelation: watch, or you will not know the hour. 1 Thessalonians: let us not sleep as the rest do; let us watch. Matthew: three followers asleep, told to watch, and "the hour has come". An exhortation becomes an event with people in it.

That is the fusion as the author put it, seen five times: a vision's content and a letter's sentence about persons, turned into something that happened to someone on a day. It is seen in five passages a reader chose. No count here shows it.

4.10.6. Standing and what is owed. The hypothesis of the layer is read, at Matt 16:17–19 (4.8) and these five; it is not shown at the scale of the ledger, and two rules built to show it failed. Owed: an instrument that can see it. The hooks that work in this file are all of one kind, a construction found in a handful of places in the whole New Testament; the rule should be that, taken as a census (every construction of two or three rare lemmas within a verse that stands in Matthew, in one epistle, and in five or fewer places in all), with the Gospels' own parallels to Matthew removed first and the count made against the same census for Mark's verses as the control.

4.11. **The order of forms.** The author, 13:35: "it just doesn't work. an intra nt narrative of beginning with nauve, crufty biography and evolving towards the abstract and visionary simply contradicts *everything* we know about second temple literature."

4.11.1. The received sequence, in full. Sayings and stories of a teacher are passed on by mouth; Paul's letters are written in the fifties, with little of the life in them; Mark writes the first Gospel about 70; Matthew and Luke follow, with a sayings source; John later; Revelation about 95, a late and separate work by a seer in Asia. The same account holds that the movement was apocalyptic from its first day. So the received order of surviving books is letters, lives, vision, with an unwritten biographical tradition set underneath all three as the root.

4.11.2. What the period's writing shows (from memory, to be checked against the seats and the handbooks before it is used anywhere). The vision and the heavenly journey are its working forms: Daniel, the books of Enoch, Jubilees, the scrolls, and after 70 the apocalypses of Ezra and Baruch. Letters are written and collected (the letters that open 2 Maccabees, the Epistle of Enoch, the halakhic letter among the scrolls). A narrative life of a teacher of one's own time is not among its forms: the Teacher of Righteousness has no life written; the lives that exist are in Greek dress and of other subjects (Philo on Moses, Josephus on himself).

4.11.3. Where the point bites. Corrected at the author's word, 13:42: "the received account puts *two separate* unexplained genre innovations first, separately. tbe epistolary form; and then as a formally unrelated later innovation, biographical tradition with no surviving text. two. not one. both of which require their own imaginary texts to explain." This paragraph as first written counted one: it said the received account sets an unwritten biographical tradition first and supplies it with posited documents. That undercounted. The received account has two inventions, made apart and unrelated in form, and each has its own shelf of texts that do not survive.

- The letter of an apostle to an assembly, in the fifties. Behind it the account sets hymns and creeds older than Paul and quoted by him, collections of proof-texts, and a body of teaching handed on, none of it extant apart from the letters said to quote it.
- The told life, from about 70. Behind it: oral tradition, the sayings source, the special sources of Matthew and of Luke, a signs source for John, a passion narrative older than Mark.

(Both lists are from memory and are to be checked against the handbooks before use.) Neither form is derived from the other in that account, and neither from the vision, which is dated last. The author's order derives both from a book that exists: the letter from the book that opens and closes as one and holds seven (4.11.5), the life from that book and the letters together (4.8, 4.10). It needs no text that is not extant. The last step, a life in a Greek host, is T_Μ's own second parameter.

4.11.4. Standing. An argument from which forms were in use, the author's. It ranks two orders by what each has to assume and shows nothing about any one verse. Its place is with the paper's P sections (P.1–P.11: the literary-genetic argument, the prevention, the burden on the supposed document), where it is not yet made. The paper stands deposited at v1.1; it is not touched here.

4.11.5. The author, 13:36: "tne epistles are formal genre innovations, the gospels are formal genre innovations, revelation is not, and it MUST be explained." Stated as a burden: the letter of an apostle to an assembly, read as scripture, is a new form; the Gospel is a new form; the apocalypse is an old one. An account of these books owes an explanation of the two new forms, and an order that puts the two inventions first and the inherited form last explains neither. One count belongs here. Revelation is itself framed as a letter and holds seven letters to assemblies inside it. "Grace to you and peace from" (χάρις, εἰρήνη and ἀπό in one verse) stands in 13 verses of the New Testament: the greeting of twelve letters under Paul's name, and Rev 1:4. A book whose last verse is "grace ... with" you or all: 13 books, eleven under Paul's name, Hebrews, and Revelation (22:21). So the new form's frame stands whole, opening and close, on the one book in the old form, addressed to seven assemblies; and the letters that share that greeting are Paul's and no one else's (1 and 2 Peter and 2 John have grace and peace without the "from"). Read with 4.7: the book that is no innovation carries the letter inside it, to seven, and the first collection of the innovation is a seven that the Muratorian list says follows it. What the count cannot say is which way the greeting travelled. Standing: the author's argument, with one count beside it.

4.12. **A seventh review, checked, and Peter's sequence as three stations** (27 November, 13:52).

4.12.1. The review, claim by claim, against the file and the tagged text.

| The review says | Checked | Standing |
|---|---|---|
| The epistles give the figure a human social world, which Revelation largely lacks; the layer has a function and is no second corpus to search | Revelation names John and Antipas among followers and no other. ἐκκλησία stands in the four Gospels at Matt 16:18 and 18:17 only (counted) | Taken. It is 4.8.4 said better |
| At Matt 16:17–19 one operation is performed on two sources in adjoining verses; the biography redistributes the properties of both onto its characters | 4.8.2–4.8.3, counted there | Taken; extended below (the foundation) |
| The epistles supply relations among persons and plot, and the search should go through conflicts of authority, relations among named followers, practices, failures of conduct, testimony turned into event | Run below on Peter | Taken as the rule of selection |
| At the transfiguration testimony has become narrative | 4.10.4. The received reading has 2 Peter recall the scene from the Gospels; that is a reading to be accounted for and no premise here (4.13). The count does not order them. See 4.12.4 | An operator named; direction to be established from the texts |
| The old genre already holds the formal resources of both innovations | 4.11.5 for the letter (counted). For the life it is the paper's own thesis | Taken |
| John: what is transferred is miraculous agency itself; do the signs realize the world the Apocalypse promises | 2BJ.3 and 2BJ.5 hold the first (the fifth point of 2BJ.5 already says what happens is reversed and what is judged is kept). The second is new: 2BJ.7 | The first was in the file; the second is added |
| Do not complete the sevenfold assignment; define a position independently of the seven; look for the same operations recurring inside Paul and across the letters | 4.7.4–4.7.5 agree on the first. The second and third are owed | Taken as the next step for 4.7 |
| The automated searches measured lexical nearness; measure successive innovation | 4.10.6 says the instrument failed. The object proposed here is better than the census proposed there | Taken; done by hand below |

Nothing in the review is contradicted by the file. As first written this paragraph added that the received dating of each epistle "matters to direction, and 2 Peter above all". Withdrawn at the author's word (4.13): a received date is not evidence of direction in this investigation.

4.12.2. The object measured. For a passage of Matthew: an innovation peculiar to Revelation that Matthew keeps (R); a further one peculiar to an epistle (E); a relation that exists only in their fusion (M). And for the first leg, whether the epistle's item can itself be derived from Revelation (R to E). Peter is the follower chosen, because Galatians 1–2 gives Cephas a career. Ten lines were sealed from a reading before any count.

4.12.3. Peter, station by station.

| Matthew | R kept | E kept | Made by the fusion | Counted |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 10:2–6, the list and the sending | twelve apostles, names, set on a wall whose gates are the twelve tribes of Israel (Rev 21:12–14) | Cephas first, then the twelve (1 Cor 15:5); Peter's apostleship is to the circumcision (Gal 2:7–8) | "the names of the twelve apostles: first Simon"; go only to the house of Israel | δώδεκα, ἀπόστολος and Ἰσραήλ within five verses: Matt 10:2 and Rev 21:14 only. **Held** |
| 14:28–31, on the water (Matthew alone) | standing on the sea (Rev 15:2; 10:5; the ledger's row) | the doubter is a wave of the sea driven by wind (Jas 1:6); Cephas afraid, drawing back (Gal 2:12) | a named follower stands on the sea, sees the wind, fears, doubts, sinks | a doubt word with sea and wind within one verse: Jas 1:6 only. **Failed**: Matthew's sea is five verses before its doubt. φοβέω is shared with Gal 2:12 |

Mark 8:33 has the rebuke without the stumbling-block; the word Matthew has and Mark lacks is the Pergamum letter's |
| 17:24–27, the tax (Matthew alone) | "the kings of the earth" (eight times in Revelation) | pay τέλος to whom τέλος is due (Rom 13:7); free, yet giving no offence (1 Cor 8–9; 1 Pet 2:16, from memory) | the sons are free, and Peter pays for both | τέλος with κῆνσος or φόρος within a verse: Matt 17:25 and Rom 13:7 only. "Kings of the earth" outside Revelation: Matt 17:25 and Acts 4:26 only. **Both held.** Ps 2:2 has the phrase: a three-way row |
| 26:70, the denial | "you did not deny my faith" (Rev 2:13; the ledger's row) | "I said to Cephas before all" (Gal 2:14) | Peter denies before all | ἔμπροσθεν πάντων: Matt 26:70, Gal 2:14, Mark 2:12 only. **Held.** Mark's denial (14:68) lacks it; Matthew has it |

Nine of ten sealed lines held. The one that failed is Matthew's own scene, where the words are there and too far apart for the window set.

4.12.4. What the stations show.

- **The first leg has words at one place.** Rev 21:14 puts the apostles' names on foundations. Eph 2:20 says the assembly is built on the foundation of the apostles. Those are the only two verses of the New Testament with both words. Matthew has the building, the assembly and a named apostle under it, and calls the foundation a rock, as Matthew's own parable does (7:25). So for 16:18 the form asked for is filled: Revelation to the epistle (foundations named for apostles; built on the foundation of the apostles), then both to Matthew (this one, named, and on him I will build). Paul's own letters say the foundation and the rock are Christ; Matthew gives them to Peter. The holder is exchanged a third time in three verses.
- **Twice Matthew has, where Mark lacks it, exactly the word of the outside text.** At 16:23 the stumbling-block, which stands with Satan only in the Pergamum letter and at the end of Romans. At 26:70 "before all", which stands in Galatians at the rebuke of Cephas. The received account calls both Matthew's additions to Mark; this bullet as first written said "adds" too, which takes the order of the Gospels as given (4.13, 4.15). What is seen is that the outside text's word is in Matthew's scene and absent from Mark's.
- **The Pergamum letter is behind two stations of one follower.** Satan and the stumbling-block (2:13–14) at the rebuke; "you did not deny" (2:13) at the denial. The letter praises an assembly for not denying where Satan dwells; Matthew's Peter is called Satan and then denies. A turned row, on a named person.
- **Galatians' order is not Matthew's.** Sealed so before counting: the stations of Gal 1:16, 2:7–9, 2:11, 2:12–14 fall at rising places in Matthew only if 10:2–6 and 14:30 are set aside. No walk is claimed.
- **Testimony into event.** At the mountain, what Matthew has beyond 2 Pet 1:16–18 divides in two. Part of it Revelation holds: the face as the sun, white, falling, fear (1:13–17); a cloud and a voice from heaven over two witnesses (11:12); the tent of God with people (21:3). Part neither holds: Moses and Elijah by name, six days, the three taken up, the touch. What 2 Peter has that Matthew lacks is its own diction (majesty, honour and glory, the holy mountain, "we were with him"). The count orders nothing. The received account reads 2 Peter as recalling the Gospels' scene; the order proposed here reads the scene as 2 Peter's testimony staged with Revelation's sight. Which it is has to be settled by a test of direction on these texts (4.13), and none has been run.

4.12.5. Standing. A reading with ten sealed lines, nine held, on passages chosen by the review's rule (a named follower acquiring or exercising authority) and then by one reader's eye inside that rule. It shows the three-station form filled at 16:18 and two Matthean additions to Mark that are the outside text's words. It does not show direction. Candidate rows, not entered: Rev 21:12–14 to Matt 10:2–6 with Israel; Rev 21:14 with 3:12 and 2:17 to Matt 16:18. Owed: the same for a second follower (the sons of Zebedee and the thrones; Judas), and the control, which is the same sealed lines run against Mark's Peter.

4.13. **No received date is a premise.** The author, 14:07: "we must never rely in any established dating in this investigation. the priors of the *entire* editice of the established chronology are *fucked* - they *cannot* be relied upon, and even where specific inference, reasoning, and relations are sound, the *entire* inferential take away must be reestablished."

4.13.1. The rule, as it binds this file. The received chronology is built on an order of the books, and that order is the thing under test. A date drawn from it cannot be used to weigh a direction here; to do so assumes the answer. So: (a) the received reading is still stated first and in full, as a claim to be accounted for; (b) no sentence of this file may say a direction is less likely because a text is dated late, or more likely because one is dated early; (c) where the received account rests on an observation that is sound in itself (a manuscript exists by a certain time; one writer quotes another; one form of a saying is shorter), the observation is kept as an observation and what follows from it is worked out again from the start, without the order it was first fitted to.

4.13.2. What may bear on order, each to be re-derived and none yet done here: tests of direction on the texts themselves (the operations of the paper's section S and of 2BH, 2BI: which text keeps, stages, restages, conflates, turns the other); the three-station form of 4.12.2, where a later text holds two earlier ones' separate innovations and a relation neither has; physical attestation (a papyrus, a quotation by a writer who can be placed), which gives a latest date for a text's existence and nothing about which of two texts came first.

4.13.3. Where this file broke the rule before it was stated, and what was done. 4.12.1 said the received dating of the epistles "matters to direction"; withdrawn in place. 4.12.4 set "the received dating" of 2 Peter beside the proposed order as if the two weighed alike; rewritten. 4.11.1 gives the received dates as the received account's own, which is rule (a), and stands. The paper (#1812 v1.1) is deposited and was not searched for the same fault here; that is owed before its next version.

4.13.4. Added 14:16, at the author's word on Josephus (4.14.2). The same rule governs controls. A control is a text the hypothesis says had no part; a text inside the hypothesized system cannot be one, whatever the received account says of its independence. Josephus and Acts were each used as a control in this file on 27 November and each is withdrawn.

4.13.5. The author, 14:17, giving the reason in full: "on this reading the entire movement is josephus bipgraphicalizing philo through the visionary revelation. so that would have very little control value." Recorded as the system's own statement of itself: Philo the office, Revelation the vision, Josephus the hand that makes the life. On it Josephus is the last text that could serve as a control, since it is the one text the hypothesis names as doing the work. One thing follows that can be looked at in texts that exist. Josephus has a known practice of turning scripture into narrated lives in a Greek host (the Antiquities on Moses and the rest). The paper's section S names five operations Matthew performs on the Septuagint and on Revelation. Whether the Antiquities performs the same five on the Septuagint, and in the same proportions, is a comparison of hands. Not run; owed.

4.13.6. The author, 14:18: "in fact josephus may be the missing context." Taken as a direction for the count of 4.14: Josephus enters as a source to be read against Matthew, by the ledger's way (relation read, then counted), beginning where Revelation and the letters reach least. Not begun here.

4.14. **How much of Matthew the letters reach: a third instrument fails, a hand list holds, and the total stands at 37%.** The author, 14:08–14:09: "we must run more tests for the epistles now, either shared with revelation or stabdalone - between philo, revelation, and epistles, i bet we can get to 50%+ explained ... of matthew."

4.14.1. A third instrument, built to price chance (sealed before its run). For each verse of Matthew, the number of three-verse windows of a source expected to share its lemmas so well if lemmas fell independently; a verse is reached below 0.01. Run on the letters, on Revelation as a calibration against the hand ledger, and on Acts as a control (Acts is no control: 4.14.2; the comparison with it is withdrawn). Result: it finds 2 of the ledger's 305 verses. It reaches 23 verses from the letters, and what it reaches is shared quotation of the Septuagint and the supper (Matt 19:5 with Eph 5:31; 21:42 with 1 Pet 2:7; 22:44 with Heb 1:13; 26:26–27 with 1 Cor 11). Four of six sealed lines failed. Three instruments now: two too loose (4.10), one too strict. None sees a relation; the ledger never was made by one.

4.14.2. Philo, and what may serve as a control. As first written this paragraph said the earlier Philo pair file (451 verses of Matthew, 42%) was noise "by its own control", Josephus (402 verses, 38%). The author, 14:16: "you cant use josephus as a control when josephus is part of the hypothesized authorship system." Withdrawn. A control has to stand outside the system whose reach is being measured; Josephus stands inside it. Acts, used as the control in 4.14.1, falls by the same reasoning and is withdrawn there too.

The count was run again against seated Greek prose taken here to be outside the system: Epictetus, Maximus of Tyre, Strabo, Diodorus. (If any of the four belongs inside, the author will say, and it comes out.) Every corpus was cut into 100-word pieces and 180 pieces drawn, twenty draws each; a verse of Matthew is hit when two of its stems, each in 60 verses of the New Testament or fewer, stand together in one piece and together in no verse of the Septuagint. Sealed first: Philo within 0.8 to 1.3 times Maximus and Epictetus; Strabo and Diodorus below Philo; Josephus above Strabo and Diodorus.

| Corpus, at equal size | Verses of Matthew hit (mean of 20 draws) | Range |
|---|---|---|
| Epictetus | 99.8 (9.3%) | 88–116 |
| Diodorus | 79.6 (7.5%) | 58–93 |
| Philo | 64.3 (6.0%) | 49–86 |
| Strabo | 56.5 (5.3%) | 43–72 |
| Maximus of Tyre | 42.0 (3.9%) | 27–64 |
| Josephus (inside the system; reported, no control) | 100.9 (9.4%) | 77–116 |

Two of three sealed lines failed: Philo is 1.53 times Maximus and 0.64 times Epictetus, outside the band on both sides, and Diodorus hits more than Philo. What the table says: by this instrument Philo reaches Matthew no more than a historian and a geographer do, and less than Epictetus. The instrument counts shared Greek. It cannot see what the archive's work on Philo found, which is offices and operations (#1758; section 5), and its 42% is evidence of nothing. So the earlier verdict stands, on a control that can bear it. Philo's part in Matthew is unmeasured. Josephus by the same instrument stands level with Epictetus; that says the instrument cannot see Josephus either, and nothing about Josephus.

4.14.3. The ledger's way, applied to the letters. Fifty-two pairs of a Matthew passage and a letter's passage were named from memory and sealed as a list before any was checked (the list is `layer/bh.py`). The rule for each: the two passages share two content lemmas each in 400 verses or fewer with one in 100 or fewer, or one lemma standing in 10 verses or fewer. The pairs being named beforehand, the rule searches nothing. Sealed: 34 or more would hold; they would cover 110 to 170 verses; with the ledger the total would be 34% to 40%; 35% to 55% of the covered verses would lie inside the ledger.

| | Result |
|---|---|
| Pairs holding | **49 of 52** |
| Verses of Matthew covered | **147** |
| Inside the ledger's 305 | 57 (39%; base rate 28.6%) |
| New verses | 90 |
| Ledger and letters together | **395 of 1,067: 37.0%** |
| Leaving out the 9 rows whose words the Septuagint also holds | 371: 34.8% |
| Adding the three pairs read in 4.10 and 4.12 that hold by the same rule | 397: 37.2% |

All four sealed lines held. The three that did not hold fail on cognates the lemma count cannot join: the merciful and mercy (Matt 5:7; Jas 2:13), peacemakers and making peace (5:9; Jas 3:18), doers and hearers (7:21–27; Jas 1:22–25).

4.14.4. The rows, by where in Matthew they fall.

- The sermon, with James, 1 Peter and Romans: the poor and the kingdom (5:3; Jas 2:5); reviled and rejoicing (5:10–12; 1 Pet 3:14, 4:13–14); good works seen (5:16; 1 Pet 2:12); the law fulfilled (5:17; Rom 13:8–10); the oath (5:33–37; Jas 5:12); those who persecute (5:39–44; Rom 12:14–21); deliver from the evil one (6:13; 2 Tim 4:18); treasure (6:19–20; Jas 5:2–3); eating, drinking, kingdom and righteousness (6:31–33; Rom 14:17); judging (7:1–2; Rom 2:1–3); ask and it is given (7:7–11; Jas 1:5, 17); figs (7:16–20; Jas 3:12).
- The mission and the sayings: the worker (10:10; 1 Tim 5:18); innocent in the midst (10:16; Phil 2:15); endure to the end (10:22, 24:13; Jas 5:11); hidden from the wise (11:25–27; 1 Cor 1–2); leaven (13:33, 16:6–12; Gal 5:9, 1 Cor 5:6–8); the belly (15:11–20; Rom 14; 1 Cor 6:13); guide of the blind (15:14; Rom 2:19); mountains (17:20, 21:21; 1 Cor 13:2); the stumbling-block (18:6–7; Rom 14:13); two or three and the gathering in the name (18:15–20; 2 Cor 13:1, 1 Cor 5:3–5); one flesh, not to separate (19:5–9; 1 Cor 6–7, Eph 5:31); the commandments (19:18–19, 22:37–40; Rom 13:8–10); humbled and exalted (23:12; Jas 4:10, 1 Pet 5:6); killing the prophets (23:31–37; 1 Thess 2:15–16).
- The end: birth pangs (24:8, 19; 1 Thess 5:3); signs and wonders (24:24; 2 Thess 2:9); trumpet and clouds (24:30–31; 1 Thess 4:16–17); the thief (24:42–44; 1 Thess 5); sowing and reaping (25:24–26; 2 Cor 9:6); hungry and thirsty (25:35–44; Rom 12:20); the consummation of the age (13:39–49, 24:3, 28:20; Heb 9:26, the noun's only place outside Matthew).
- The life: son of David, son of Abraham (1:1; Rom 1:3, Gal 3:16); children of Abraham (3:9; Rom 9:7–8); the twelve (10:2–6; 1 Cor 15:5–7); not flesh and blood (16:17; Gal 1:16); raised on the third day (16:21, 17:23, 20:19; 1 Cor 15:4); the mountain (17:1–8; 2 Pet 1:16–18); the supper (26:26–28; 1 Cor 11:23–25); watch and sleep (26:38–45; 1 Thess 5:6–10); the veil (27:51; Heb 6:19, 9:3, 10:20); the sleepers raised (27:52–53; 1 Cor 15:20); authority in heaven and earth (28:18; Col 1:16–20); baptized into the name (28:19; 1 Cor 1:13–15).

4.14.5. What the 37% is and is not.

- It is two hand lists added together, made to two standards. The ledger's rows each carry a relation read, an operator named and a count of where else the construction stands. The letters' rows carry shared words under a rule, and no operator and no count of rarity yet. Some are exact and rare (the oath; innocent in the midst, three places in all; the consummation; the veil; the supper). Some share words any two texts on the subject would share (killing prophets; baptized into a name).
- It says nothing of direction. Every one of these pairs is known to the received account, which reads them as Paul and James echoing sayings of Jesus, or as common tradition. Here that reading is a claim to be accounted for (4.13) and the test of direction on each row is owed.
- The list is what one reader remembered in a few minutes. It is no census of what the letters hold. Hebrews, 1 Peter and James are thin in it for their size.
- Where it lands is the thing to notice. The ledger's 305 are thin in the sermon and the sayings. The letters' rows fall there. The two sources reach different parts of the book, with an overlap of 57 verses, about a third above chance.

4.14.6. The author's wager. 50% of Matthew from Philo, Revelation and the letters. Standing at 37% from two of the three, with the third unmeasured and the letters' list a first pass. Not reached. What would move it, in order: the letters' list made thoroughly and letter by letter (James, 1 Peter and Hebrews read through against Matthew, as Revelation was); each of its rows brought to the ledger's standard, with an operator and a count; an instrument for Philo that can see offices and operations, tested against prose from outside the system. Until the second is done the 37% is a count of candidates, and the 28.6% remains the figure of record.

4.15. **A note for later: Mark last.** The author, 14:12: "its possible mark came last. the most biographical fusion, refined, traces stripped down. just something to check and a reminder that we cannot rely on constructed chronology. sidenote for later."

4.15.1. The reminder caught this file out a second time. 4.12 said "twice Matthew adds to Mark exactly the word of the outside text", of the stumbling-block at Matt 16:23 and "before all" at 26:70. "Adds" assumes Mark stood first. That is the constructed order of the Gospels, used as a premise. The observation without it: at both places Matthew has the word that the outside text has, and Mark has the same scene without it. Whether Matthew put it in or Mark took it out is what the note asks. 4.12 is rewritten to say only what is seen.

4.15.2. What the note predicts, for when it is taken up; nothing is run here. If Mark is the last and most refined fusion, then across the rows that have a Marcan parallel Mark should lack the hook words more often than keep them, should lack them more often than Luke does, and should show no hook words of its own that Matthew lacks. If Mark stands first, the hooks Matthew has and Mark lacks should look like additions: detachable, at the edges of the scene. The paper's own figure is the place to start: 100 of the 458 Mark-parallel verses of Matthew are reached from Revelation.

4.16. **Josephus read against Matthew, the ledger's way.** The author, 14:21: "read against matthew the ledger's way..."

4.16.1. The received reading. Josephus is the outside witness: the historian from whom the Gospels' rulers, sects and places are checked, and whom Luke, on one much-argued view, had read. Parallels between Matthew and Josephus are read as two writers reporting the same world. Three have a literature of their own: Zechariah son of Barachiah (Matt 23:35) against Zechariah son of Baris killed in the temple (War 4.335); the labourers paid for one hour (Matt 20) against the temple workmen (Ant. 20.220); Jesus son of Ananias (War 6.300–309). No one counts how much of Matthew stands in Josephus, for the reason no one counted Revelation: on the received account Josephus is a control and has no place among the sources.

4.16.2. The method. Thirty-eight pairs named from memory, each with the Greek words the relation turns on, sealed as a list before any was checked (`control/bj.py`). A pair holds when every named word stands in the Matthew verses and all stand together within three consecutive sections of the named work of Josephus, as seated. Loci were not trusted from memory; the run reports where the words stand, and each place quoted below was then read in the seat. Sealed: 27 or more would hold; 90 to 150 verses; 40 to 80 new verses beyond the ledger and the letters; total 41% to 45%; nine or more of eleven rows expected exact and rare.

4.16.3. Result.

| | As sealed | With three spellings corrected |
|---|---|---|
| Pairs holding | 32 of 38 | 35 of 38 |
| Verses of Matthew | 98 | 107 |
| New beyond the ledger and the letters | 58 | 66 |
| Ledger, letters and Josephus together | 455 of 1,067: **42.6%** | 463: **43.4%** |
| The same without the names class | 443: 41.5% | 443: 41.5% |

All four sealed lines held. Three pairs missed only because the seated Matthew spells Ἰωάνης, Πειλᾶτος and Καισαρίας; the second column corrects the pattern and changes nothing else. The three that do not hold: the star (Matthew has no city in those verses); the Jesus who answers the governor nothing (the name stands at War 6.300 and the answer at 6.305, five sections apart, outside the window set); and the Joseph who asks the ruler for the crucified (Matthew lacks the verb of taking down; a relation, listed without a count).

4.16.4. The rows that are exact. Each place read in the seat.

| Matthew | Josephus | What stands in both | Where else in Josephus |
|---|---|---|---|
| 27:6, "it is not lawful to put them into the κορβανᾶς" | War 2.175, Pilate spends "the sacred treasure, which is called κορβωνᾶς" | the word; the one place it stands in the New Testament and in the War, and in the War it is Pilate's chapter | nowhere |
| 17:24, the δίδραχμα | Ant. 18.312, "the δίδραχμον which it is ancestral for each to pay to God" | the word; in the New Testament only here | nowhere in the Antiquities |
| 5:33–34, "you shall not swear falsely … do not swear at all" | War 2.135, the Essenes: "what they say is stronger than an oath; swearing they avoid, thinking it worse than false swearing" | ὀμνύω with ἐπιορκ-; ἐπιορκέω stands in the New Testament only here | one place in the War |
| 20:1–12, "these last worked one hour" and are paid the wage | Ant. 20.220, the temple workmen: "if one worked a single hour of the day, he at once received the wage for it" | μίαν ὥραν, μισθός, and the work | the three words meet elsewhere; this is the sentence |
| 24:27–31, from the east to the west; from the four winds; a great voice | War 6.301, Jesus son of Ananias: "a voice from the east, a voice from the west, a voice from the four winds, a voice against Jerusalem and the temple, a voice against bridegrooms and brides" | ἀνατολή, δύσις, the four winds, φωνή | nowhere |
| 24:24–26, false prophets, great signs, "he is in the wilderness" | War 2.259–261, deceivers lead the crowd into the wilderness where God will show signs; then "the Egyptian false prophet" | ψευδοπροφήτης, ἔρημος, σημεῖα | nowhere |
| 24:15, "the abomination of desolation spoken of through Daniel the prophet" | Ant. 10.276, "Daniel also wrote of the Romans' rule, and that it would be made desolate by them" | Daniel named with the desolation; Matthew alone of the Gospels names Daniel | once more, Ant. 12.321 |
| 23:35, Zechariah son of Barachiah, killed between the sanctuary and the altar | War 4.335, "Zechariah son of Baris", killed in the middle of the temple (4.343) | the name, "son of", Bar- | nowhere |
| 12:22–45, demons cast out; "by whom do your sons cast them out"; the spirit that returns; "more than Solomon is here" | Ant. 8.45–47, Solomon's art against demons, "so that they never return"; Eleazar draws one out and adjures it "never to come back into him, naming Solomon" | demons, Solomon, the return | Solomon's own chapters |
| 3:1–4, John in the wilderness, his clothing, his food | Life 11, Bannus "living in the wilderness, using clothing from trees, taking food that grew of itself, washing often in cold water for purity" | ἐρημία, clothing, τροφή, washing | nowhere in the Life |
| 10:9–10, no two tunics, no sandals | War 2.126, the Essenes "change neither garments nor sandals until they are torn" (2.124–125: they carry nothing on the road) | ὑποδήματα with the garment | one place |
| 1:15–16, "Matthan begot Jacob, Jacob begot Joseph" | Life 4–5, "Matthias … to him is born Joseph … and to Joseph, Matthias … and to Matthias, I" | a genealogy whose foot is Matth- and Joseph | the Life opens with it |

Eleven of eleven expected rows hold by the rule. The wage is the weakest by the rule (its three words are common) and the strongest by the sentence.

4.16.5. The names. Herod and the children, Archelaus over Judaea, Herod the tetrarch, John called the baptizer (Ant. 18.116), Herodias and the brother's wife, Pilate the governor, Caiaphas the high priest, Pharisees with Sadducees, Capernaum (once in the War, 3.519), Gennesar, the Decapolis, Caesarea of Philip, Gadara. Thirty-four verses. This is T_Μ's second parameter, the host "furnished with a land's names", found in one author. The names are counted apart because any account has to grant them.

4.16.6. What it opens.

- **Josephus reaches where the ledger does not.** Of the 107 verses, 33 are in Revelation's ledger and 14 in the letters' rows; 66 are new. They fall in the infancy, the Baptist, the mission charge, the woes and the trial: the furniture and the incidents.
- **Three joined crossings, by the test of 4.8.1.** Matt 24:24–31 holds Revelation's false prophet and signs (the ledger's rows) and the War's false prophet, wilderness, east, west and four winds. Matt 20:1–16 holds Revelation's wage, first and last (the paper's first row) and the Antiquities' one hour and the wage. Matt 5:33–37 holds Revelation's oath by heaven and earth, James's prohibition, and the War's oath with false swearing. Three sources on one saying: the layers of this section and of 4.8–4.9 meet at the oath.
- **The woe.** Jesus son of Ananias cries a voice against Jerusalem and the temple, against bridegrooms and brides, is brought to the governor, answers nothing, is scourged and released as mad, and is killed in the siege crying "woe to Jerusalem" (War 6.300–309). Matthew 23–27 has the woes, "Jerusalem, Jerusalem", the bridegroom, the four winds, the governor who marvels that he answers nothing, the scourging. Only the four winds and the voice passed the window. The rest is a reading, and is the place to work next.
- **The foot of the genealogy.** Matthew's ends Matthan, Jacob, Joseph. The Life's ends Matthias, Joseph, Matthias, and the writer. Stated as what stands in the two texts; nothing is built on it here.

4.16.7. Standing. Forty-three per cent of Matthew now has a named counterpart in Revelation, the letters or Josephus. It is three hand lists at two standards: the ledger's 305 verses, each row with an operator and a count; and two lists made from memory in a day, under a rule of shared words. The figure of record stays 28.6% until the letters' and Josephus's rows carry operators and counts. Direction is untested on every row of both lists (4.13). The author's wager stands at 43 of 50, with Philo unmeasured and neither new list made thoroughly. Owed, in order: the War and the Life read through against Matthew, section by section; the woe of 4.16.6 worked as a walk, as Rev 12 was; each row given its operator; the comparison of hands (4.13.5).

4.16.8. Where this file is kept. The author, 14:23: "we should be stashing the notebook on revfirst - alexanarch takes a long time to build and uses more vercel compute." From v0.72 the notebook is kept in the revelationfirst repository at `unfolding/notebook/`, as a file beside the unfolding demonstration; no page, sitemap or index of that site is changed by it. The copies beside #1813 on alexanarch stand as they are, the last landed there being named in the reply that carries this version.

## 5. Philo as a supplementary source

5.1. What *In the Midst of the Seven* (#1758) shows Revelation doing to Philo, as operations, each at its section:

1. STATION. One lampstand with seven lamps becomes seven lampstands, each answerable for itself, with the consequence at 2:5 that one can be removed (§11.1b; 17.1k).
2. NAME-THE-SOCKET. Philo takes Ἀνατολή for the Logos from an oracle spoken to Ἰησοῦς the high priest and cuts the addressee; the Apocalypse's one in the midst wears the ποδήρης and is named Ἰησοῦς (17.1b, n, o; argued, 17.2a: "the withheld name as a socket the receiver fills").
3. INVERT-THE-VOTE. Philo's divine ψῆφος is the vote of condemnation; the white ψῆφος of Rev 2:17 is the acquittal (7b.1, 7b.9; 17.1q).
4. HIDE-THE-MANNA. Philo's manna is the Logos, unknown to those who taste it; Rev 2:17 gives "the hidden manna" (7b.5).
5. OFFICE. The law of displacement, the prophet uttering another's words (Her. 259), becomes a commission: Τάδε λέγει, and γράψον twelve times, each letter addressed to a messenger (§15; 17.1j).
6. SEAL-THE-NAME. The sealed name on the plate, heard only by the purified, beside the name written that no one knows (7b.3; Rev 19:12).

Not established there, and kept so here: the route (Philo's text or a tradition carrying it), and direction in any single case (17.2).

5.2. How this opens the map. Two sources stand behind a New Testament passage on the received account: the Septuagint and common tradition. The map adds two columns per arrow: is the unit in Philo; is it in Revelation's transformed form or Philo's. Where an epistle has the unit in the form Revelation gave it (seven lampstands, a white vote, a named one in the midst), Philo cannot be the direct source and the arrow runs through Revelation. Where it has Philo's form, Philo is a rival source and the arrow is in doubt. Hebrews is the first place to look: #1802 §13.6 records that dependence on Philo "is heard for Hebrews and filed for the Apocalypse", and the hologram counts four arrows landing on Hebrews. The census already carries the Philo flag on every shared sequence. Stated with the review's care: inheriting a derived form supports passage through Revelation or through a tradition that carried Revelation's form; it does not by itself establish that the later writer had the book.

5.3. The chain this gives, for the working model only: Septuagint and Philo → Revelation (operations of 5.1; upstream, #1758, #1511) → Gospels and epistles (operations of 2.6 and of the James rules). An operator vocabulary that describes both halves in the same terms would be the strongest form of the programme: Revelation doing to Philo what Matthew does to Revelation. Two already rhyme: INVERT-THE-VOTE with the adversarial class M̄; OFFICE with CITE-AS-SCRIPTURE and CITE-PROMISE.

5.4. Newton. #1735 §I cites him: in 1733 he argued that the Apocalypse "seems to be alluded to in the Epistles of Peter and that to the Hebrews, and therefore to have been written before them", and catalogued the allusions: the two-edged sword of the word, the heavenly city with foundations, the σαββατισμός. Two of his three are already arrows in the hologram (Heb 4:8–14; Heb 11:10 in M4). His *Observations* are not seated; the Newton seat holds the *Principia* and the *Opticks*. Seating part 2, chapter 1, would give a list of arrows drawn by an earlier hand to score the map against. Owed.

5.5. **Melchizedek** (the author: "ive always thought the nag hammadi melchizedek epistle was older than its accounted"). NHC IX,1 is seated in Coptic (EA-CORPORA-02/15, `nhc-ix-melchizedek.txt`, 516 lines). It is not read here. It belongs on the map beside Hebrews 7 and Philo's Melchizedek (Leg. 3.79–82, from memory), as a third text holding a priest who is the Logos or stands for him, and it is a revelation delivered to its speaker. Operation owed: read it in the seat, and set its Melchizedek against Hebrews' and against the one in the midst of Rev 1.

5.6. The engine runs Philo → Revelation in the same operators as Revelation → the Gospels (2B.3, the last six rows). Four chains are in: the lampstand and the one in the midst (Her. 219 → Rev 1:12–13); the vote (Deus 75 → Rev 2:17); the manna (Leg. 3.174–175 → Rev 2:17); the name on the head (Mos. 2.114 → Rev 19:12, where Philo's plate has "four engravings of a name" and the rider has four names). The fifth, Revelation's manna on to Heb 9:4, was entered as support for a route through Revelation and was a misreading: the covering in Heb 9:4 is the ark's gold. It is corrected at 2G.1 and now counts against that route. OFFICE (Her. 259 → Τάδε λέγει, γράψον) is not in: it has no shared wording for a member to hold, and the engine is an engine of wording.

## 7. Questions for the author

- The four names: Rev 19:11–16, as read in 3.2, or another four?
- "The seven curses": taken here as the seven last plagues of Rev 15–16, which the book calls a sign. If the seven are others, 2A.4 changes and 2A.2–2A.3 stand.
